Profinite almost rigidity in 3-manifolds
Xiaoyu Xu

Abstract
. We prove that any compact, orientable 3-manifold with empty or toral boundary is profinitely almost rigid among all compact, orientable 3-manifolds. In other words, the profinite completion of its fundamental group determines its homeomorphism type to finitely many possibilities. Moreover, the profinite completion of the fundamental group of a mixed 3-manifold together with the peripheral structure uniquely determines the homeomorphism type of its Seifert part, i.e. the maximal graph manifold components in the JSJ-decomposition. On the other hand, without assigning the peripheral structure, the profinite completion of a mixed 3-manifold group may not uniquely determine the fundamental group of its Seifert part. The proof is based on JSJ-decomposition.



1.
Introduction

It is a useful idea to distinguish the properties of a finitely generated group
G
through the collection of its finite quotient groups, denoted by
\mathcal{C}(G)
. When
G=\pi_{1}(M^{3})
is a 3-manifold group,
\mathcal{C}(G)
corresponds to the lattice of finite regular coverings of
M
. It is known that among finitely generated groups, the profinite completion encodes full data of finite quotients.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
G
be a group, the
profinite completion
of
G
is defined as

\widehat{G}=\varprojlim\limits_{N\lhd_{f}G}\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$G$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$N$}\;,

where
N
ranges over all finite-index normal subgroups of
G
.

Fact 1.2

(
[
7
]
)
.

For two finitely generated groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
,
\mathcal{C}(G_{1})=\mathcal{C}(G_{2})
if and only if
\widehat{G_{1}}\cong\widehat{G_{2}}
.

The geometrization theorem implies that a 3-manifold is largely determined by its fundamental group. For instance, the fundamental group of a closed, orientable, prime 3-manifold determines its homeomorphism type except for the special case of lens spaces, see
[
1
, Theorem 2.1.2]
.
As we are studying the fundamental group
\pi_{1}M^{3}
through its finite quotients
\mathcal{C}(\pi_{1}M)
, it is natural to ask whether
\mathcal{C}(\pi_{1}M)
(or equivalently,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
) determines the homeomorphism type of
M
. This question has been formulated as the profinite rigidity within 3-manifolds.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a class of 3-manifolds. For any
M\in\mathscr{M}
, we denote

\Delta_{\mathscr{M}}(M)=\{N\in\mathscr{M}\mid\widehat{\pi_{1}N}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\}.

We say that a manifold
M
is
profinitely rigid
in
\mathscr{M}
if
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}}(M)=\{M\}
, and
M
is
profinitely almost rigid
in
\mathscr{M}
if
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}}(M)
is finite.
The class
\mathscr{M}
itself is called
profinitely (almost) rigid
if every
M\in\mathscr{M}
is profinitely (almost) rigid in
\mathscr{M}
.

To avoid the trivial ambiguities, we always include the following convention.

Convention 1.4
.

In our context, we always assume that a compact 3-manifold has no boundary spheres. Indeed, the boundary spheres can be excluded through capping 3-balls while preserving the fundamental group.

Within 3-manifolds, the question of profinite rigidity is closely related with geometrization. In a series of works, Wilton-Zalesskii
[
43
,
44
,
45
]
showed that among compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary, the profinite completion determines whether the 3-manifold is geometric; it further determines the geometry type within the geometric case, and determines the prime decomposition and the JSJ-decomposition in the non-geometric case.

For geometric 3-manifolds, Grunewald-Pickel-Segal
[
11
]
showed the profinite almost rigidity of
Sol
-manifolds, while Stebe
[
33
]
and more examples by Funar
[
9
]
showed that the class of
Sol
-manifolds is not profinitely rigid. Wilkes
[
38
]
showed the profinite almost rigidity of closed orientable Seifert fibered spaces, including the profinite rigidity in
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{E}^{1}
,
\mathbb{E}^{3}
,
Nil
and
{\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})}}
geometries, and the only non-rigid exceptions are those constructed by Hempel
[
15
]
within
\mathbb{H}^{2}\times\mathbb{E}^{1}
geometry.
Indeed, the non-rigid examples
[
9
,
15
,
33
]
are all given by commensurable surface bundles over circle. Recently, Liu
[
18
]
proved the profinite almost rigidity of finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds. In addition, Bridson, Reid, McReynolds and Spitler
[
3
,
4
]
have found various examples of profinitely rigid closed hyperbolic 3-manifolds, including the Weeks manifold and the
\mathrm{Vol(3)}
manifold. Indeed, they showed that the fundamental groups of these hyperbolic 3-manifolds are actually profinitely rigid among all finitely generated, residually finite groups. However, whether profinite rigidity holds in the hyperbolic case is still unknown.

For non-geometric 3-manifolds, Wilkes
[
40
]
proved that closed graph manifolds are profinitely almost rigid but not profinitely rigid,
and he presented a complete profinite classification for graph manifolds, including many profinitely rigid examples. Therefore, the crucial topic remaining for profinite almost rigidity in 3-manifolds is the mixed manifolds (including purely hyperbolic ones).

Within 3-manifolds, two well-known theorems exhibit the relationship between the fundamental group and the homeomorphism type of a 3-manifold.

Theorem 1.5

(
[
37
, Corollary 6.5]
)
.

The fundamental group of a compact, Haken, boundary-incompressible 3-manifold together with its peripheral structure (i.e. the conjugacy classes of the peripheral subgroups) uniquely determines the homeomorphism type of this 3-manifold.

Theorem 1.6
.

Among compact, orientable 3-manifolds, the fundamental group determines the homeomorphism type to finitely many possibilities, but possibly not unique.

Theorem 1.6
was proven by Johannson
[
16
, Corollary 29.3]
for irreducible 3-manifolds with non-empty incompressible boundary, and independently by Swarup
[
34
]
for irreducible 3-manifolds with boundary, without the boundary-incompressibility assumption. The full version of
Theorem 1.6
then follows from the validity of the Kneser’s conjecture
[
13
]
, together with the geometrization theorem for the closed case, see
[
1
, Theorem 2.1.2]
.

The goal of this paper is to establish a partial analog of these two theorems in the profinite setting.

Let us first consider
Theorem 1.5
. Accordingly, in dealing with 3-manifolds with boundary, it is worthwhile to investigative a specific class of profinite isomorphisms, i.e. those respecting the peripheral structure.

Definition 1.7
.

For compact 3-manifolds
M
and
N
with (possibly empty) incompressible boundary, an isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}

respects the peripheral structure
if there is a one-to-one correspondence between the boundary components of
M
and
N
, simply denoted as
\partial_{i}M\leftrightarrow\partial_{i}N
, such that
f
sends
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
, the closure of a peripheral subgroup, to a conjugate of
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}N}
in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
.

We also agree that any profinite isomorphism between closed 3-manifolds automatically respects the peripehral structure.

Indeed, as is shown in
Theorem 2.5
, the profinite completion of a compact 3-manifold group
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
together with its peripheral structure encodes the same data as the “peripheral pair quotients” of
\pi_{1}M
, i.e. the finite quotients of
\pi_{1}M
marked with the conjugacy classes of peripheral subgroups, denoted by
\mathcal{C}(\pi_{1}M;\pi_{1}\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\pi_{1}\partial_{m}M)
(see
Definition 2.4
).

Our first result can be viewed as a profinite version of
Theorem 1.5
in the Seifert part of mixed 3-manifolds.

In the following context, a
mixed 3-manifold
denotes a compact, orientable and irreducible 3-manifold with empty or incompressible toral boundary, whose JSJ-decomposition is non-trivial and contains at least one hyperbolic piece.
The
Seifert part
of a mixed 3-manifold is the union of its maximal graph manifold components in its JSJ-decomposition. By convention, the Seifert part of a purely-hyperbolic manifold is defined as an empty space.

Theorem A
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are mixed 3-manifolds, with empty or toral boundary. If
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
through an isomorphism respecting the peripheral structure, then the Seifert parts of
M
and
N
are homeomorphic.

Note that any profinite isomorphism between closed manifolds does respect the peripheral structure by our definition. Hence, we have the following corollary.

Corollary B
.

Among closed mixed 3-manifolds, the profinite completion of the fundamental group determines the homeomorphism type of the Seifert part.

Any profinite isomorphism between finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds respects the peripheral structure (
Lemma 6.3
). However, this is not always true for Seifert fibered spaces, as for isomorphisms between their original fundamental groups. Indeed, flexibility may occur when the profinite isomorphism does not respect the peripheral structure.

Despite
Theorem A
, from which
\mathcal{C}(\pi_{1}M;\pi_{1}\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\pi_{1}\partial_{n}M)
determines the homeomorphism type of the Seifert part within mixed manifolds,
\mathcal{C}(\pi_{1}M)
itself may not even determine the fundamental group of the Seifert part as shown by the following theorem. This shows sharp contrast with the classical case
[
28
]
, where
\pi_{1}M
, without assigning the peripheral structure, is sufficient to determine the fundamental group of the Seifert part, through the group theoretical JSJ-decomposition.

Theorem C
.

There exists a pair of irreducible, mixed 3-manifolds
M
,
N
with incompressible toral boundary, such that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
by an isomorphism not respecting the peripheral structure, while the fundamental groups of the Seifert part of
M
and
N
are not isomorphic.

In combination with
[
28
]
, this implies
\pi_{1}M\not\cong\pi_{1}N
, which disproves the group-level profinite rigidity in the fundamental group of mixed 3-manifolds.

Our next result is a profinite version of
Theorem 1.6
.
Though the absolute profinite rigidity does not hold for the fundamental group of 3-manifolds with toral boundary by
Theorem C
, the profinite completion of fundamental group, or equivalently
\mathcal{C}(\pi_{1}M)
, still determines the homeomorphism type of such a 3-manifold to finitely many possibilities. We remind the readers that the following result does not require the peripheral structure.

Theorem D
.

Any compact, orientable 3-manifold with empty or toral boundary is profinitely almost rigid in the class of all compact, orientable 3-manifolds.

The following two tables conclude current results for profinite rigidity and almost rigidity in 3-manifolds based on geometric decomposition.

As for bounded 3-manifolds, in order to exclude the ambiguity that non-homeomorphic bounded 3-manifolds may have isomorphic fundamental groups, we say a class of manifolds
\mathscr{M}
has
group-level profinite rigidity
if for any
M,N\in\mathscr{M}
,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
implies
\pi_{1}M\cong\pi_{1}N
.

)

1.1.
Ingredients of proof

1.1.1.
Congruent isomorphism

Wilton-Zalesskii
[
45
]
(see also Wilkes
[
39
,
41
]
for the bounded case) proved the profinite detection of geometric decomposition in 3-manifolds. In this paper, we reformulate their results using the language of profinite Bass-Serre theory.

Definition 1.8

(Congruent isomorphism)
.

A
congruent isomorphism

f_{\bullet}
between two finite graphs of (profinite) groups
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
and
(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
consists of:

(1)

an isomorphism of oriented graphs
{f^{\dagger}}:\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma^{\prime}
;

(2)

an isomorphism of (profinite) groups
f_{z}:\mathcal{G}_{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(z)}
for each
z\in\Gamma
;

(3)

a coherence relation at each edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
, i.e. commutative by composing with a conjugation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{d_{i}(e)}}

\circlearrowleft

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(d_{i}(e))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(d_{i}(e))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}^{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d_{i}(e)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi^{\prime})_{i}^{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{i}^{e}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
C_{h_{i}^{e}}(x)=h_{i}^{e}\cdot x\cdot(h_{i}^{e})^{-1}
denotes the conjugation by
h_{i}^{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(d_{i}(e))}
, and
i=0,1
.

Theorem 1.9

(cf.
Theorem 5.5
)
.

Suppose
M,N
are two orientable, irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary, which are not closed
Sol
-manifolds. Then, any isomorphism
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
induces a congruent isomorphism between their JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma_{N})
, after reassigning orientation of the JSJ-graphs. In addition, the congruent isomorphism matches up the hyperbolic/Seifert fibered type of the vertices.

1.1.2.
Peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity

In light of
Theorem 1.9
, it is crucial to analyze the properties of a profinite isomorphism
f_{v}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{v}}
between the JSJ-pieces when restricted to the closure of a peripheral subgroup
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}
. This implies information about the gluing map at this JSJ-torus, as shown by the following commutative diagram.

(1.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{v}}}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{0}}\circ f_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{1}}\circ f_{v}}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
\subseteq

Note that the closure of a peripheral subgroup corresponding to an incompressible toral boundary component can be naturally identified as
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)
(cf.
Lemma 6.1
). We depict a special property of the map
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)\to\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}(\partial_{j}N)
called peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity.

Definition 1.11

(Peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity)
.

Let
M
and
N
be compact, orientable 3-manifolds with incompressible toral boundary. Let
\partial_{i}M
be a boundary component of
M
, and let
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
be a conjugacy representative of the corresponding peripheral subgroup. Suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism. For
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, we say that
f
is
peripheral
\lambda
-regular
at
\partial_{i}M
if the following holds.

(1)

There exists a boundary component
\partial_{j}N
of
N
, a conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
, and an element
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
such that
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=C_{g}^{-1}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N})
, where
C_{g}
denotes the conjugation by
g
.

(2)

There exists an isomorphism
\psi:\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\otimes\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
\lambda
denotes the scalar multiplication by
\lambda
in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.

For simplicity, we say that
f
is
peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular
at
\partial_{i}M
if it is peripheral
\lambda
-regular for some
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

1.1.3.
Profinite almost auto-symmetry

When
f
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M
, there are two main ingredients to be considered
in
Definition 1.11
(
2
), namely the coefficient
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and the group isomorphism
\psi
. In proof of profinite (almost) rigidity, it is crucial to determine whether
\psi
can be induced by an ambient homeomorphism
\Phi:M\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N
, for which we introduce an accurate definition as follows.

Let
M
and
N
be compact, orientable 3-manifolds with incompressible toral boundary. Let
\partial_{i}M
be a boundary component of
M
, and let
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M
be a conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup.
Suppose
\Phi:M\to N
is a homeomorphism, which sends
\partial_{i}M
to a boundary component
\partial_{j}N
. Up to a choice of basepoints, let
\Phi_{\ast}:\pi_{1}M\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\pi_{1}N
denote the induced isomorphism between the fundamental groups, and let
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N=\Phi_{\ast}(\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M)
be the corresponding conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup.

Definition 1.12
.

Under the above assumptions,
suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is a profinite isomorphism which is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M
.
We say that
f
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by a homeomorphism
\Phi
with coefficient
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, if there exists an element
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
such that
C_{g}\circ f
sends
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
to
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}
, and the isomorphism

C_{g}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}:\;\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N\cong\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}

can be decomposed as
\lambda\otimes(\Phi_{\ast}|_{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})
.

Let
M
be a compact, orientable 3-manifold with incompressible toral boundary, and let
\mathcal{P}=\{\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M\}
be a collection of some boundary components of
M
. We define two subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(\widehat{\pi_{1}M})
:

\begin{gathered}\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P}):=\left\{f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\left|\;\begin{gathered}\text{for each }1\leq i\leq k\text{,}\\
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})\text{ is a conjugate of }\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M},\\
\text{ and }f\text{ is }\text{peripheral }\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}\text{-regular at }\partial_{i}M\end{gathered}\right.\;\right\},\\[3.87498pt]
\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P}):=\left\{f\in\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})\left|\,\begin{gathered}\text{there is a homeomorphism }\Phi:M\to M,\text{ such}\\
\text{that }f\text{ restricting }\text{on each }\partial_{i}M\text{ is induced by}\\
\text{the homeomorphism }\Phi\text{ with coefficient }\lambda_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}\end{gathered}\right.\right\}.\end{gathered}

We remind the readers that in the definition of
\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
, the ambient homeomorphism
\Phi
is unified for all components
\partial_{i}M
, while the coefficients
\lambda_{i}
might be different from each other.

Definition 1.13

(Profinite almost auto-symmetry)
.

Under the above assumptions, we say that
M
is
profinitely almost auto-symmetric at
\mathcal{P}

if
\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
is a finite-index subgroup in
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
.

1.1.4.
Gluing geometric pieces

Based on
Theorem 1.9
, the proof for profinite almost rigidity in mixed 3-manifolds consists of two parts.
Firstly, the profinite almost rigidity in the geometric case implies that 3-manifolds profinitely isomorphic to a given mixed 3-manifold can only be built up from a finite number of possible geometric pieces. Secondly, the peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity and the profinite almost auto-symmetry ensure that the gluings between the geometric pieces also fall into finitely many possibilities.

The key results within this process are listed as follows.

Theorem 1.14
.

(1)

(cf.
[
18
, Theorem 9.1]
) The class of finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds is profinitely almost rigid.

(2)

(cf.
Theorem 7.2
) Suppose
M
and
N
are finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds with cusps. Then, any profinite isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at all boundary components of
M
.

(3)

(cf.
Corollary 7.3
) Any cusped finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold
M
is profinitely almost auto-symmetric at
\partial M
.

Theorem 1.15
.

(1)

(cf.
Theorem 9.4
, and
[
40
]
for the closed case) The class of graph manifolds, with empty or toral boundary, is profinitely almost rigid.

(2)

(cf.
Theorem 9.11
) Let
M
and
N
be graph manifolds with non-empty boundary. Suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism respecting the peripheral structure, and that
f
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at (at least) one boundary component of
M
. Then,
M
and
N
are homeomorphic.

(3)

(cf.
Theorem 9.12
) Let
M
be a graph manifold with boundary, and let
\mathcal{P}
be a collection of some boundary components of
M
. Then,
M
is profinitely almost auto-symmetric at
\mathcal{P}
.

1.2.
Structure of the paper

Section 2
and
Section 3
serve as preliminary materials for this paper.
Section 4
and
Section 5
are further expansions on the profinite detection of 3-manifold decomposition. In particular,
Theorem 1.9
is introduced in
Section 5
.

The most important concept, peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity, is introduced in
Section 6
, where we verify its “well-definedness”, and prove a gluing lemma.

In
Section 7
, we characterize profinite isomorphisms between hyperbolic 3-manifolds based on the results of Liu
[
18
]
, and prove
Theorem 1.14
.

Section 8
studies profinite isomorphisms between Seifert fibered spaces, mostly inspired by Wilkes
[
38
,
40
]
.
As the building blocks of a graph manifold, this serves as a preparation for
Section 9
, where we investigate the profinite properties of graph manifolds possibly with boundary, and prove
Theorem 1.15
.

We prove the main conclusions in
Section 10
.
Theorem A
is proven in
§ 10.1
,
Theorem C
is proven in
§ 10.2
, and finally
Theorem D
is proven in
§ 10.3
.

2.
Profinite groups

A
profinite space

X=\varprojlim X_{i}
is an inverse limit of finite spaces equipped with discrete topology over a directed partially ordered set, and a
profinite group

G=\varprojlim G_{i}
is an inverse limit of finite groups equipped with discrete topology over a directed partially ordered set.

It is shown in
[
25
, Theorem 1.1.12]
that
X
is a profinite space if and only if
X
is compact, Hausdorff and totally disconnected, and in
[
25
, Theorem 2.1.3]
that
G
is a profinite group if and only if
G
is a topological group whose underlying space is a profinite space.

2.1.
Profinite completion

Let
G
be an abstract group. The
profinite topology
on
G
is generated by the sub-basis consisting of all the (left and right) cosets of finite-index subgroups in
G
.
A subset
E\subseteq G
is called
separable
if
E
is closed in the profinite topology.

G
is called
residually finite
if
\{1\}
is separable.
G
is called
LERF
if any finitely generated subgroup of
G
is separable.

Recall in
Definition 1.1
, the profinite completion of an abstract group
G
is defined as a profinite group

\widehat{G}=\varprojlim\limits_{N\lhd_{f}G}\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$G$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$N$},

where the notation
\lhd_{f}
denotes finite-index normal subgroup.

There is a canonical homomorphism
\iota:G\to\widehat{G}
,
g\mapsto(gN)_{N\lhd_{f}G}
, so that
\iota(G)
is dense in
\widehat{G}
. Since
\mathrm{ker}\,\iota=\bigcap_{N\lhd_{f}G}N
,
\iota
is injective if and only if
G
is residually finite. Moreover, the profinite completion is functorial. Any homomorphism of abstract groups
f:H\to G
induces a homomorphism of profinite groups
\widehat{f}:\widehat{H}\to\widehat{G}
so that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}}

We list two useful properties of the completion functor.

Proposition 2.1

(
[
25
, Proposition 3.2.5]
)
.

The profinite completion functor is right exact, i.e. for a short exact sequence of abstract groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\;,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

there is an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}

Proposition 2.2

(
[
25
, Lemma 3.2.6]
)
.

If
f:H\to G
is injective, then
\widehat{f}
is injective if and only if the profinite topology on
G
induces the full profinite topology of
H
by
f
, i.e. for any finite-index subgroup
M\leq H
, there exists a finite-index subgroup
N\leq G
such that
f^{-1}(N)\subseteq M
.

For any subgroup
H\leq G
, we denote
\overline{H}
as the closure of
\iota(H)
in
\widehat{G}
. Note that
H
does not always inject into
\overline{H}
, for instance, when
G
is not residually finite. In addition, the surjective homomorphism
\widehat{H}\to\overline{H}
is an isomorphism if and only if the condition in
Proposition 2.2
holds.

Proposition 2.3

(
[
25
, Proposition 3.2.2]
)
.

Let
G
be an abstract group. Then there is a one-to-one correspondence.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\{\text{Finite-index subgroups of }G\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\{\text{Open subgroups of }\widehat{G}\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H}\cong\widehat{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1:1}

In addition, this correspondence sends normal subgroups to normal subgroups.

2.2.
Profinite isomorphism and finite quotients

The profinite completion encodes full data of finite quotients as stated in
Fact 1.2
. In this subsection, we introduce a more general version.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
G
be a group and
H_{1},\cdots,H_{m}
be a collection of subgroups. Denote the set of
finite quotients of
G
marked with conjugacy classes of subgroups as

\mathcal{C}(G;H_{1},\cdots,H_{m}):=\left\{\left.\left[\raise 2.32501pt\hbox{$G$}\big/\lower 2.32501pt\hbox{$U$};\,\raise 2.32501pt\hbox{$H_{1}$}\big/\lower 2.32501pt\hbox{$(H_{1}\cap U)$},\cdots,\raise 2.32501pt\hbox{$H_{m}$}\big/\lower 2.32501pt\hbox{$(H_{m}\cap U)$}\right]\,\right|\,U\lhd_{f}G\right\},

where
[A;B_{1},\cdots,B_{m}]
represents the isomorphism class of group marked with
m
conjugacy classes of subgroups. To be precise,

[A;B_{1},\cdots,B_{m}]=[A^{\prime};B_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,B_{m}^{\prime}]
if and only if there exists an isomorphism
f:A\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}A^{\prime}
, such that
f(B_{i})
is a conjugate of
B_{i}^{\prime}
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be finitely generated groups. Suppose
H_{1},\cdots,H_{m}
are subgroups of
G
, and
H_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,H_{m}^{\prime}
are subgroups of
G^{\prime}
.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathcal{C}(G;H_{1},\cdots,H_{m})=\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime};H_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,H_{m}^{\prime})
.

(2)

There exists an isomorphism as profinite groups
f:\widehat{G}\to\widehat{G^{\prime}}
, such that
f(\overline{H_{i}})
is a conjugate of
\overline{H_{i}^{\prime}}
in
\widehat{G^{\prime}}
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.

(3)

There exists an isomorphism as abstract groups
f:\widehat{G}\to\widehat{G^{\prime}}
, such that
f(\overline{H_{i}})
is a conjugate of
\overline{H_{i}^{\prime}}
in
\widehat{G^{\prime}}
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.

Remark 2.6
.

When
m=0
,
\mathcal{C}(G;)
is simply
\mathcal{C}(G)
defined as usual. Therefore,
Fact 1.2
is the special case of
Theorem 2.5
.

The proof of this theorem is a standard generalization of
[
7
]
(see also
[
25
, Theorem 3.2.7]
), together with the theorem of Nikolov-Segal
[
20
]
. As the proof is irrelevant with our mainline, we include the proof in
Appendix A
for completeness.

2.3.
Profinite completion and abelianization

Definition 2.7
.

(1)

Let
H
be an abstract group, the
abelianization
of
H
is denoted as
{H}^{\text{ab}}=H/[H,H]
, where
[H,H]
is the commutator subgroup.

(2)

Let
G
be a profinite group, the
topological abelianization
of
G
is denoted as
{G}^{\text{Ab}}=G/\overline{[G,G]}
, where
\overline{[G,G]}
is the closure of the commutator subgroup.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
G
be a group, there is a natural isomorphism
{\widehat{G}}^{\text{Ab}}\cong\widehat{{G}^{\text{ab}}}
.

Proof.

According to
Proposition 2.1
, the short exact sequence
1\to[G,G]\to G\to{G}^{\text{ab}}\to 1
induces an exact sequence of profinite groups
\widehat{[G,G]}\to\widehat{G}\to\widehat{{G}^{\text{ab}}}\to 1
. Therefore, there is a natural isomorphism
\widehat{G}/\overline{[G,G]}\cong\widehat{{G}^{\text{ab}}}
. It is obvious that
\overline{[G,G]}\subseteq\overline{[\widehat{G},\widehat{G}]}
. On the other hand, since the image of
G
is dense in
\widehat{G}
, we have
\overline{[\widehat{G},\widehat{G}]}\subseteq\overline{[G,G]}
. Thus,
\overline{[G,G]}=\overline{[\widehat{G},\widehat{G}]}
, and
\widehat{G}/\overline{[G,G]}=\widehat{G}/\overline{[\widehat{G},\widehat{G}]}={\widehat{G}}^{\text{Ab}}
.∎

For any abelian group
G
, let
\mathrm{tor}(G)
denote its torsion subgroup.
Let
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
denote the profinite completion of the infinite cyclic group. Then,
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
can be identified with the direct product of the
p
-adic integer rings, i.e.
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=\prod_{p:\,\text{prime}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated abelian group. Then
\widehat{G}
is also abelian, and

(1)

the inclusion
G\hookrightarrow\widehat{G}
induces an isomorphism
\mathrm{tor}(G)\cong\mathrm{tor}(\widehat{G})
;

(2)

there is a natural isomorphism
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{G}
;

(3)

there is a natural isomorphism
\widehat{G}/\mathrm{tor}(\widehat{G})\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{G/\mathrm{tor}(G)}
.

Proof.

For a direct product of groups
H=H_{1}\times H_{2}
, it is easy to verify that
\{N_{1}\times N_{2}\mid N_{1}\lhd_{f}H_{1},\,N_{2}\lhd_{f}H_{2}\}
is a cofinal system of the finite index normal subgroups of
H
. Therefore,

(2.10)

\widehat{H}=\varprojlim\limits_{N_{i}\lhd_{f}H_{i}}\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$H_{1}\times H_{2}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$N_{1}\times N_{2}$}=\varprojlim\limits_{N_{i}\lhd_{f}H_{i}}\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$H_{1}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$N_{1}$}\times\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$H_{2}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$N_{2}$}=\widehat{H_{1}}\times\widehat{H_{2}}.

We decompose the finitely generated abelian group
G
as
G=\mathbb{Z}^{m}\times\mathrm{tor}(G)
, where
\mathrm{tor}(G)
is a finite group. The above conclusion shows that
\widehat{G}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}\times\mathrm{tor}(G)
.
Since
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion-free, it follows that
\mathrm{tor}(\widehat{G})=\mathrm{tor}(G)
, finishing the proof of (1).

In addition, decomposing
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
as
\prod\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we have an isomorphism
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
for any prime power
p^{n}
. Any finite abelian group
T
can be decomposed as a direct sum of subgroups in the form of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
, so there is a natural isomorphism
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}T\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}T\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{T}
. Thus, the decomposition
G=\mathbb{Z}^{m}\times\mathrm{tor}(G)
implies (2).

(3) follows from the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{tor}(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G/\mathrm{tor}(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

deduced from
Proposition 2.1
and
Proposition 2.2
.
∎

For an abelian group
G
, denote
G_{\mathrm{free}}=G/\mathrm{tor}(G)
. Then,

Lemma 2.8
and
Lemma 2.9
together imply the following proposition.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
G,H
be finitely generated groups, and
f:\widehat{G}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{H}
be an isomorphism of profinite groups. Then
f
induces an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}:\;\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{G}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{H}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

so that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{G}}^{\text{Ab}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{H}}^{\text{Ab}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G}^{\text{ab}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{G}^{\text{ab}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{H}^{\text{ab}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H}^{\text{ab}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widehat{{G}^{\text{ab}}})_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widehat{{H}^{\text{ab}}})_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{G}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{{H}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{G}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{H}^{\text{ab}}_{\mathrm{free}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

3.
Bass-Serre theory in the profinite setting

3.1.
Graph of groups

In this paper, we will only focus on finite graph of groups, and we will omit the notation “finite” if the finiteness is clear.

A
finite oriented graph

\Gamma
consists of a finite set
\Gamma=V(\Gamma)\sqcup E(\Gamma)
, where
V(\Gamma)
and
E(\Gamma)
denote the set of vertices and edges respectively, and two
relation maps

d_{0},d_{1}:E(\Gamma)\to V(\Gamma)
, which assigns the two endpoints of each edge.

In the classical setting, a
finite graph of groups
, denoted by
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
, consists of a finite connected oriented graph
\Gamma
, a group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
associated to each
x\in V(\Gamma)\cup E(\Gamma)
, and injective homomorphisms
\varphi_{i}^{e}:\mathcal{G}_{e}\to\mathcal{G}_{d_{i}(e)}
for each
e\in E(\Gamma)
and
i=0,1
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
be a finite graph of groups and let
S
be a maximal subtree of
\Gamma
, the fundamental group with respect to
S
is defined as

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma,S)=\frac{\left(\ast_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathcal{G}_{v}\right)\ast F_{E(\Gamma)}}{\left\langle\!\left\langle t_{e}:e\in E(S),\,\varphi_{1}^{e}(g)^{-1}t_{e}\varphi_{0}^{e}(g)t_{e}^{-1}:e\in E(\Gamma)\text{ and }g\in\mathcal{G}_{e}\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}\>,

where
F_{E({\Gamma})}
is the free group over
E({\Gamma})
with generators
t_{e}
,
e\in E(\Gamma)
.

It follows from
[
30
, Proposition 20]
that up to isomorphism,
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma,S)
is independent with the choice of the maximal subtree
S
. Thus, we may omit the notation
S
and simply denote the fundamental group as
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
when there is no need for a precise maximal subtree.

A geometric interpretation of graph of groups turns out to be a graph of topological spaces. Indeed, let
X_{v}
(resp.
X_{e}
) be CW-complexes so that
\pi_{1}X_{v}=\mathcal{G}_{v}
(resp.
\pi_{1}X_{e}=\mathcal{G}_{e}
), and let
\phi_{i}^{e}:X_{e}\to X_{d_{i}(e)}
be the maps (up to homotopy) so that
\phi_{i}^{e}
induces
\varphi_{i}^{e}:\pi_{1}X_{e}=\mathcal{G}_{e}\to\pi_{1}X_{d_{i}(e)}=\mathcal{G}_{d_{i}(e)}
. We can construct a CW-complex according to this information.

\displaystyle X_{\Gamma}=\left(\bigsqcup_{e\in E(\Gamma)}X_{e}\times[0,1]\right)\bigcup_{\phi_{i}^{e}:\,X_{e}\times\{i\}\to X_{d_{i}(e)}}\left(\bigsqcup_{v\in V(\Gamma)}X_{v}\right)

It turns out that
\pi_{1}(X_{\Gamma})\cong\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
, see
[
29
]
.
In view of this method, the fundamental group of an irreducible 3-manifold splits as a graph of group through JSJ-decomposition.

The theory of graph of groups generalizes to profinite groups.

Definition 3.2
.

A
finite graph of profinite groups
, denoted by
(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
, consists of a finite, connected, oriented graph
\Gamma
, a profinite group
\mathcal{P}_{x}
for each
x\in V(\Gamma)\cup E(\Gamma)
, and a continuous injective homomorphism
\varphi_{i}^{e}:\mathcal{P}_{e}\to\mathcal{P}_{d_{i}(e)}
for each
e\in E(\Gamma)
and
i=0,1
.

Analogous to
Definition 3.1
, we can define a profinite version of the fundamental group of a finite graph of profinite groups following

[
24
]
.
Let
G\amalg H
denote the free profinite product of two profinite groups
G
and
H
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
be a finite graph of profinite groups and let
S
be a maximal subtree of
\Gamma
. The
profinite fundamental group
with respect to
S
is defined as

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma,S)=\left.\left((\amalg_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathcal{P}_{v})\amalg\mathcal{F}_{E(\Gamma)}\right)\right/N,

where
\mathcal{F}_{E({\Gamma})}
is the free profinite group over the finite space
E({\Gamma})
with generators denoted by
t_{e}
(
e\in E(\Gamma)
), and

N={\overline{\left\langle\!\left\langle t_{e}:e\in E(S),\,\varphi_{1}^{e}(g)^{-1}t_{e}\varphi_{0}^{e}(g)t_{e}^{-1}:e\in E(\Gamma)\text{ and }g\in\mathcal{P}_{e}\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}}

is the minimal closed normal subgroup containing the generators.

\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma,S)
is a profinite group, and is irrelevant, up to isomorphism, with the choice of the maximal subtree
S
as shown by
[
24
, Theorem 6.2.4]
. Similarly, we denote the profinite fundamental group by
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
if the clarification of the maximal subtree is not essential.

Definition 3.4
.

A finite graph of profinite groups
(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
is called
injective
if for any vertex
v\in\Gamma
, the homomorphism
\mathcal{P}_{v}\to(\amalg_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathcal{P}_{v})\amalg\mathcal{F}_{E(\Gamma)}\to\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
is injective.

Note that there exist non-injective examples in the profinite settings as constructed by
[
23
]
, though injectivity always holds
in the classical settings, see

[
10
, Proposition 6.2.1]
.

One may relate a graph of abstract groups with a graph of profinite groups as shown by the following construction.
Let
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
be a finite graph of groups, and suppose that the profinite topology of
\mathcal{G}_{d_{i}(e)}
induces the full profinite topology on
\mathcal{G}_{e}
through
\varphi_{i}^{e}
for any
e\in E(\Gamma)
and
i=0,1
. Then
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)
is a finite graph of profinite groups, where each vertex group or edge group (
\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{v}}
or
\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{e}}
) is the profinite completion of the corresponding vertex or edge group. The continuous homomorphism
\widehat{\varphi_{i}^{e}}:\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{e}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{d_{i}(e)}}
is indeed injective by
Proposition 2.2
.

From
Definition 3.1
and
Definition 3.3
, one establishes a group homomorphism
\psi:\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)\to\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)
through the canonical homomorphisms
\mathcal{G}_{v}\to\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{v}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{e}\to\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{e}}
. Clearly, the image of
\psi
is dense in
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)
. It follows that
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)
is indeed the profinite completion of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
under suitable conditions.

Definition 3.5
.

A graph of groups
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
is called
efficient
if the following three properties hold.

(1)

\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
is residually finite.

(2)

The profinite topology of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
induces the full profinite topology on each vertex subgroup
\mathcal{G}_{v}
and each edge subgroup
\mathcal{G}_{e}
.

(3)

All vertex subgroups and edge subgroups are separable in
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
.

Proposition 3.6

(
[
24
, Theorem 6.5.3]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
be a finite graph of group which is efficient.

(1)

\widehat{\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)}
and
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)
are isomorphic as profinite groups. Indeed, there is an isomorphism such that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(2)

(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)
is injective.

In view of (
2
), we usually identify
\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{v}}
with its image in
\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\Gamma)
.

3.2.
Profinite Bass-Serre tree

The classical construction of a Bass-Serre tree turns out to be a profinite tree in the profinite settings. We briefly introduce the definitions in this subsection, and refer the readers to
[
24
]
for more details.

Definition 3.7
.

(1)

A
profinite graph
consists of a profinite space
\Gamma
, a closed subspace
V(\Gamma)\subset\Gamma
, and two continuous maps
d_{0},d_{1}:\Gamma\to V(\Gamma)
such that
d_{i}|_{V(\Gamma)}=id
. We denote
E(\Gamma):=\Gamma\setminus V(\Gamma)
. An element in
V(\Gamma)
is called a
vertex
and an element in
E(\Gamma)
is called an
edge
.

By an
abstract graph
we mean a combinatorial graph not assigning a specific topology. The notation distinguishes from a profinite graph which is equipped with a compact, Hausdorff, totally disconnected topology.

(2)

Let
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
be two profinite graphs. An
isomorphism

\Phi:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a homeomorphism which is meanwhile an isomorphism of abstract oriented graphs, i.e.
\Phi(d_{i}(x))=d_{i}^{\prime}(\Phi(x))
for any
x\in\Gamma
and
i=0,1
.

(3)

A profinite graph
\Gamma
is a
profinite tree
if the following chain complex is exact.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}[\![(E(\Gamma),\ast)]\!]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}[\![V(\Gamma)]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

(4)

We say that
a profinite group
G
acts on a profinite graph
\Gamma
if there is a combinatorial group action
G\curvearrowright\Gamma
so that the map
G\times\Gamma\to\Gamma
,
(g,x)\mapsto g\cdot x
is continuous.

(5)

A
path
of length
n
in a profinite graph
\Gamma
consists of
n+1
vertices
v_{0},\cdots,v_{n}\in V(\Gamma)
, and
n
edges
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\in E(\Gamma)
, such that
e_{i}
adjoins
v_{i-1}
and
v_{i}
. The path is called
injective
if
v_{0},\cdots,v_{n}
are
n+1
distinct vertices.

(6)

The action by a profinite group
G
on a profinite tree
T
is called
n
-acylindrical
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
if the stabilizer of any injective path of length greater than
n
is trivial.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
be a finite graph of profinite groups, and denote
\Pi=\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
. For each
v\in V(\Gamma)
, let
\Pi_{v}
denote the image of
\mathcal{P}_{v}
in
\Pi
through the map
\mathcal{P}_{v}\to(\amalg_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathcal{P}_{v})\amalg\mathcal{F}_{E(\Gamma)}\to\Pi
, and for each
e\in E(\Gamma)
, let
\Pi_{e}
denote the image of
\mathcal{P}_{e}
in
\Pi
through the map
\varphi_{1}^{e}:\mathcal{P}_{e}\to\mathcal{P}_{d_{1}(e)}
. Then, for each
x\in V(\Gamma)\cup E(\Gamma)
,
\Pi_{x}
is a closed subgroup in
\Pi
, so the coset space
\Pi/\Pi_{v}
is a profinite space.
Let

V(T)=\bigsqcup_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\Pi/\Pi_{v},\quad E(T)=\bigsqcup_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\Pi/\Pi_{e},\quad\text{and }T=V(T)\bigsqcup E(T)

be equipped with disjoint-union topology. The relation map is given by

d_{0}(g\Pi_{e})=gt_{e}\Pi_{d_{0}(e)},\quad d_{1}(g\Pi_{e})=g\Pi_{d_{1}(e)},\quad\forall e\in E(\Gamma),g\in\Pi.

Then
T
is called the
profinite Bass-Serre tree
associated to
(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
.

Indeed,
T
is a disjoint union of finitely many profinite spaces, and hence
T
is a profinite space.
It is easy to verify that the relation maps
d_{0},d_{1}
are continuous.
Moreover,
[
24
, Corollary 6.3.6]
shows that
T
is indeed a profinite tree. In fact, omitting the topology and the orientation,
T
is combinatorially a disjoint union of abstract trees, see
[
24
, Exercise 2.4.4]
.

We conclude the profinite version of Bass-Serre theory as the following proposition.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
be a finite graph of profinite groups, and let
T
be the associated profinite Bass-Serre tree. Then, there is a natural action by
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
on
T
through left multiplication, which is obviously continuous. In addition,
T/\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)=\Gamma
, and the stabilizer of each vertex or edge in
T
are conjugates of the image of
\mathcal{P}_{v}
or
\mathcal{P}_{e}
in
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
.

3.3.
Congruent isomorphism

In
Definition 1.8
we have defined the notion of a congruent isomorphism between graph of groups. Let us recall its definition.

A
congruent isomorphism

f_{\bullet}
between two finite graphs of abstract/profinite groups
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
and
(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
consists of the following ingredients.

(1)

There is an isomorphism of oriented graphs
{f^{\dagger}}:\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma^{\prime}
;

(2)

For each
x\in V(\Gamma)\cup E(\Gamma)
, there is an isomorphism of abstract/profinite groups
f_{x}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(x)}
;

(3)

For each
e\in E(\Gamma)
and
i=0,1
, there exists an element
h_{i}^{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(d_{i}(e))}
such that

h_{i}^{e}\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})_{i}^{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}\left(f_{e}(g)\right)\cdot(h_{i}^{e})^{-1}=f_{d_{i}(e)}\left(\varphi_{i}^{e}(g)\right),\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{G}_{e}.

In other words, the following diagram commutes.

(3.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{d_{i}(e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(d_{i}(e))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(d_{i}(e))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}^{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d_{i}(e)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi^{\prime})_{i}^{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{i}^{e}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The following two results are direct generalizations from the classical Bass-Serre theory into the profinite version.

First, being congruently isomorphic is a sufficient condition for having isomorphic profinite fundamental groups.

Proposition 3.11
.

Suppose
f_{\bullet}
is a congruent isomorphism between two finite graphs of profinite groups
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
and
(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
. Then
f_{\bullet}
induces an isomorphism
f:\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
, such that for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
, there exists
x_{v}\in\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
so that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{x_{v}}}

As a corollary, for any connected subgraph
\Delta\subseteq\Gamma
, denote
\Delta^{\prime}={f^{\dagger}}(\Delta)
. Then
f_{\bullet}
induces an isomorphism
f_{\Delta}:\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Delta)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Delta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

This follows from the same proof of
[
6
, Chapter 8, Lemma 21]
in the classical Bass-Serre theory. One can also prove this proposition directly from
Definition 3.3
, by induction on
\#E(\Gamma)
starting from the simplest cases of HNN-extensions and amalgamations.
∎

Conversely, a profinite group may split as a graph of profinite groups in many ways. The following lemma provides a method based on Bass-Serre theory to show when two splittings are essentially the same, i.e. when two graphs of profinite groups are congruently isomorphic.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
and
(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
be two injective finite graphs of profinite groups, and let
T
and
T^{\prime}
denote their profinite Bass-Serre trees. Suppose there exist an isomorphism of profinite fundamental groups
f:\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
and a
f
-equivariant isomorphism of oriented abstract graphs
\Phi:T\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}T^{\prime}
on which the profinite fundamental groups act. Then
f
induces a congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
between
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
and
(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We denote the quotient maps as

p:T\to T/\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)=\Gamma\quad\text{and}\quad q:T^{\prime}\to T^{\prime}/\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})=\Gamma^{\prime}.

Then, the
f
-equivariant isomorphism
\Phi
induces a combinatorial isomorphism between the oriented quotient graphs, which we denote as
{f^{\dagger}}:\Gamma=T/\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}T^{\prime}/\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})=\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Since
(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
is injective, we can identify
\mathcal{G}_{v}
with its image in
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
, and identify
\mathcal{G}_{e}
with its image in
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
through the map
\varphi_{1}^{e}:\mathcal{G}_{e}\to\mathcal{G}_{d_{1}(e)}
. The similar notation follows for
(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
.
Then,
for each
x\in\Gamma
,
\mathcal{G}_{x}\leq\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
is the stabilizer of a pre-image
\widetilde{x}\in p^{-1}(x)\subseteq T
that corresponds to the coset
\mathcal{G}_{x}
in
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)/\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Likewise, for each
y\in\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{H}_{y}\leq\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
is the stabilizer of a pre-image
\widetilde{y}\in q^{-1}(y)\subseteq T^{\prime}
that corresponds to the coset
\mathcal{H}_{y}
in
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})/\mathcal{H}_{y}
.

For each
x\in\Gamma
,
\Phi(p^{-1}(x))=q^{-1}({f^{\dagger}}(x))
, so
\Phi(\widetilde{x})
and
\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(x)}
belong to the same orbit of
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
. To be explicit, there exists
h_{x}\in\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
such that
\Phi(\widetilde{x})=h_{x}\cdot\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(x)}
. As a result,
f
induces an isomorphism of profinite groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{x}:\mathcal{G}_{x}=\mathrm{Stab}(\widetilde{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Stab}(\Phi(\widetilde{x}))=\mathrm{Stab}(h_{x}{\tiny\cdot}\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(x)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Stab}(\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(x)})=\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(x)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{x}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Given
e\in E(\Gamma)
and
i=0,1
, denote
v=d_{i}(e)
and the relation map
\varphi_{i}^{e}
can be determined as follows.
d_{i}(\widetilde{e})\in p^{-1}(v)
belongs to the same orbit of
\widetilde{v}
, and there exists a specific element
g\in\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)
such that
d_{i}(\widetilde{e})=g\cdot\widetilde{d_{i}(e)}=g\cdot\widetilde{v}
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{i}_{e}:\;\mathcal{G}_{e}=\mathrm{Stab}(\widetilde{e})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Stab}(d_{i}(\widetilde{e}))=\mathrm{Stab}(g\cdot\widetilde{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Stab}(\widetilde{v})=\mathcal{G}_{v}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-1}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Similarly, there exists
h\in\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
such that
d_{i}^{\prime}(\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(e)})=h\cdot\widetilde{d_{i}^{\prime}({f^{\dagger}}(e))}=h\cdot\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
, and the relation map
(\varphi^{\prime})^{i}_{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}
in
(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
is decomposed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi^{\prime})^{i}_{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}:\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}=\mathrm{Stab}(\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(e)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Stab}(d_{i}^{\prime}(\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Stab}(h\cdot\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(v)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Stab}(\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(v)})=\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-1}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Thus, the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{e}}^{-1}\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{e}^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{v}}^{-1}\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h^{-1}h_{e}^{-1}f(g)h_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{{f^{\dagger}}(e)}^{\prime i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h^{-1}}}

In fact,
h^{-1}h_{e}^{-1}f(g)h_{v}\in\mathrm{Stab}(\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(v)})=\mathcal{H}_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
as shown by the following relations.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\widetilde{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(g\cdot\widetilde{v})=\Phi(d_{i}(\widetilde{e}))=d_{i}(\Phi(\widetilde{e}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}^{\prime}(\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(e)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

This implies the commutative diagram (
3.10
) by taking

{h_{i}^{e}=(h^{-1}h_{e}^{-1}f(g)h_{v})^{-1}}.

Therefore,
f_{\bullet}
is indeed a congruent isomorphism.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

(1)

It is important that the graphs of profinite groups are injective, so that we can identify the profinite groups
\mathcal{G}_{v}
with the vertex stabilizers in
T
. The remaining proof is in fact the same as in classical Bass-Serre theory, where all graphs of (abstract) groups are automatically injective according to
[
10
, Proposition 6.2.1]
.

(2)

The isomorphism of abstract graphs
\Phi:T\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}T^{\prime}
is indeed an isomorphism of profinite graphs. In fact, for each
x\in\Gamma
, we can choose a preimage
\tilde{x}\in T
. Then
\Phi(\tilde{x})
is also a preimage of
{f^{\dagger}}(x)\in\Gamma^{\prime}
. By construction of the profinite Bass-Serre trees,

T=\bigsqcup_{x\in\Gamma}\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{$\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)$}\Big/\lower 4.30554pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Stab}({\tilde{x}})$}\,,\;T^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{x\in\Gamma}\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{$\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})$}\Big/\lower 4.30554pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Stab}({\Phi(\tilde{x})})$}

are equipped with disjoint union topology of coset spaces. The
f
-equivariance of
\Phi
implies that
f
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathrm{Stab}({\tilde{x}})\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}{\mathrm{Stab}({\Phi(\tilde{x})})}
, which is an isomorphism as profinite groups. Thus,
\Phi
is a homeomorphism on each coset space, and is thus a homeomorphism on the disjoint union.

(3)

Moreover,
f_{\bullet}
again induces an isomorphism
f^{\prime}:\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{G},\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{H},\Gamma^{\prime})
by
Proposition 3.11
. It follows that
f
and
f^{\prime}
only differ by choices of sections of
T\to\Gamma
and
T^{\prime}\to\Gamma^{\prime}
in defining the profinite fundamental groups, resulting possible conjugations. This ambiguity can be eliminated as shown by
[
24
, Theorem 6.2.4]
.

4.
Profinite detection of prime decomposition

Let
M
be a compact orientable 3-manifold. Then
M
can be decomposed as a connect sum

M\cong M_{1}\#\cdots\#M_{m}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{\#r}
via the
Kneser-Milnor prime decomposition
,
where each
M_{i}
is irreducible and
M_{i}\not\cong S^{3}
. In addition, the numbers
m
and
r
are unique, and
the prime factors
M_{i}
are uniquely determined up to homeomorphism and reordering.

In fact, when
M
is boundary-incompressible, the Kneser-Milnor prime decomposition is exactly correspondent to the prime decomposition of its fundamental group as a free product
[
13
]
. Thus, the numbers
m
and
r
as well as the fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(M_{i})
can be uniquely determined by
\pi_{1}(M)
, through a group theoretical method.

The similar conclusion holds in the profinite settings.

Theorem 4.1

(
[
41
, Theorem 6.22]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be two compact, orientable, boundary-incompressible 3-manifolds, with prime decomposition
M\cong M_{1}\#\cdots\#M_{m}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{\#r}
and
N\cong N_{1}\#\cdots\#N_{n}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{\#s}
, where
M_{i}
and
N_{j}
are irreducible. Suppose
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. Then
m=n
,
r=s
, and up to reordering,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{i}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{i}}
.

In combination with the cohomological goodness of 3-manifold groups
[
5
]
,
Theorem 4.1
implies the following corollary which might be familiar to experts.

Corollary 4.2
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are two compact, orientable, boundary-incompressible 3-manifolds, and
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
.

(1)

If
M
is irreducible, then so is
N
.

(2)

If
M
is closed, then so is
N
.

(3)

If
\partial M
only consists of tori, then so does
\partial N
.

Proof.

Let
M\cong M_{1}\#\cdots\#M_{m}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{\#r}
and
N\cong N_{1}\#\cdots\#N_{n}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{\#s}
be the Kneser-Milnor prime decompositions of
M
and
N
. According to
Theorem 4.1
,
m=n
,
r=s
, and up to a reordering,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{i}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{i}}
.

This directly implies (
1
), which is equivalent to
m=1
and
r=0
. For (
2
) and (
3
), it suffices to prove the conclusion for each pair of irreducible factors
M_{i}
and
N_{i}
.

If
M_{i}
has finite fundamental group, then
M_{i}
is closed and
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{i}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{i}}\cong\pi_{1}M_{i}
is also finite. Since
\pi_{1}N_{i}
is residually finite
[
14
]
, it follows that
\pi_{1}N_{i}
is also finite and
N_{i}
is also closed.

If
M_{i}
has infinite fundamental group, then
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{i}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{i}}
is also infinite, and so is
\pi_{1}N_{i}
. The standard application of sphere theorem implies that
M_{i}
and
N_{i}
are aspherical.
[
5
, Theorem 3.5.1
\&
Lemma 3.7.1]
shows that
\pi_{1}M_{i}
and
\pi_{1}N_{i}
are cohomologically good in the sense of Serre
[
31
, pp. 15-16]
. In particular, for any prime number
p
, there is an isomorphism
H^{k}(\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{i}},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong H^{k}(\pi_{1}M_{i},\mathbb{F}_{p})
induced by inclusion, and similarly,
H^{k}(\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{i}},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong H^{k}(\pi_{1}N_{i},\mathbb{F}_{p})
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
M_{i}
and
N_{i}
are aspherical, we obtain

\displaystyle H^{k}(M_{i};\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong H^{k}(\pi_{1}M_{i},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong H^{k}(\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{i}},\mathbb{F}_{p})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle H^{k}(\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{i}},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong H^{k}(\pi_{1}N_{i},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong H^{k}(N_{i};\mathbb{F}_{p}),

\displaystyle\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

Consequently,
M_{i}
is closed if and only if
H^{3}(M_{i};\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong H^{3}(N_{i};\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all prime numbers
p
, which is equivalent to
N_{i}
being closed. This proves (
2
).

In addition, the Poincaré duality implies
\chi(\partial M_{i})=2\chi(M_{i})
. Thus, when
M_{i}
and
N_{i}
have infinite fundamental groups,

\displaystyle\chi(\partial M_{i})=2\chi(M_{i})=2\chi(N_{i})=\chi(\partial N_{i}).

Note that
\partial M_{i}
consists of tori if and only if
M_{i}
is not closed and
\chi(\partial M_{i})=0
, and likewise for
N_{i}
. This proves (
3
).
∎

5.
Profinite detection of JSJ-decomposition

Based on
Theorem 4.1
, we may restrict ourselves to the profinite properties of irreducible 3-manifolds. The technique of this paper mainly adapts to irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary, as they admit JSJ-decomposition by merely tori.
In this section, we shall briefly review the ingredients of
[
45
, Theorem B]
which encodes the information of JSJ-decomposition from the profinite completion, and obtain a new description of this result (
Theorem 5.5
) which better fits in the technique of this paper.

5.1.
JSJ-decomposition as a graph of groups

The JSJ-decomposition together with the hyperbolization theorem yields the modern version as follows, and we refer the readers to
[
1
, Theorem 1.7.6]
for general references.

Theorem 5.1

(JSJ-Decomposition)
.

Let
M
be a compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifold with empty or incompressible toral boundary. Then there exists a minimal collection
\mathcal{T}
of disjoint embedded incompressible tori, such that each connected component of
M\setminus\mathcal{T}
is either a Seifert fibered space or a finite-volume hyperbolic manifold. In addition,
\mathcal{T}
is unique up to isotopy.

We say that the JSJ-decomposition is
non-trivial
, if
\mathcal{T}
is non-empty. The JSJ-decomposition identifies
\pi_{1}M
as the fundamental group of a graph of groups, based on the geometric construction introduced in
§ 3.1
. We denote this graph of group
(\mathcal{G}_{M},\Gamma_{M})
as the
JSJ-graph of groups
of
M
. Indeed, the underlying graph
\Gamma_{M}
is a finite graph, the edge groups are
\pi_{1}(T^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, and the vertex groups are the fundamental groups of the Seifert fibered or hyperbolic JSJ-pieces.

Wilton-Zalesskii
[
42
, Theorem A]
showed that the JSJ-graph of groups is efficient when
M
is closed, based on the finite covers of the geometric pieces constructed by Hamilton
[
12
]
, and the criteria for residual finiteness by Hempel
[
14
]
. The proof works exactly the same when
M
has incompressible toral boundary, see also
[
39
]
.

Proposition 5.2

(
[
42
, Theorem A]
)
.

Let
M
be a compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifold with empty or incompressible toral boundary. Then the JSJ-graph of groups of
M
is efficient.

Let
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
be the profinite completion of the JSJ-graph of groups, which we denote as the
JSJ-graph of profinite groups
. Then
Proposition 5.2
together with
Proposition 3.6
implies that
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
is injective, and

\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M}).

5.2.
Standard profinite tree

As we have established an isomorphism
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
,
by
Proposition 3.9
,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
acts on the corresponding profinite Bass-Serre tree, which we refer to as the
standard profinite tree

\hat{T}_{M}
.

Proposition 5.3

(
[
40
, Proposition 6.8]
)
.

Let
M
be a compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifold with empty or incompressible toral boundary. Suppose
M
is not a closed 3-manifold admitting
Sol
-geometry. Then the action by
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
on the standard profinite tree
\hat{T}_{M}
is 4-acylindrical.

In fact,
[
40
, Proposition 6.8]
was only stated for closed manifolds, while the case-by-case proof adapts with no alteration to the setting with toral boundary.

The following trailblazing result was first proven by Wilton-Zalesskii
[
45
]
.

Proposition 5.4

(
[
45
, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary, which are not closed 3-manifolds admitting
Sol
-geometry. Suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism of profinite groups. We may forget the topology and the orientation of the standard profinite trees
\hat{T}_{M}
and
\hat{T}_{N}
, and view them as abstract combinatorial graphs. Then there exists an
f
-equivariant isomorphism of abstract unoriented graphs
\Phi:\hat{T}_{M}\to\hat{T}_{N}
.

In fact,
[
45
, Theorem 4.3]
proved this proposition in the closed case.
In general, the two essential steps, the efficiency of the JSJ-graph of groups and the acylindricity of
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\curvearrowright\hat{T}_{M}
, also work for the bounded case as shown by
Proposition 5.2
and
Proposition 5.3
. The remaining proof follows from
[
45
, Theorem 4.3]
with no alteration.

5.3.
Congruent isomorphism for JSJ-decomposition

Combining
Proposition 5.4
with
Lemma 3.12
, we can transform the result of Wilton-Zalesskii into the formulation of congruent isomorphisms between JSJ-graphs of profinite groups.

Theorem 5.5
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are two orientable, irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary, which are not closed 3-manifolds admitting
Sol
-geometry. If
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism of profinite groups, then
f
induces a congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
between their JSJ-graph of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma_{N})
after reassigning orientations of the JSJ-graphs. In addition,
{f^{\dagger}}
sends hyperbolic vertices to hyperbolic vertices, and sends Seifert fibered vertices to Seifert fibered vertices.

Proof.

Let us first explain the assignment of orientations.
By
Proposition 5.4
, there is an
f
-equivariant isomorphism
\Phi:\hat{T}_{M}\to\hat{T}_{N}
as abstract unoriented graphs. The
f
-equivariance implies an isomorphism between the unoriented quotient graphs
{f^{\dagger}}:\Gamma_{M}=\hat{T}_{M}/\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\to\hat{T}_{N}/\widehat{\pi_{1}N}=\Gamma_{N}
.
We can choose orientations on
\Gamma_{M}
and
\Gamma_{N}
, so that the induced
{f^{\dagger}}:\Gamma_{M}\to\Gamma_{N}
respects orientation. The orientations lift to
\hat{T}_{M}
and
\hat{T}_{N}
so that the combinatorial isomorphism
\Phi:\hat{T}_{M}\to\hat{T}_{N}
also respects the orientation.

Recall that the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma_{N})
are injective, according to
Proposition 5.2
and
Proposition 3.6
. As a result,
f
induces a congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graph of profinite groups as shown by
Lemma 3.12
.

The detection of hyperbolic or Seifert type of the vertices follows from the works of Wilton-Zalesskii
[
43
,
44
]
(see also
[
22
, Theorem 4.18 and 4.20]
), where they showed that a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold and a compact Seifert fibered space do not have profinitely isomorphic fundamental groups.
∎

6.
Peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity

In
Definition 1.11
, we introduced the definition of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity. In this section, we shall present a detailed explanation on this concept.
Let us first describe the peripheral subgroups in the profinite completion of a 3-manifold group.

Lemma 6.1
.

Suppose
M
is a compact, orientable 3-manifold with incompressible toral boundary. For each peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, there is a natural isomorphism

\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)}\cong\overline{\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)}.

In particular,
\overline{\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

[
12
, Theorem 1]
shows that any abelian subgroup of
\pi_{1}M
is separable in
\pi_{1}M
. Thus, all finite-index subgroups of
\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
are separable in
\pi_{1}M
, and consequently,
the profinite topology of
\pi_{1}M
induces the full profinite topology on
\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)
.
This lemma then follows
Proposition 2.2
which implies
\widehat{\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)}\cong\overline{\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)}
, and
Lemma 2.9
which implies
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)}
.
∎

6.1.
Peripheral structure in the profinite completion

6.1.1.
The hyperbolic case

For a cusped hyperbolic 3-manifold, the peripheral subgroups form a malnormal family in its fundamental group. The same conclusion holds in the profinite completion.

Lemma 6.2

(
[
43
, Lemma 4.5]
)
.

Let
M
be a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold with cusps. Let
\left\{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\right\}
be the conjugacy representatives of the peripheral subgroups. Then their closures
\{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\}
in the profinite completion
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
form a malnormal family, i.e.
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\cap g\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M}g^{-1}
is trivial unless
i=j
and
g\in\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
.

In particular, the closures of distinct peripheral subgroups are not conjugate, and
N_{\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds, and
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism. Then
f
respects the peripheral structure.

Proof.

[
45
, Proposition 3.1]
shows that any closed subgroup of
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
conjugates into the closure of a unique peripheral subgroup. In particular, when
M
is closed,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
does not contain any closed subgroup isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
by
[
43
, Theorem A]
.

According to
Lemma 6.2
, the closure of any peripheral subgroup in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
is malnormal. Thus, it cannot be properly contained in any abelian subgroup. Therefore, the closure of the peripheral subgroups in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
are exactly the distinct conjugacy representatives of the maximal closed subgroups in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.

This holds likewise for
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. Thus, the peripheral structure of a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold is given by the conjugacy classes of the maximal closed
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
subgroups, and is preserved by the isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
.
∎

6.1.2.
The Seifert fibered case

Any compact orientable Seifert fibered space
M
with non-empty incompressible toral boundary falls into exactly one of the following three cases.

(1)

M\cong S^{1}\times S^{1}\times I
. In this case, the two boundary components are parallel.

(2)

M\cong S^{1}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$\SavedStyle\times$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.65277pt\raisebox{6.33333pt}{\hbox{$\SavedStyle_{\lx@scalerel@obj{\mkern 1.0mu\sim}}$}}\hskip-3.65277pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}}S^{1}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$\SavedStyle\times$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.65277pt\raisebox{6.33333pt}{\hbox{$\SavedStyle_{\lx@scalerel@obj{\mkern 1.0mu\sim}}$}}\hskip-3.65277pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}}I
is the twisted
I
-bundle over Klein bottle, which is also referred to as the
minor Seifert manifold
following the notation of Wilkes
[
38
]
.

(3)

M
fibers over a base orbifold
\mathcal{O}
with negative Euler characteristic. This is referred to as a
major Seifert manifold
following
[
38
]
.

Lemma 6.4

(
[
40
, Lemma 6.4]
)
.

Suppose
M
is a major Seifert fibered manifold. Let
\{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\}
be the conjugacy representatives of the peripheral subgroups, and let
K
be the infinite cyclic subgroup of
\pi_{1}M
generated by a regular fiber. Then for
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
,
g\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}g^{-1}\cap\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M}=\overline{K}
unless
i=j
and
g\in\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
.

In particular, the closures of distinct peripheral subgroups are not conjugate, and
N_{\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
M
be the minor Seifert fibered manifold, and let
\pi_{1}\partial M
be the peripheral subgroup of the unique boundary component. Then,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}
is an index-2 normal subgroup in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
, and each
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\setminus\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}
acts by conjugation on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}
as the profinite completion of an involution in
\mathrm{Aut}(\pi_{1}\partial M)
.

Proof.

It is known that
\pi_{1}M\cong\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}=\left\langle a\right\rangle\rtimes\left\langle b\right\rangle
, where
bab^{-1}=a^{-1}
.

Proposition 2.1
and
Proposition 2.2
then imply that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\rtimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=\widehat{\langle a\rangle}\rtimes\widehat{\langle b\rangle}
. For any
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
,
b^{\lambda}
acts on
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=\widehat{\left\langle a\right\rangle}
as scalar multiplication by
(-1)^{\lambda\pmod{2}}
.

Note that the peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\partial M=\left\langle a\right\rangle\times\left\langle b^{2}\right\rangle
is an index-2 normal subgroup in
\pi_{1}M
. Thus by
Proposition 2.3
,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}=\widehat{\left\langle a\right\rangle}\times\widehat{\left\langle b^{2}\right\rangle}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is also an index-2 normal subgroup in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\rtimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Any element
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\setminus\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}
can be expressed as
g=bh
for some
h\in\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}
, and
b
acts by conjugation on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}
as the profinite completion of the involution
\sigma\in\mathrm{Aut}(\pi_{1}\partial M)
defined by
\sigma(a)=a^{-1}
and
\sigma(b^{2})=b^{2}
. Then,
g
also acts by conjugation on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M}
as
\widehat{\sigma}
.
∎

6.1.3.
The general case

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose
M
is a compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifold with incompressible toral boundary, and
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
acts on the standard profinite tree
\hat{T}_{M}
. Let
\partial_{i}M
be a boundary component of
M
, and let
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
be a conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup. Then the subgroup
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\subseteq\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
fixes a unique vertex in
\hat{T}_{M}
.

Proof.

It suffices to assume
M
has non-trivial JSJ-decomposition. Suppose
\partial_{i}M
belongs to a JSJ-piece
M_{x}
. Up to conjugation, we may assume
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{x}\subseteq\pi_{1}M
. According to
Proposition 3.9
,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}
fixes a vertex
v\in V(\hat{T}_{M})
, so that
v
belongs to the pre-image of
x
under the quotient map
\hat{T}_{M}\to\Gamma_{M}
. Thus,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
also fixes
v
.

Suppose
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
fixes another vertex
w\neq v\in V(\hat{T}_{M})
. Then,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
fixes pointwise the minimal profinite subtree
\Delta
containing
v
and
w
(see
[
24
, Proposition 2.4.9]
). By
Definition 3.8
,
E(\hat{T}_{M})
is closed in the disjoint union topology
\hat{T}_{M}=V(\hat{T}_{M})\bigsqcup E(\hat{T}_{M})
, so
E(\Delta)=E(\hat{T}_{M})\cap\Delta
is also closed in
\Delta
. Then, according to
[
24
, Proposition 2.1.6 (c)]
,
\Delta
contains an edge
e
adjoint to the vertex
v
.
Let
\partial_{k}M_{x}
denote the boundary component of
M_{x}
given by the JSJ-torus corresponding to the image of
e
in
\Gamma_{M}
. Then,
\partial_{i}M\neq\partial_{k}M_{x}
. In particular,
\#\partial M_{x}\geq 2
, so
M_{x}
is either a hyperbolic or a major Seifert piece.

In addition, according to
Proposition 3.9
,
\mathrm{Stab}(e)
is conjugate to
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{x}}
within
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}=\mathrm{Stab}(v)
. Thus,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\subseteq h\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{x}}h^{-1}
for some
h\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}
. However, this yields a contradiction with
Lemma 6.2
if
M_{x}
is hyperbolic, or with
Lemma 6.4
if
M_{x}
is a major Seifert piece, finishing the proof.
∎

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
M
be a compact, orientable 3-manifold with incompressible toral boundary. For each boundary component
\partial_{i}M
, let
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
denote a conjugacy representative of peripheral subgroup in
\pi_{1}M
.

(1)

Let
\partial_{i}M
and
\partial_{j}M
be two different boundary components of
M
.

(i) If
\partial_{i}M
and
\partial_{j}M
belong to the same prime factor homeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{1}\times I
, then
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M
are conjugate in
\pi_{1}M
.

(ii) Otherwise,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
and
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M}
are not conjugate in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
.

(2)

Let
N_{\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}\left(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\right)
denote the normalizer of
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
.

(i) If the prime factor of
M
containing
\partial_{i}M
is homeomorphic to the orientable
I
-bundle over Klein bottle, then

N_{\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}\left(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\right)
contains
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
as an index-2 subgroup, and any element
g\in N_{\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}\left(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\right)\setminus\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
acts by conjugation on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
as the profinite completion of an involution in
\mathrm{Aut}(\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M)\cong\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii) Otherwise,
N_{\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}\left(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\right)=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
