, and acts trivially by conjugation on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
.

Proof.

We first show that this proposition holds when
M
is irreducible.
When
M
has trivial JSJ-decomposition, the conclusion follows from

Lemma 6.2
,
Lemma 6.4
and
Lemma 6.5
. Now we assume that
M
has non-trivial JSJ-decomposition.

Let
\partial_{i}M
and
\partial_{j}M
be two possibly identical boundary components, which belong to the JSJ-pieces
M_{x}
and
M_{y}
respectively. For each
v\in V(\Gamma_{M})
, fix a conjugacy representative
\pi_{1}M_{v}\leq\pi_{1}M
. Up to conjugation, we may assume
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{x}\subseteq\pi_{1}M
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{y}\subseteq\pi_{1}M
. Suppose
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}=g\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M}g^{-1}
for some
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
. It suffices to show that
M_{x}=M_{y}
,
\partial_{i}M=\partial_{j}M
, and
g\in\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
, so that we deduce the conclusion from the proven case for the JSJ-piece.

We consider the action by
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
on the standard profinite tree
\hat{T}_{M}
, and let
q:\hat{T}_{M}\to\hat{T}_{M}/\widehat{\pi_{1}M}=\Gamma_{M}
denote the quotient map. Then
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}
fixes a unique vertex
v\in q^{-1}(x)
, and
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{y}}
fixes a unique vertex
w\in q^{-1}(y)
, since the edge stabilizers are abelian. Thus,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\subseteq\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}
fixes
v
, and
g\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M}g^{-1}\subseteq g\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{y}}g^{-1}
fixes
g\cdot w
. As a result,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
fixes both
v
and
g\cdot w
in
V(\hat{T}_{M})
.

According to
Lemma 6.6
,
v=g\cdot w
.
In particular,
v
and
w
belong to the same orbit; thus,
M_{x}=M_{y}
, which in turn implies that
v=w
and
g\in\mathrm{Stab}(v)=\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}
. Therefore,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}=g\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M}g^{-1}
within
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}
, so
\partial_{i}M=\partial_{j}M
and
g\in\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
according to
Lemma 6.2
and
Lemma 6.4
.

Now we move on to the case when
M
is reducible, with prime decomposition
M=M_{1}\#\cdots\#M_{m}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{r}
. Then, fixing the conjugacy representatives, we have

\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{1}}\amalg\cdots\amalg\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{m}}\amalg\widehat{F_{r}}.

Given two peripheral subgroups
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M
, up to conjugation, we may as well assume
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{k}\subseteq\pi_{1}M
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{l}\subset\pi_{1}M
. In this case if
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}=g\cdot\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M}\cdot g^{-1}
for some
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
, then
k=l
and
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{k}}
. Now the conclusion follows from the irreducible case proved for
M_{k}
.
∎

6.2.
Well-definedness of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity

Recall in
Definition 1.11
, we have defined the notion of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity. Let
M
and
N
be compact, orientable 3-manifolds with incompressible toral boundary. Let
\partial_{i}M
be a boundary component of
M
and let
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
be a conjugacy representative of the corresponding peripheral subgroup. Let
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
be a profinite isomorphism. For
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
,
f
is
peripheral
\lambda
-regular
at
\partial_{i}M
if the following two conditions hold.

(1)

There exists a boundary component
\partial_{j}N
of
N
, a conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
, and an element
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
such that
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=C_{g}^{-1}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N})
.

(2)

There exists an isomorphism
\psi:\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
such that

C_{g}\circ f=\lambda\otimes\psi:\,\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N\cong\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}.

And
f
is called
peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular
at
\partial_{i}M
if it is peripheral
\lambda
-regular for some
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

Proposition 6.7
guarantees the “well-definedness” of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity.
In fact,
Proposition 6.7
(
1
) implies that the boundary component
\partial_{j}N
in condition (
1
) is unique, except for the case of parallel boundary components in
S^{1}\times S^{1}\times I
. In addition,
Proposition 6.7
(
2
) further implies that the existence of decomposition as
\lambda\otimes\psi
in condition (
2
) does not depend on the choice of the conjugacy representative
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
, nor does it depend on the choice of the element
g
which induces the conjugation. In particular, the coefficient
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
is unique up to a
\pm
-sign.

6.3.
Geometric meaning of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity

The geometric explanation of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity is that the given profinite isomorphism carries profinite isomorphisms between the Dehn fillings at the corresponding boundary component.

To be explicit, suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M
, and
C_{g}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}=\lambda\otimes\psi
. For each boundary slope
c\in\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
,
\psi\in\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M,\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N)
sends
c
to a boundary slope
\psi(c)\in\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
. Let
M_{c}
be the Dehn filling of
M
at
\partial_{i}M
along the slope
c
, and let
N_{\psi(c)}
be the Dehn filling of
N
at
\partial_{j}N
along the slope
\psi(c)
. Then
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{c}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\psi(c)}}
.

Indeed,
\pi_{1}M_{c}\cong\pi_{1}M/\langle\!\langle c\rangle\!\rangle
, and
Proposition 2.1
implies
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{c}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M}/\overline{\langle\!\langle c\rangle\!\rangle}
. Then the profinite isomorphism
f
descends to an isomorphism between the quotients

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{c}}\cong\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$\widehat{\pi_{1}M}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$\overline{\left\langle\!\langle c\right\rangle\!\rangle}$}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$\widehat{\pi_{1}N}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$\overline{\left\langle\!\langle\psi(c)\right\rangle\!\rangle}$}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\psi(c)}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

This idea is further expanded in
[
47
]
.

6.4.
Passing peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity to JSJ-pieces

In this subsection, we consider the following setting.
Suppose
M
and
N
are compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary which are not closed
Sol
-manifolds. Suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is a profinite isomorphism, and let
f_{\bullet}
be the congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\mathcal{G}_{M},\Gamma_{M})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{N},\Gamma_{N})
induced by
f
according to
Theorem 5.5
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\partial_{i}M
be a boundary component of
M
which belongs to a JSJ-piece
M_{v}
. Suppose
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})
is conjugate to
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}
in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
for some boundary component
\partial_{j}N
. Then
\partial_{j}N\subseteq N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
, and
f_{v}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})
is conjugate to
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}
within
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
.

Proof.

For each
u\in V(\Gamma_{M})
, fix a conjugacy representative
\pi_{1}M_{u}\leq\pi_{1}M
.
Suppose
\partial_{j}N
belongs to the JSJ-piece
N_{w}
. Up to conjugation, we may assume
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{v}\subseteq\pi_{1}M
, and
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N\subseteq\pi_{1}N_{w}\subseteq\pi_{1}N
. Based on this assumption, suppose
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=C_{h}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N})
for some
h\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
.

By construction of
Theorem 5.5
, there exists an element
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
such that the following diagram commutes.

(6.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g}^{-1}}

In particular,
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})\subseteq C_{g}(\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}})
, so

(6.10)

\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}\subseteq C_{h^{-1}g}(\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}).

Consider the action by
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
on the standard profinite tree
\hat{T}_{N}
. Denote the quotient map as
q:\hat{T}_{N}\to\hat{T}_{N}/\widehat{\pi_{1}N}=\Gamma_{N}
. Since the edge stabilizers are abelian,
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
fixes a unique vertex
x\in q^{-1}({f^{\dagger}}(v))
, and
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{w}}
fixes a unique vertex
y\in q^{-1}(w)
. Then by (
6.10
),
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}\subseteq\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{w}}
fixes both
y
and
h^{-1}g\cdot x
. According to
Lemma 6.6
,
y=h^{-1}g\cdot x
. In particular,
x
and
y
belong to the same
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
-orbit, so
w={f^{\dagger}}(v)
and in turn,
x=y
.

Therefore,
\partial_{j}N\subseteq N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
, and
h^{-1}g\in\mathrm{Stab}(x)=\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
. Then, according to diagram (
6.9
),
f_{v}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=C_{g^{-1}h}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N})
, where
g^{-1}h\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
.
∎

Corollary 6.11
.

f
respects the peripheral structure if and only if for each
v\in V(\Gamma_{M})
,
f_{v}
respects the peripheral structure.

Proof.

Let us first prove the “only if” direction. In fact, according to the coherence relation (
3.10
) in the congruent isomorphism, each
f_{v}
automatically preserves the peripheral subgroups given by the JSJ-tori, up to conjugacy. On the other hand, according to
Lemma 6.8
, the components of
\partial M\cap M_{v}
and
\partial N\cap N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
are in one-to-one correspondence, and their corresponding conjugacy classes of peripheral subgroups are also preserved by
f_{v}
.

The “if” direction is easier. For
\partial_{i}M\subseteq M_{v}\cap\partial M
, by assumption,
f_{v}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})
is conjugate to a peripheral subgroup
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
. Then,
\partial_{j}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}\subseteq N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}\cap\partial N
, since the peripheral structures of
M_{v}
and
N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
given by the JSJ-tori are preserved by
f_{v}
due to the same reason as above. This gives a one-to-one correspondence between the components of
M_{v}\cap\partial M
and
N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}\cap\partial N
, which can be combined into a one-to-one correspondence between the components of
\partial M
and
\partial N
. In addition,
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})
is conjugate to
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
according to the commutative diagram (
6.9
), so
f
also respects the peripheral structure.
∎

When considering 3-manifolds admitting JSJ-decomposition, it is a crucial step to switch from the peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity of a profinite isomorphism between two irreducible 3-manifolds to the peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity of the induced profinite isomorphism between the JSJ-pieces.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\partial_{i}M
be a boundary component of
M
which belongs to a JSJ-piece
M_{v}
. Then
f
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M
if and only if
f_{v}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M
.

Proof.

The “if” direction is clear from the commutative diagram (
6.9
). Now we expand on the “only if” direction.

Indeed,
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})
is conjugate to a peripheral subgroup
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}
in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. By
Lemma 6.8
,
\partial_{j}N\subseteq N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
. Up to taking suitable conjugacy representatives, we assume
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{v}\subseteq\pi_{1}M
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N\subseteq\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}\subseteq\pi_{1}N
, and there exists
k\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
such that
f_{v}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=C_{k}^{-1}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N})
. Then, by the commutative diagram (
6.9
), there exists
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
such that
f_{v}=C_{g}^{-1}\circ f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}\to\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
and
f(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M})=C_{gk^{-1}}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N})
.

According to the well-definedness of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity (
Proposition 6.7
), the map

C_{kg^{-1}}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}:\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N\cong\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}

can be decomposed as
\lambda\otimes\psi
, where
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and
\psi:\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
, regardless of the choice of the conjugator
kg^{-1}
and the conjugacy representatives of peripheral subgroups.
This implies that

C_{k}\circ f_{v}|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}=\lambda\otimes\psi:\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\subseteq\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{x}}\longrightarrow\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}\subseteq\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(x)}},

where
k\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
, which is to say that
f_{v}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M
.
∎

6.5.
The gluing lemma

The most essential application of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity is displayed in the commutative diagram (
1.10
).
In fact,
under the condition of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity, we can directly compare the gluing maps between corresponding JSJ-pieces in a profinitely isomorphic pair of irreducible 3-manifolds.
Namely, at each edge of the JSJ-graph, one derives a gluing map
\psi_{e}=\varphi_{1}\circ\varphi_{0}^{-1}:\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}\to\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}
as is witnessed in the JSJ-graph of groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}M_{u}\supseteq\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}T^{2}_{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}\subseteq\pi_{1}M_{v}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Lemma 6.13

(Gluing lemma)
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary which are not closed
Sol
-manifolds. Let
f_{\bullet}
be a congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma_{N})
.
Suppose
u,v\in V(\Gamma_{M})
are two possibly identical vertices connected by an edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{M})
. Following diagram (
1.10
), denote the gluings at the JSJ-tori corresponding to
e
and
{f^{\dagger}}(e)
as

M_{u}\supseteq\partial_{k}M_{u}\longleftrightarrow\partial_{i}M_{v}\subseteq M_{v}\text{ and }N_{{f^{\dagger}}(u)}\supseteq\partial_{l}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(u)}\longleftrightarrow\partial_{j}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}\subseteq N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}.

(1)

The profinite isomorphism
f_{u}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{u}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(u)}}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{k}M_{u}
if and only if
f_{v}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M_{v}
. In addition, when peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity holds, the coefficients in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
are identical up to a
\pm
-sign.

(2)

Assume peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity in (
1
) holds. Suppose
f_{u}
restricting on
\partial_{k}M_{u}
is induced by a homeomorphism
\Phi_{u}:M_{u}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(u)}
with coefficient
\lambda
, and
f_{v}
restricting on
\partial_{k}M_{v}
is induced by a homeomorphism
\Phi_{v}:M_{v}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
with the same coefficient
\lambda
(see
Definition 1.12
).
Then,

\Phi_{u}
and
\Phi_{v}
are compatible, up to isotopy, with the gluing maps at the JSJ-tori.
In particular,

\bullet

if
u\neq v
, then
\Phi=(\Phi_{u},\Phi_{v})
yields a homeomorphism
M_{u}\cup_{T^{2}}M_{v}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(u)}\cup_{T^{2}}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
;

\bullet

if
u=v
and
\Phi_{u}=\Phi_{v}
, then
\Phi_{u}
induces a homeomorphism
M_{u}\cup_{T^{2}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(u)}\cup_{T^{2}}
.

Proof.

(
1
) For brevity, we identify
\Gamma_{M}
and
\Gamma_{N}
with the same graph
\Gamma
through the isomorphism
{f^{\dagger}}
. Then, the congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
implies the following commutative diagram

(6.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{v}}}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{0}}\circ f_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{e}}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{1}}\circ f_{v}}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
\subseteq

where
h_{0}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
and
h_{1}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{v}}
.

By
Lemma 6.1
, we further obtain

(6.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\psi_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{0}}\circ f_{u}|_{{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{h_{1}}\circ f_{v}|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\psi_{e}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
\psi_{e}
and
\psi_{e}^{\prime}
are respectively gluing maps of
M
and
N
at the JSJ-torus corresponding to
e
.

According to
Proposition 6.7
, regardless of the choice of
h_{0}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
, if
f_{u}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{k}M_{u}
, then
C_{h_{0}}\circ f_{u}|_{{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}}
admits a decomposition as
\lambda\otimes\varphi_{u}
. In this case, the commutative diagram (
6.15
) implies that
C_{h_{1}}\circ f_{v}|_{{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}}}
admits a decomposition as
\lambda\otimes(\psi_{e}^{\prime}\circ\varphi_{u}\circ\psi_{e}^{-1})
, which is to say that
f_{v}
is peripheral
\lambda
-regular at
\partial_{i}M_{v}
. The converse holds similarly by symmetry, which implies (
1
).

(
2
) Let us first consider
f_{u}
. The commutative diagram (
6.14
) implies
f_{u}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}})=C_{h_{0}}^{-1}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}})
. Consequently, the closure of a peripheral subgroup corresponding to
\Phi_{u}(\partial_{k}M_{u})
is a conjugate of
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
.
Since
N
is not a closed
Sol
-manifold,
N_{u}
is not homeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{1}\times I
, and by
Proposition 6.7
(
1
),
\Phi_{u}(\partial_{k}M_{u})=\partial_{l}N_{u}
. In particular, there exists
s_{0}\in\pi_{1}N
such that
\Phi_{u\ast}(\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u})=C_{s_{0}}(\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u})
.
By
Definition 1.12
, there exists an element
g_{0}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
such that

C_{g_{0}}\circ f_{u}|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}:\;\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}\xrightarrow{\lambda\otimes\Phi_{u\ast}}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{s_{0}}(\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u})\cong\overline{C_{s_{0}}(\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u})}.

Since
\pi_{1}N_{u}
is residually finite, we identify
\pi_{1}N_{u}
as a subgroup in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
through the canonical homomorphism. Then
s_{0}
is also viewed as an element in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
. Consequently, we derive that
f_{u}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}})=C_{g_{0}}^{-1}(C_{s_{0}}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}))
. Recall that
f_{u}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}})=C_{h_{0}}^{-1}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}})
. Thus,

s_{0}^{-1}g_{0}h_{0}^{-1}\in N_{\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}}(\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}).

If
N_{u}
is not the orientable
I
-bundle over Klein bottle,
Proposition 6.7
(
2
) implies that
s_{0}^{-1}g_{0}h_{0}^{-1}\in\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
, so it acts trivially by conjugation on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
. In particular,

C_{h_{0}}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}=C_{s_{0}^{-1}g_{0}h_{0}^{-1}}\circ C_{h_{0}}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}=\lambda\otimes(C_{s_{0}^{-1}}\circ\Phi_{u\ast}):\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}\to\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}.

If
N_{u}
is the orientable
I
-bundle over Klein bottle, since
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
contains
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
as an index-2 open subgroup and
\pi_{1}N_{u}
is dense in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}
, we can find an element
t_{0}\in\pi_{1}N_{u}
, such that
t_{0}s_{0}^{-1}g_{0}h_{0}^{-1}\in\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
. In this case, we have

C_{h_{0}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\circ$}}f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}=C_{t_{0}s_{0}^{-1}g_{0}h_{0}^{-1}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\circ$}}C_{h_{0}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\circ$}}f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}=\lambda\otimes(C_{t_{0}s_{0}^{-1}}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{.8}{$\circ$}}\Phi_{u\ast}):\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}\to\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}.

Let
r_{0}=s_{0}^{-1}
in the former case, and
r_{0}=t_{0}s_{0}^{-1}
in the latter case. In both cases, we have

r_{0}\in\pi_{1}N_{u}
, and

C_{h_{0}}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}}=\lambda\otimes(C_{r_{0}}\circ\Phi_{u\ast}):\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}\to\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}.

Similarly, for
f_{v}
, there exists
r_{1}\in\pi_{1}N_{v}
such that

C_{h_{1}}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}}=\lambda\otimes(C_{r_{1}}\circ\Phi_{v\ast}):\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{v}\cong\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{v}}.

Now we can cancel the coefficient
\lambda
in the commutative diagram (
6.15
), so that we obtain the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\psi_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes(C_{r_{0}}\circ\Phi_{u\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes(C_{r_{1}}\circ\Phi_{v\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\psi_{e}^{\prime}}

Restricting to the
\mathbb{Z}
-submodules
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
etc., we recover a commutative diagram between the original peripheral subgroups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}M_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}M_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}N_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}N_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{r_{0}}\circ\Phi_{u_{\ast}}}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{e}}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{r_{1}}\circ\Phi_{v_{\ast}}}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{e}^{\prime}}
\subseteq

where
r_{0}\in\pi_{1}N_{u}
and
r_{1}\in\pi_{1}N_{v}
.

Since
\mathrm{MCG}(T^{2})\cong\mathrm{Aut}(\pi_{1}T^{2})
, this implies that
\Phi_{u}
and
\Phi_{v}
are compatible, up to isotopy, with the gluing maps at the JSJ-tori. Therefore, when
u\neq v
,
\Phi=(\Phi_{u},\Phi_{v})
yields a homeomorphism between
M_{u}\cup_{T^{2}}M_{v}
and
N_{u}\cup_{T^{2}}N_{v}
; and when
u=v
, if
\Phi_{u}
is isotopic to
\Phi_{v}
, then
\Phi_{u}
directly induces a homeomorphism between
M_{u}\cup_{T^{2}}
and
N_{u}\cup_{T^{2}}
.
∎

Remark 6.16
.

In practice, one might encounter the case where
f_{u}
restricting on
\partial_{k}M_{u}
is induced by a homeomorphism
\Phi_{u}
with coefficient
\lambda
, while
f_{v}
restricting on
\partial_{k}M_{v}
is induced by a homeomorphism
\Phi_{v}
with coefficient
-\lambda
. In this case, the conclusions of
Lemma 6.13
(
2
) do not hold.

The gluing lemma can be easily generalized, with the vertices
u
and
v
replaced by subgraphs
\Lambda
and
\Upsilon
.

Corollary 6.17
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary which are not closed
Sol
-manifolds.
Let
f_{\bullet}
be a congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma_{N})
.
Suppose
\Lambda
and
\Upsilon
are either disjoint or identical connected subgraphs of
\Gamma_{M}
connected by an edge
e
. Let
M_{\Lambda}
,
N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Lambda)}
,
M_{\Upsilon}
,
N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon)}
be the submanifolds corresponding to these subgraphs. According to
Proposition 3.11
and
Proposition 5.2
, the congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
restricting on the subgraphs induces two isomorphisms

\displaystyle f_{\Lambda}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\Lambda}}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},{f^{\dagger}}(\Lambda))\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Lambda)}},

\displaystyle f_{\Upsilon}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\Upsilon}}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Upsilon)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon))\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon)}}.

Denote the boundary gluings at the JSJ-tori corresponding to
e
and
{f^{\dagger}}(e)
as

M_{\Lambda}\supseteq\partial_{k}M_{\Lambda}\longleftrightarrow\partial_{i}M_{\Upsilon}\subseteq M_{\Upsilon}\text{ and }N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Lambda)}\supseteq\partial_{l}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Lambda)}\longleftrightarrow\partial_{j}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon)}\subseteq N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon)}.

(1)

f_{\Lambda}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{k}M_{\Lambda}
if and only if
f_{\Upsilon}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M_{\Upsilon}
. In addition, when peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity is satisfied, the coefficients in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
are identical up to a
\pm
-sign.

(2)

Assume peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity in (
1
) holds. Suppose
f_{\Lambda}
restricting on
\partial_{k}M_{\Lambda}
is induced by a homeomorphism
\Phi_{\Lambda}:M_{\Lambda}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Lambda)}
with coefficient
\lambda
, and
f_{\Upsilon}
restricting on
\partial_{i}M_{\Upsilon}
is induced by a homeomorphism
\Phi_{\Upsilon}:M_{\Upsilon}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon)}
with the same coefficient
\lambda
.
Then,

\Phi_{\Lambda}
and
\Phi_{\Upsilon}
are compatible, up to isotopy, with the gluing maps at the JSJ-tori.
In particular,

\bullet

if
\Lambda
is disjoint with
\Upsilon
, then
\Phi=(\Phi_{\Lambda},\Phi_{\Upsilon})
yields a homeomorphism
M_{\Lambda}\cup_{T^{2}}M_{\Upsilon}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Lambda)}\cup_{T^{2}}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon)}
;

\bullet
if
\Lambda=\Upsilon
and
\Phi_{\Lambda}=\Phi_{\Upsilon}
, then
\Phi_{\Upsilon}
directly induces a homeomorphism
M_{\Upsilon}\cup_{T^{2}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(\Upsilon)}\cup_{T^{2}}
.

Proof.

In fact, one can obtain a similar commutative diagram as (
6.14
), and the remaining proof is exactly the same as
Lemma 6.13
.
∎

7.
Finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds

By a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold, we mean a compact orientable
3-manifold with empty or incompressible toral boundary, whose interior can be equipped with a complete Riemannian
metric of constant sectional curvature
-1
which has finite volume.

Recently, Liu
[
18
]
proved the following significant result.

Theorem 7.1

(
[
18
]
)
.

The class of finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds is profinitely almost rigid.

However, the profinite automorphism group of a finite-volume hyperbolic manifold has not been fully understood. In this section, we characterize some features of profinite isomorphisms between hyperbolic 3-manifolds when restricted on the peripheral subgroups.
This section is mainly devoted to the proof of the following theorem.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
M
and
N
be cusped finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds, so that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
.

(1)

For any isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
, there exists
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
such that
f
is peripheral
\lambda
-regular at all boundary components of
M
.

(2)

For any chosen peripheral subgroups
P=\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
and
Q=\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
, denote

B(M,N;P,Q)=\left\{\psi:P\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}Q\left|\;\begin{aligned} &\text{there exists }f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}\text{ and }\eta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}\text{,}\\
&\text{such that }f(\overline{P})=\overline{Q}\text{ and }f|_{\overline{P}}=\eta\otimes{\psi}\end{aligned}\right.\right\}.

Then
\#B(M,N;P,Q)\leq 12
.

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
M
be a cusped finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold, and let
\mathcal{P}
be a collection of some boundary components of
M
. Then
M
is profinitely almost auto-symmetric at
\mathcal{P}
(see
Definition 1.13
).

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}=\{\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M\}
, and choose conjugacy representatives of peripheral subgroups
P_{1}=\pi_{1}\partial_{1}M,\cdots,P_{k}=\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M
. Then
B(M,M;P_{i},P_{i})
can be viewed as a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(P_{i})\cong\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

According to
Proposition 6.7
, there is a well-defined group homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}:\;\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}{B(M,M;P_{i},P_{i})}/{\{\pm id\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([\psi_{1}],\cdots,[\psi_{k}])}

such that for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
C_{g_{i}}\circ f|_{\overline{P_{i}}}=\lambda_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}
for some
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
and
\lambda_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. Indeed
Theorem 7.2
(
1
) implies that the coefficients
\lambda_{i}
are unified up to an ambiguity of
\pm
-sign.

Then, any
f\in\ker(\mathcal{F})
restricting on each
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the identity homeomorphism
Id_{M}
with a coefficient in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. In other words,
\ker(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
.
Moreover, according to
Theorem 7.2
(
2
),
\#B(M,M;P_{i},P_{i})\leq 12
. Thus,
\ker(\mathcal{F})
has index at most
6^{k}
in
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
, which is to say
\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
has finite index in
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
.
∎

7.1.
Homological
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity and Thurston norm

For a topological space
X
, we denote
H_{1}({X},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
as
H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Z})
modulo its torsion subgroup. Then, there is a natural isomorphism
H^{1}(X;\mathbb{R})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{1}({X},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}},\mathbb{R})
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are 3-manifolds,
and
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism. Note that
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})={\pi_{1}(M)}^{\text{ab}}
and
H_{1}(N;\mathbb{Z})={\pi_{1}(N)}^{\text{ab}}
. According to
Proposition 2.11
,
f
induces an isomorphism
f_{\ast}:\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
. In particular,
b_{1}(M)=b_{1}(N)
.

When
M
and
N
are finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds, Liu
[
18
]
proved two essential features of this map
f_{\ast}
, namely homological
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity and the preservation of Thurston norm.

Proposition 7.4

(
[
18
, Theorem 6.1
\&
Corollary 6.3]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds with positive first Betti number. Suppose there is an isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
, and denote
f_{\ast}:\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
as the induced isomorphism.

(1)

There exists an element
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and an isomorphism
\Phi:H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
such that
f_{\ast}=\lambda\otimes\Phi
.

(2)

The dual map

\Phi^{\ast}:H^{1}(N;\mathbb{R})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}},\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{\,\cong\,}\mathrm{Hom}(H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}},\mathbb{R})\cong H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})

induced by
\Phi

preserves the Thurston norm
\parallel\cdot\parallel_{\mathrm{Th}}
(see
[
35
]
for definition); in other words,
{{\parallel\alpha\parallel}_{\mathrm{Th}}}={{\parallel\Psi^{\ast}(\alpha)\parallel}_{\mathrm{Th}}}
for all
\alpha\in H^{1}(N;\mathbb{R})
.

7.2.
Finite cover and peripheral subgroups

In this subsection, we prove the following proposition as a preparation for the proof of
Theorem 7.2
. The proof is largely based on the virtual specialization theorem for finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds
[
46
]
.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
M
be a cusped finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold. Then, there exists a finite regular cover
M^{\star}
of
M
such that every boundary component of
{M}^{\star}
is
H_{1}
-injective, i.e.
H_{1}(\partial_{j}M^{\star};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(M^{\star};\mathbb{Z})
is injective for each boundary component
\partial_{j}M^{\star}
.
As a consequence, the map
\pi_{1}(\partial_{j}M^{\star})\to\pi_{1}(M^{\star})\to H_{1}({M^{\star}},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
is injective.

This allows us to virtually embed the peripheral subgroup into the homology group of the entire manifold, providing a method to deduce peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity (
Theorem 7.2
) from the homological
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity (
Proposition 7.4
).

Before the proof of
Proposition 7.5
, let us introduce a notion.

A subgroup
H\leq G
is called
a retract of
G
if there exists a group homomorphism
\varphi:G\to H
such that
\varphi|_{H}=id
, and
H\leq G
is called
a virtual retract of
G
if there exists a finite-index subgroup
K\leq G
, such that
K\supseteq H
and
H
is a retract of
K
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Suppose
H
is a retract of
G
. Then, the homomorphism
H^{ab}=H_{1}(H;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})=G^{ab}
is injective.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:G\to H
be the homomorphism so that
\varphi|_{H}=id
. Then the conclusion follows directly from the diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{ab}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\ast}}

∎

The next lemma is a combination of
[
19
, Proposition 1.5 and Corollary 1.6]
.

Lemma 7.7

(
[
19
]
)
.

If a group
G
is virtually compact special, then any finitely generated, virtually abelian subgroup of
G
is a virtual retract of
G
.

We are now ready to prove
Proposition 7.5
.

Proof of
Proposition 7.5
.

Suppose
M
has
n
boundary components
\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{n}M
.
For each boundary component
\partial_{i}M
, fix a conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup
P_{i}=\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M)\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
.
[
46
, Theorem 14.29]
implies that
\pi_{1}M
is virtually compact special. Thus,
Lemma 7.7
implies that
P_{i}
is a virtual retract of
\pi_{1}M
, i.e. there exists a finite index subgroup
K_{i}\leq\pi_{1}M
containing
P_{i}
so that
P_{i}
is a retract of
K_{i}
.

Let
N_{i}\to M
denote the finite cover corresponding to the subgroup
K_{i}
. Note that
P_{i}\subseteq K_{i}
, so
\partial_{i}M
lifts to
N_{i}
. In other words, there is a component
\widetilde{\partial_{i}M}
in the pre-image of
\partial_{i}M
in
N_{i}
, which maps homeomorphically to
\partial_{i}M
such that
P_{i}\subseteq K_{i}=\pi_{1}(N_{i})
is exactly the corresponding peripheral subgroup of
\widetilde{\partial_{i}M}
. Then, according to
Lemma 7.6
, the homomorphism
P_{i}=P^{ab}_{i}=H_{1}(\widetilde{\partial_{i}M};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(N_{i};\mathbb{Z})=K_{i}^{ab}
induced by inclusion is injective.

Let
H
be a finite-index normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}M
contained in
\cap_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}
, and let
M^{\star}
be the finite regular cover of
M
corresponding to the subgroup
H
. We show that
M^{\star}
satisfies our requirement.

In fact, the covering map
p:M^{\star}\to M
factors through each
N_{i}
, which we denote as
M^{\star}\xrightarrow{p_{i}}N_{i}\to M
. We claim that any component
S\subseteq p_{i}^{-1}(\widetilde{\partial_{i}M})
is
H_{1}
-injective in
M^{\star}
. In fact, the covering map
T^{2}\cong S\to\widetilde{\partial_{i}M}\cong T^{2}
is
\pi_{1}
-injective, and is hence
H_{1}
-injective since the fundamental group of a torus is abelian. Thus, we have the following commutative diagram, implying that
H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(M^{\star};\mathbb{Z})
is injective.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(M^{\star};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\widetilde{\partial_{i}M};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(N_{i};\mathbb{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{i}}_{\ast}}

Note that any component in
p_{i}^{-1}(\widetilde{\partial_{i}M})
is indeed a component of
p^{-1}(\partial_{i}M)
. In other words, for each boundary component
\partial_{i}M\subseteq\partial M
, there exists an
H_{1}
-injective component in
p^{-1}(\partial_{i}M)
. Recall that
M^{\star}
is a regular covering of
M
, so the
H_{1}
-injective component in
p^{-1}(\partial_{i}M)
can be carried to any component of
p^{-1}(\partial_{i}M)
by a deck transformation, which is an automorphism on
M^{\star}
. Thus, for each
i
, all components of
p^{-1}(\partial_{i}M)
are
H_{1}
-injective in
M^{\star}
. Therefore,
M^{\star}
satisfies our requirement.

The injectivity of
\pi_{1}(\partial_{j}M^{\star})\to H_{1}({M^{\star}},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
follows from the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P=\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M^{\star}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}M^{\star}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\partial_{j}M^{\star};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(M^{\star};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}({M^{\star}},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

since
\pi_{1}(\partial_{j}\widetilde{M})\cong H_{1}(\partial_{j}\widetilde{M};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
is torsion-free.
∎

7.3.
Peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity in the hyperbolic case

We first point out that in order to prove (
2
) of
Theorem 7.2
, it suffices to prove a weaker conclusion.

Lemma 7.8
.

\#B(M,N;P,Q)<+\infty
implies
\#B(M,N;P,Q)\leq 12
.

Proof.

This follows from two direct observations.

First, if
B(M,N;P,Q)
is non-empty, by choosing an element
\phi\in B(M,N;P,Q)
, we establish a one-to-one correspondence between
B(M,N;P,Q)
and
B(M,M;P,P)
, through sending
\psi\in B(M,N;P,Q)
to
\phi^{-1}\circ\psi\in B(M,M;P,P)
.

Second,
B(M,M;P,P)
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(P)\cong\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

The conclusion then follows from the fact that any finite subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
contains at most 12 elements, see
[
36
]
.
∎

The proof of
Theorem 7.2
consists of two steps. We first prove a special case of the theorem as shown by the following lemma, and then relate the general case with this special case through the method of finite coverings.

Lemma 7.9
.

Theorem 7.2
holds if for each peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
, the map
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\to\pi_{1}M\to H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
is injective.

Proof.

(
1
)
Let
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
be an isomorphism.
For any peripheral subgroup
P=\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
,
Lemma 6.3
implies that there is a peripheral subgroup
Q=\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
such that
f(\overline{P})=C_{g}^{-1}(\overline{Q})
for some
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. Denote
f^{\prime}=C_{g}\circ f
, so that
f^{\prime}(\overline{P})=\overline{Q}
.

Let
f_{\ast}:\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
be the induced isomorphism. Since
f_{\ast}
is defined on the abelianization level, it is not affected by the conjugation of
g
. Thus,
f_{\ast}=f^{\prime}_{\ast}
. In this case,
b_{1}(N)=b_{1}(M)\geq 2
, so according to
Proposition 7.4
(
1
), there exists
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and

\Phi:H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}

such that
f^{\prime}_{\ast}=f_{\ast}=\lambda\otimes\Phi
.

Consider the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}|_{\overline{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{\ast}=f_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{\ast}=f_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\otimes\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}|_{\overline{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Note that
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion-free, so
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is a flat
\mathbb{Z}
-module. Thus, the injectivity of
P\to H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
implies the injectivity of
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}P\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
.
It follows from the commutative diagram that the map
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{Q}\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
is also injective. Consequently,
Q\to H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
is also injective. Thus, we can canonically identify
P
as a subgroup of
H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
, and identify
Q
as a subgroup of
H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
. Since
f^{\prime}_{\ast}=f_{\ast}=\lambda\otimes\Phi
, the commutative diagram implies that
\Phi(P)=Q
. Therefore,
f^{\prime}|_{\overline{P}}=\lambda\otimes\Phi|_{P}
, where
\Phi|_{P}:P\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}Q\subseteq H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
is an isomorphism. Thus,
f
is peripheral
\lambda
-regular at
\partial_{i}M
, and the coefficient
\lambda
is unified for all boundary components
\partial_{i}M
. This proves (
1
) of
Theorem 7.2
.

(
2
) Let
\Phi:H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
be as in part (1). Note that the dual isomorphism
\Phi^{\ast}:H^{1}(N;\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
preserves the integral classes. In addition,
Proposition 7.4
(
2
) implies that
\Phi^{\ast}
also preserves the Thurston norm.
Denote

A^{\ast}(M,N):=\left\{\Psi^{\ast}:H^{1}(N;\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})\,\left|\;\begin{gathered}\Psi^{\ast}\text{ preserves the integral lattice}\\
\text{and the Thurston norm}\end{gathered}\right.\right\}.

Then
\Phi^{\ast}\in A^{\ast}(M,N)
.

We denote the dual of
A^{\ast}(M,N)
as

A(M,N)=\left\{\Psi:H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\mid\Psi^{\ast}\in A^{\ast}(M,N)\right\}.

Then,
\Phi\in A(M,N)
.

There is an apparent bijection between
A(M,N)
and
A^{\ast}(M,N)
. In addition,
\Phi
establishes a bijection between
A^{\ast}(M,M)
and
A^{\ast}(M,N)
, by sending
\Psi^{\ast}\in A^{\ast}(M,M)
to
\Psi^{\ast}\circ\Phi^{\ast}\in A^{\ast}(M,N)
. Thus,
\#A(M,N)=\#A^{\ast}(M,N)=\#A^{\ast}(M,M)
.

Note that
both
B(M,N;P,Q)
and
A(M,N)
are invariant under scalar multiplication by
\pm 1
.
Therefore, part (1) shows that any element
\psi\in B(M,N;P,Q)
is a restriction of some element
\Phi\in A(M,N)
on
P
, i.e.
\psi=\Phi|_{P}
. Thus,

\#B(M,N;P,Q)\leq\#A(M,N)=\#A^{\ast}(M,M).

Since
M
is a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold, the Thurston norm is indeed a norm on the real vector space
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
, according to
[
17
, Theorem 2.11]
. Furthermore, according to
[
35
, Theorem 2]
, the unit ball
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{Th}}(M)
of the Thurston norm for
M
is a convex polyhedron. Indeed,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{Th}}(M)
is the convex hull of finitely many points in
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
symmetric about the origin. Note that there are only finitely many linear automorphisms of a finite-dimensional real vector space preserving a finite, compact, symmetric, codimension-0 polyhedron. Since any element in
A^{\ast}(M,M)
preserves
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{Th}}(M)
, it follows that
\#A^{\ast}(M,M)<\infty
.

Thus,
\#B(M,N;P,Q)<\infty
. Combining with
Lemma 7.8
, this proves (
2
) of
Theorem 7.2
.
∎

Before completing the proof of
Theorem 7.2
, we need a last group theoretical preparation.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
P
and
Q
be finitely generated free abelian groups. Suppose
P_{0}
is a finite-index subgroup of
P
, and identify
\overline{P_{0}}
with
\widehat{P_{0}}
.

(1)

For every homomorphism
\varphi_{0}:P_{0}\to Q
, there exists at most one homomorphism
\varphi:P\to Q
such that
\varphi|_{P_{0}}=\varphi_{0}
.

(2)

For every continuous homomorphism
\Phi_{0}:\widehat{P_{0}}\to\widehat{Q}
, there exists at most one continuous homomorphism
\Phi:\widehat{P}\to\widehat{Q}
such that
\Phi|_{\overline{P_{0}}}=\Phi_{0}
.

(3)

Based on (
2
), suppose there exists
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and
\varphi_{0}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(P_{0},Q)
such that
\Phi_{0}=\lambda\otimes{\varphi_{0}}
. If the continuous homomorphism
\Phi
exists, then
\Phi
can be decomposed as
\lambda\otimes{\varphi}
, where
\varphi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(P,Q)
and
\varphi|_{P_{0}}=\varphi_{0}
.

Proof.

(
1
) This follows from a general conclusion for torsion-free abelian groups. In fact, since
P_{0}
is a finite-index subgroup, there exists a positive integer
n
such that
n\cdot P\subseteq P_{0}
. Suppose
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are homomorphisms so that
\varphi|_{P_{0}}=\varphi_{0}=\varphi^{\prime}|_{P_{0}}
. Then, for any element
x\in P
,
n\cdot x\in P_{0}
and
n\cdot\varphi(x)=\varphi(n\cdot x)=\varphi^{\prime}(n\cdot x)=n\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(x)
. Thus,
n\cdot(\varphi(x)-\varphi^{\prime}(x))=0
. Since
Q
is torsion-free, we derive that
\varphi(x)=\varphi^{\prime}(x)
for all elements
x\in P
.

(
2
) Note that the profinite completion of a finitely generated free abelian group is a finitely generated free
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
-module, while
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\prod\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is torsion-free. Thus,
\widehat{P}
and
\widehat{Q}
are torsion-free. Besides,
\widehat{P_{0}}
is a finite-index subgroup of
\widehat{P}
by
Proposition 2.3
. Hence, (
2
) follows from the same proof as (
1
).

(
3
) Now we suppose
\Phi_{0}=\lambda\otimes{\varphi_{0}}
. We identify
P
and
Q
with their images in
\widehat{P}
and
\widehat{Q}
through the canonical homomorphisms. In order to show that the homomorphism
\Phi
can be decomposed as
\Phi=\lambda\otimes{\varphi}
, it suffices to prove that
\varphi(P)\subseteq\lambda\cdot Q
. We still denote
n
as the positive integer so that
n\cdot P\subseteq P_{0}
. Then for any
x\in P
,
n\cdot x\in P_{0}\subseteq\overline{P_{0}}
and
n\cdot\Phi(x)=\Phi_{0}(nx)\in\lambda Q
. Thus,
n\cdot\Phi(x)\in\lambda\cdot Q\cap n\cdot\widehat{Q}=\lambda(Q\cap n\cdot\widehat{Q})
, where the last equality holds since
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. By
[
40
, Lemma 2.2]
,
Q\cap n\cdot\widehat{Q}=n\cdot Q
, so
n\cdot\Phi(x)\in n\lambda\cdot Q
. Again, since
\widehat{Q}
is torsion-free, we derive that
\Phi(x)\in\lambda Q
, finishing the proof of (
3
).
∎

We are now ready to prove the general case of
Theorem 7.2
.

Proof of
Theorem 7.2
.

(
1
)
According to
Proposition 7.5
, there exists a
m
-fold regular cover
M^{\star}\to M
so that the map
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M^{\star}\to H_{1}({M^{\star}},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
is injective for each peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M^{\star}
. We identify
\pi_{1}M^{\star}
as an index-
m
normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}M
. Then
\overline{\pi_{1}M^{\star}}
is an open normal subgroup of
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
with index
m
, according to
Proposition 2.3
.

For any isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
,
f
sends
\overline{\pi_{1}M^{\star}}
to an index-
m
open normal subgroup of
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. Again, by
Proposition 2.3
,
f(\overline{\pi_{1}M^{\star}})
is the closure of an index-
m
normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}N
. Since
\pi_{1}N
is finitely generated,
\pi_{1}N
contains only finitely many index-
m
normal subgroups. Each of them corresponds to a
m
-fold regular cover of
N
, denoted by
N^{\star}_{1},\cdots,N^{\star}_{n}
, where each
\pi_{1}N^{\star}_{k}
is an index-
m
normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}N
.
Thus, there exists
1\leq k\leq n
such that
f(\overline{\pi_{1}M^{\star}})=\overline{\pi_{1}N^{\star}_{k}}
, and we denote the restriction of
f
as
f_{0}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M^{\star}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N^{\star}_{k}}
.

For each peripheral subgroup
P=\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
,
P_{0}:=P\cap\pi_{1}M^{\star}
can be identified as a peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\widetilde{\partial_{i}M}\subseteq\pi_{1}M^{\star}
, where
\widetilde{\partial_{i}{M}}
is one component of the pre-image of
\partial_{i}M
in
M^{\ast}
. In particular,
P_{0}
is a finite-index subgroup of
P
.

Note that
M^{\star}
and
N^{\star}_{k}
are again finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds. According to
Lemma 6.3
,
f_{0}
respects the peripheral structure, so there exists a peripheral subgroup
Q_{0}=\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N^{\star}_{k}
and an element
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{k}^{\star}}
such that
f_{0}(\overline{P_{0}})=C_{g}^{-1}(\overline{Q_{0}})
. Let
\partial_{j}N
be the image of
\partial_{j}{N_{k}^{\star}}
in
N
, and let
Q=\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
be the conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup which contains
Q_{0}
. Then,
f(\overline{P})
contains
f_{0}(\overline{P_{0}})
and intersects non-trivially with
C_{g}^{-1}(\overline{Q})
. Since
f
also respects the peripheral structure by
Lemma 6.3
,
f(\overline{P})
is also a conjugate of the closure of a peripheral subgroup in
N
. Then the malnormality of peripheral subgroups (
Lemma 6.2
) implies that
f(\overline{P})
is exactly
C_{g}^{-1}(\overline{Q})
.

Denote
f^{\prime}=C_{g}\circ f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. Note that
\widehat{\pi_{1}N^{\star}_{k}}
is a normal subgroup of
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
, so we still have
f^{\prime}(\widehat{\pi_{1}M^{\star}})=\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{k}^{\star}}
. In addition,
f^{\prime}(\overline{P_{0}})=\overline{Q_{0}}
, and
f^{\prime}(\overline{P})=\overline{Q}
.

Recall that all boundary components of
M^{\star}
are
H_{1}
-injective. According to
Lemma 7.9
, there exists a unified
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
such that
f_{0}
is peripheral
\lambda
-regular at all boundary components of
M^{\star}
.
In other words,
f^{\prime}|_{\overline{P_{0}}}=C_{g}\circ f_{0}|_{\overline{P_{0}}}
can be decomposed as
\lambda\otimes\psi_{0}
, where
\psi_{0}\in B(M^{\star},N_{k}^{\star};P_{0},Q_{0})\subseteq\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(P_{0},Q_{0})
. It then follows from
Lemma 7.10
(
3
) that
{f^{\prime}}|_{\overline{P}}=\lambda\otimes\psi
, where
\psi:P\to Q
satisfies
\psi|_{P_{0}}=\psi_{0}
, and
\psi
must be an isomorphism since
f^{\prime}(\overline{P})=\overline{Q}
. This implies that
f
is peripheral
\lambda
-regular at each
\partial_{i}M
, which finishes the proof of
Theorem 7.2
(
1
).

(
2
)
In addition, given any peripheral subgroups
P=\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
and
Q=\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
, for each
1\leq k\leq n
, denote

B_{k}(M,N;P,Q)=\left\{\psi:P\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}Q\left|\,\begin{gathered}\text{there exist }f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}\text{ and }\eta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times},\\
\text{such that }f\big(\overline{\pi_{1}M^{\star}}\big)=\overline{\pi_{1}N_{k}^{\star}},\\
f(\overline{P})=\overline{Q}\text{, and }f|_{\overline{P}}=\eta\otimes{\psi}\end{gathered}\right.\right\}.

By the reasoning in part (1),
B(M,N;P,Q)=\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}B_{k}(M,N;P,Q)
. Denote
P_{0}=P\cap\pi_{1}M^{\star}
, and
Q_{0}^{k}=Q\cap\pi_{1}N_{k}^{\star}
for each
1\leq k\leq n
, which are finite-index subgroups of
P
and
Q
respectively.
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}^{k}
are in fact peripheral subgroups in
\pi_{1}M^{\star}
and
\pi_{1}N^{\star}_{k}
. In addition, any element
\psi\in B_{k}(M,N;P,Q)
restricts to an element
\psi_{0}\in B(M^{\star},{N_{k}^{\star}};P_{0},Q_{0}^{k})
. Thus, by
Lemma 7.10
(
1
),
\#B_{k}(M,N;P,Q)\leq\#B(M^{\star},{N_{k}^{\star}};P_{0},Q_{0}^{k})
.

As a consequence,

\#B(M,N;P,Q)\leq\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\#B_{k}(M,N;P;Q)\leq\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\#B(M^{\star},{N_{k}^{\star}};P_{0},Q_{0}^{k})\leq 12n<\infty,

where the final inequality follows from
Lemma 7.9
. The proof of
Theorem 7.2
(
2
) then follows from
Lemma 7.8
.
∎

8.
Seifert fibered spaces

In our context, all Seifert fibered 3-manifolds are compact and orientable.

8.1.
Profinite properties of Seifert fibered spaces

Recall that Seifert fibered 3-manifolds with non-empty incompressible boundary consist of three cases, following the notation of
[
38
]
:

•

the thickened torus
S^{1}\times S^{1}\times I
;

•

the minor Seifert manifold, namely the twisted
I
-bundle over Klein bottle
S^{1}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$\SavedStyle\times$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.65277pt\raisebox{6.33333pt}{\hbox{$\SavedStyle_{\lx@scalerel@obj{\mkern 1.0mu\sim}}$}}\hskip-3.65277pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}}S^{1}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$\SavedStyle\times$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.65277pt\raisebox{6.33333pt}{\hbox{$\SavedStyle_{\lx@scalerel@obj{\mkern 1.0mu\sim}}$}}\hskip-3.65277pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}}I
;

•

major Seifert manifolds, i.e. those admitting a Seifert fibration over a base orbifold
\mathcal{O}
with negative Euler characteristic and non-empty boundary.

According to
[
27
, Lemma 3.2]
, from a Seifert fibration
M\to\mathcal{O}
one derives a short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow\pi_{1}M\longrightarrow\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow 1,

where
K
denotes the cyclic subgroup generated by a regular fiber, and
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
denotes the orbifold fundamental group. When
M
has non-empty boundary,
K
is infinite cyclic. In particular, when
M
is a major Seifert fibered manifold,
M
admits a unique Seifert fibration and
K
is the unique maximal virtually central subgroup of
\pi_{1}M
.

In addition, the LERFness of
\pi_{1}M

[
26
]
together with
Proposition 2.1
and
Proposition 2.2

implies a short exact sequence

(8.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{K}\cong\overline{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

Proposition 8.2

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
M
be a Seifert fibered manifold with non-empty incompressible boundary.
Then,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
determines whether
M
is major, minor, or the thickened torus. In addition, when
M
is a major Seifert manifold,

(1)

\overline{K}
is the unique maximal virtually central normal procyclic subgroup of
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
;

(2)

\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
determines whether the base orbifold
\mathcal{O}
is orientable;

(3)

\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
determines the isomorphism type of the orbifold fundamental group
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

Among Seifert fibered manifolds with non-empty incompressible boundary, a manifold
M
is major, minor, or the thickened torus if and only if the fundamental group
\pi_{1}M
is not virtually abelian, virtually abelian but non-abelian, or abelian, which can be detected from the profinite completion.

When
M
is a major Seifert manifold, (
1
) follows from
[
38
, Theorem 5.5]
. In addition, the base orbifold
\mathcal{O}
is orientable if and only if
K
is central in
\pi_{1}M
, which is equivalent to
\overline{K}
being central in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
. Thus, by (
1
),
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
determines whether
\mathcal{O}
is orientable. Moreover, (
1
) shows that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
determines the isomorphism type of
\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M}/\overline{K}
. Note that
\chi(\mathcal{O})<0
, so
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
is a finitely-generated Fuchsian group.
[
2
, Theorem 1.1]
shows that the isomorphism type of
\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}}
determines the isomorphism type of
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
, which finishes the proof of (
3
).
∎

Corollary 8.3
.

The class of groups

\mathcal{S}=\{\pi_{1}M\mid M\text{: orientable Seifert fibered space with }\text{non-empty }\text{boundary}\}

is (relatively) profinite rigid. In other words, for any two Seifert fibered spaces
M
and
N
with non-empty boundary,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
if and only if
\pi_{1}M\cong\pi_{1}N
.

Proof.

The only Seifert fibered space with compressible boundary is the solid torus, whereas
\pi_{1}(D^{2}\times S^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is profinitely rigid among all finitely-generated residually finite groups. Also note that
\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times S^{1}\times I)\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
and
\pi_{1}(S^{1}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$\SavedStyle\times$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.65277pt\raisebox{6.33333pt}{\hbox{$\SavedStyle_{\lx@scalerel@obj{\mkern 1.0mu\sim}}$}}\hskip-3.65277pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}}S^{1}\mathbin{\ThisStyle{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$\SavedStyle\times$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.65277pt\raisebox{6.33333pt}{\hbox{$\SavedStyle_{\lx@scalerel@obj{\mkern 1.0mu\sim}}$}}\hskip-3.65277pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}}I)\cong\mathbb{Z}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}
are both profinitely rigid in
\mathcal{S}
by
Proposition 8.2
. Thus, it suffices to restrict ourselves to major Seifert manifolds.

We claim that for a major Seifert manifold
M
, the isomorphism type of
\pi_{1}M
is uniquely determined by the following two data:

(1)

the isomorphism type of the orbifold fundamental group
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
;

(2)

whether
\mathcal{O}
is orientable.

Part of this result was proven in
[
15
, Lemma 4.2]
.

In fact,
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
is virtually free, and its isomorphism type determines the orbifold Euler characteristic
\chi(\mathcal{O})
. To be explicit, if
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
contains an index-
m
subgroup isomorphic to
F_{r}
, then
\chi(\mathcal{O})=\frac{1-r}{m}
. In addition,
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
determines the singularities of
\mathcal{O}
, each corresponding to a conjugacy class of maximal torsion subgroups in
\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}
, and the index of the singular point is exactly the order of the (finite cyclic) torsion subgroup. We denote the index of singularities by
p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}
, and denote the underlying surface of
\mathcal{O}
as
F
. Then,
\chi(F)=\chi(O)+\sum(1-\frac{1}{p_{i}})
can also be determined from these data.

As shown in
Figure 1
, the orbifold
\mathcal{O}
can be obtained from
F
by gluing disks
D_{i}
with exactly one singularity of index
p_{i}
to a boundary component of
F
along the arcs
\alpha_{i}
. Consequently,
M
can be obtained from the orientable
S^{1}
-bundle over
F
, by gluing solid tori
V_{i}
to a boundary component
S^{1}\times\partial_{i}F
along annuli
S^{1}\times\alpha_{i}
, so that the fiber
S^{1}\times\{\ast\}
intersects
p_{i}
times the meridian of
V_{i}
.

A surface with no singular points, and several disks glued to one of its boundary components along disjoint arcs, each containing one singular point.

Figure 1.
The orbifold
\mathcal{O}

Note that the fundamental group of the orientable
S^{1}
-bundle over
F
has the presentation:
\left\langle c_{1},\cdots,c_{k},h\mid c_{j}hc_{j}^{-1}=h^{\epsilon}\right\rangle
, where
k=1-\chi(F)
, and
\epsilon=1
if
F
is orientable, while
\epsilon=-1
if
F
is non-orientable. Here,
h
represents a regular fiber
S^{1}\times\{\ast\}
. Then, by Seifert–van Kampen theorem, we obtain that
\pi_{1}(M)=\left\langle c_{1},\cdots,c_{k},h,v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\mid c_{j}hc_{j}^{-1}=h^{\epsilon},h=v_{i}^{p_{i}}\right\rangle
, where each
v_{i}
is a conjugacy representative of the core curve of
V_{i}
. Hence, the isomorphism type of
\pi_{1}M
is determined from the two data (
1
) and (
2
).

Since these two data can be encoded from
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
as shown by
Proposition 8.2
, we derive that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
determines the unique isomorphism type of
\pi_{1}M
.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

Also note that the class
\mathcal{S}
can be profinitely distinguished from the fundamental groups of all compact, orientable 3-manifolds, according to
Corollary 4.2
which determines irreducibility and the toral boundary, together with
Theorem 5.5
which determines the JSJ-decomposition. Thus, any
G\in\mathcal{S}
is profinitely rigid among the fundamental groups of all compact, orientable 3-manifolds.

Note that in the bounded case, the group theoretic profinite rigidity does not imply rigidity in the class of manifolds as defined in
Definition 1.3
, since there exist non-homeomorphic Seifert manifolds with isomorphic fundamental groups. Nevertheless, we can still derive profinite almost rigidity in the manifold version.

Proposition 8.5
.

The class of orientable Seifert fibered spaces is profinitely almost rigid.

Proof.

The only orientable Seifert fibered space with compressible boundary is the solid torus, which has the same fundamental group as
S^{2}\times S^{1}
.
Except for this case,
Corollary 4.2
implies that
the profinite completion detects whether the manifold is closed.
The profinite almost rigidity of closed Seifert fibered 3-manifolds follows from
[
38
, Theorem 5.1]
; and the profinite almost rigidity of Seifert fibered 3-manifolds with incompressible boundary follows from
Corollary 8.3
, since there are only finitely many Seifert fibered spaces with isomorphic fundamental groups, see
Theorem 1.6
.
∎

8.2.
Profinite isomorphism between Seifert manifolds

We start with the simple case of minor Seifert manifolds.
The fundamental group of a minor Seifert manifold can be presented as

(8.6)

\pi_{1}M=\left\langle a,b\mid bab^{-1}=a^{-1}\right\rangle,

where
\langle a\rangle
represents the fiber subgroup when the manifold is viewed as a Seifert fibration over the Möbius band, while
\langle b^{2}\rangle
represents the fiber subgroup when it is viewed as a Seifert fibration over a disk with two singular points of index 2. Then.
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}=\overline{\left\langle a\right\rangle}\rtimes\overline{\left\langle b\right\rangle}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\rtimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
. The unique peripheral subgroup in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
is given by
\overline{\left\langle a\right\rangle}\times\overline{\left\langle b^{2}\right\rangle}
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are minor Seifert manifolds, whose fundamental groups are
presented as

\begin{gathered}\pi_{1}M=\left\langle a,b\mid bab^{-1}=a^{-1}\right\rangle,\;\;\pi_{1}N=\left\langle\alpha,\beta\mid\beta\alpha\beta^{-1}=\alpha^{-1}\right\rangle.\end{gathered}

For any isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
, there exist
\lambda,\mu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and
\theta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

f(a)=\alpha^{\mu},\;f(b)=\alpha^{\theta}\beta^{\lambda},\;f(b^{2})=\beta^{2\lambda}.

In particular,
f
always respects the peripheral structure.

Proof.

It is easy to verify that the center of
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
is
\overline{\left\langle b^{2}\right\rangle}
, and similarly, the center of
\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is
\overline{\left\langle\beta^{2}\right\rangle}
. Thus,
f(\overline{\left\langle b^{2}\right\rangle})=\overline{\left\langle\beta^{2}\right\rangle}
so there exists
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
such that
f(b^{2})=\beta^{2\lambda}
.

Let
p:\widehat{\pi_{1}N}\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\widehat{\left\langle\beta\right\rangle}
denote the homomorphism defined by quotienting
\overline{\left\langle\alpha\right\rangle}
. Then
p(f(b))^{2}=p(f(b^{2}))=\beta^{2\lambda}
. Since
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion-free,
p(f(b))=\beta^{\lambda}
, which implies that
f(b)=\alpha^{\theta}\beta^{\lambda}
for some
\theta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Since
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is abelian, we have
p(f(a))^{2}=p(f(bab^{-1}a))=p(f(\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}))=\beta^{0}
. As
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion-free, it follows that
p(f(a))=\beta^{0}
, i.e.
f(a)\in\overline{\left\langle\alpha\right\rangle}
, or equivalently,
f(\overline{\left\langle a\right\rangle})\subseteq\overline{\left\langle\alpha\right\rangle}
. The symmetric proof for
f^{-1}
implies that

f^{-1}(\overline{\left\langle\alpha\right\rangle})\subseteq\overline{\left\langle a\right\rangle}
. As a consequence,

f(\overline{\left\langle a\right\rangle})=\overline{\left\langle\alpha\right\rangle}
, so
f(a)=\alpha^{\mu}
for some
\mu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

In particular,
f(\overline{\left\langle a\right\rangle}\times\overline{\left\langle b^{2}\right\rangle})=\overline{\left\langle\alpha\right\rangle}\times\overline{\left\langle\beta^{2}\right\rangle}
. In other words,
f
respects the peripheral structure.
∎

In order to avoid ambiguity in assigning the fiber subgroup of the minor Seifert manifold, we include the following convention

Convention 8.8
.

From now on, we always view the minor Seifert manifold as a Seifert fibration over the disk with two singular points of index 2. In this case, the fiber subgroup
K_{M}\leq\pi_{1}M
is generated by
b^{2}
, following the presentation (
8.6
).

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
M
and
N
be major or minor Seifert manifolds such that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. Then, any isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
splits as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{K_{M}}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{K_{N}}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\natural}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Proof.

In view of (
8.1
), it suffices to show that
f(\overline{K_{M}})=\overline{K_{N}}
.
By
Proposition 8.2
, either
M
and
N
are both minor, or they are both major. For the minor case, this follows from
Lemma 8.7
; while in the major case, this follows from
Proposition 8.2
(
1
).
∎

Example 8.10
.

Let
M
and
N
be major or minor Seifert manifolds, and let
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
be an isomorphism. Suppose for some boundary components
\partial_{i}M
and
\partial_{j}N
,
f
sends
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
to a conjugate of
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}
. We may choose a free basis
\{h_{M},\epsilon_{i}\}
for
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, so that
h_{M}
is a generator for the fiber subgroup
K_{M}
. Similarly, choose a free basis
\{h_{N},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime}\}
for
\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N
so that
h_{N}
generates
K_{N}
. Since
f(\overline{K_{M}})=\overline{K_{N}}
, up to composing with a conjugation by
g\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
,
C_{g}\circ f(h_{M})=h_{N}^{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, and the map
C_{g}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}:\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\to\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N}
can be represented by an upper triangle matrix:

C_{g}\circ f\begin{pmatrix}h_{M}&\epsilon_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}h_{N}&\epsilon_{j}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&\rho\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&\rho\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}).

Indeed, the entries
\lambda
and
\mu
are uniquely determined by
f
up to
\pm
-signs, that is to say they are not affected by different choices of bases.
In particular, when
M
and
N
are minor Seifert manifolds, if we choose
(h,\epsilon)=(b^{2},a)
following (
8.6
), then
C_{g}\circ f
is represented by a diagonal matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\mu\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

8.3.
Profinite isomorphism respecting peripheral structure

For a Seifert manifold
M
with boundary,
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
marked with the peripheral structure
\{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\}
entails extra information beyond
Proposition 8.2
. For instance, this additionally determines the number of boundary components of the base orbifold, which determines the unique isomorphism type of the base orbifold. Following the setting of
Lemma 8.9
, if
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\to\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
respects the peripheral structure, then so does
f_{\flat}
. In addition, we have the following characterization for
f_{\flat}
.

Definition 8.11

(
[
40
, Definition 2.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an orientable 2-orbifold with boundary, with fundamental group

\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}=\left<a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},e_{1},\ldots,e_{s},u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{g},v_{g}\mid a_{j}^{p_{j}}\right>,

where the boundary components of
\mathcal{O}
are represented by the conjugacy classes of the elements
e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}
together with

e_{0}=\left(a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1}\cdots e_{s}[u_{1},v_{1}]\cdots[u_{g},v_{g}]\right)^{-1}.

Then, an
exotic automorphism of
\mathcal{O}
of type
\mu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
is an automorphism
\psi\colon\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}}\to\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}}
such that
\psi(a_{j})\sim a_{j}^{\mu}
and
\psi(e_{i})\sim e_{i}^{\mu}
for all
1\leq j\leq r
and
0\leq i\leq s
, where
\sim
denotes conjugacy in
\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}}
.

Similarly, let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
be a non-orientable 2-orbifold with boundary, with fundamental group

\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\left<a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},e_{1},\ldots,e_{s},u_{1},\ldots,u_{g}\mid a_{j}^{p_{j}}\right>,

where the boundary components of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
are represented by the conjugacy classes of the elements
e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}
together with

e_{0}=\left(a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1}\cdots e_{s}u_{1}^{2}\cdots u_{g}^{2}\right)^{-1}.

Let
o\colon\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}\to\{\pm 1\}
be the orientation homomorphism of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.
Then, an
exotic automorphism of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
of type
\mu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
is an automorphism
\psi\colon\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}\to\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}
such that
\psi(a_{j})\sim a_{j}^{\mu}
and
\psi(e_{i})=g_{i}\cdot e_{i}^{o(g_{i})\mu}\cdot g_{i}^{-1}
, where
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 8.12

(
[
38
,
40
]
)
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are major or minor Seifert manifolds, and
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism respecting the peripheral structure.
Then:

(1)

The base orbifolds
\mathcal{O}_{M}
and
\mathcal{O}_{N}
are isomorphic.

(2)

By choosing an appropriate isomorphism of orbifolds,
\mathcal{O}_{M}
and
\mathcal{O}_{N}
can be identified as the same orbifold
\mathcal{O}
, so that the isomorphism
f_{\flat}
given by
Lemma 8.9
is an exotic automorphism of
\mathcal{O}
of type
\mu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

(3)

By choosing generators
h_{M}\in K_{M}
and
h_{N}\in K_{N}
, the isomorphism
f_{\natural}
given by
Lemma 8.9
sends
h_{M}
to
h_{N}^{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

(4)

Denote
\kappa=\mu^{-1}\lambda
. If
M
has Seifert invariants
(p_{j},q_{j})
, then by identifying the base orbifolds as in (
2
) and the fiber subgroups as in (
3
),
N
has Seifert invariants
(p_{j},q_{j}^{\prime})
where
q_{j}^{\prime}\equiv\kappa q_{j}\pmod{p_{j}}
.

Proof.

When
M
and
N
are major Seifert manifolds, this follows from
[
40
, Theorem 2.7]
and
[
38
, Theorem 5.8]
.

When
M
and
N
are minor Seifert manifolds, this follows from
Lemma 8.7
. In fact, following the group presentation (
8.6
), the boundary component of the base orbifold
D^{2}_{(2,2)}
is represented by the image of
a
in
\pi_{1}M/\langle b^{2}\rangle
, and
f(a)=\alpha^{\mu}
according to
Lemma 8.7
, which proves (
2
). Meanwhile, (
4
) automatically holds in the minor case, since
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
has only one element.
∎

In this case,
(M,N)
is called a
Hempel pair
of scale factor
\kappa
, and
f
is called a profinite isomorphism of
scale type

(\lambda,\mu)
. Note that the parameters
\lambda
and
\mu
are uniquely determined up to
\pm
-signs, regardless of the group presentation one chooses.

Proposition 8.12
can also be described group-theoretically. For instance, when
\mathcal{O}
is non-orientable, we can express
\pi_{1}M
and
\pi_{1}N
as

(8.13)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}M=\left<\left.\begin{gathered}a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{s},\\
u_{1},\ldots,u_{g},h_{M}\end{gathered}\right.\left|\,\begin{gathered}e_{0}=\left(a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1}\cdots e_{s}u_{1}^{2}\cdots u_{g}^{2}\right)^{-1}\\
a_{j}^{p_{j}}h_{M}^{q_{j}}=1,[a_{j},h_{M}]=1,\\
[e_{i},h_{M}]=1,\,u_{i}h_{M}u_{i}^{-1}=h_{M}^{-1}\end{gathered}\right.\right>,

\displaystyle\pi_{1}N=\left<\left.\begin{gathered}a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{r}^{\prime},e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{s}^{\prime},\\
u_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{g}^{\prime},h_{N}\end{gathered}\right.\left|\,\begin{gathered}e_{0}^{\prime}=\left(a_{1}^{\prime}\cdots a_{r}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\cdots e_{s}^{\prime}u_{1}^{\prime 2}\cdots u_{g}^{\prime 2}\right)^{-1}\\
(a_{j}^{\prime})^{p_{j}}h_{N}^{q_{j}^{\prime}}=1,\,[a_{j}^{\prime},h_{N}]=1,\\
[e_{i}^{\prime},h_{N}]=1,\,u_{i}^{\prime}h_{N}u_{i}^{\prime-1}=h_{N}^{-1}\end{gathered}\right.\right>,

where
q_{j}^{\prime}\equiv\kappa q_{j}\pmod{p_{j}}
, such that
f(h_{M})=h_{N}^{\lambda}
, and
f(e_{i})=g_{i}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{o(g_{i})\mu}g_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
for some
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
and
\rho_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
. Here,

o:\widehat{\pi_{1}N}\longrightarrow\widehat{\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{N}}\longrightarrow\{\pm 1\}

denotes the orientation homomorphism.

Similarly, when
\mathcal{O}
is orientable, there exist presentations for
\pi_{1}M
and
\pi_{1}N

(8.14)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}M=\left<\left.\begin{gathered}a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{s},\\
u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{g},v_{g},h_{M}\end{gathered}\right.\left|\,\begin{gathered}e_{0}=\left(a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1}\cdots e_{s}[u_{1},v_{1}]\cdots[u_{g},v_{g}]\right)^{-1}\\
a_{j}^{p_{j}}h_{M}^{q_{j}}=1,\,h_{M}\text{ central}\end{gathered}\right.\right>,

\displaystyle\pi_{1}N=\left<\left.\begin{gathered}a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{r}^{\prime},e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{s}^{\prime},\\
u_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{g}^{\prime},v_{g}^{\prime},h_{N}\end{gathered}\right.\left|\,\begin{gathered}e_{0}^{\prime}=\left(a_{1}^{\prime}\cdots a_{r}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\cdots e_{s}^{\prime}[u_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime}]\cdots[u_{g}^{\prime},v_{g}^{\prime}]\right)^{-1}\\
(a_{j}^{\prime})^{p_{j}}h_{N}^{q_{j}^{\prime}}=1,\,h_{N}\text{ central}\end{gathered}\right.\right>,

where
q_{j}^{\prime}\equiv\kappa q_{j}\pmod{p_{j}}
, such that
f(h_{M})=h_{N}^{\lambda}
, and
f(e_{i})=g_{i}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{\mu}g_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
for some
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
and
\rho_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For consistency of notation, when
\mathcal{O}
is orientable, we define

o:\widehat{\pi_{1}N}\to\{\pm 1\}

to be the trivial homomorphism.

Consequently, when
f
respects the peripheral structure, regardless whether
\mathcal{O}
is orientable,
f
restricting on the peripheral subgroups can be represented by matrices as shown by
Example 8.10
:

C_{g_{i}^{-1}}\circ f\begin{pmatrix}h_{M}&e_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}h_{N}&e_{i}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}o(g_{i})\lambda&o(g_{i})\rho_{i}\\
0&o(g_{i})\mu\end{pmatrix},

where the pair of diagonal elements
(\lambda,\mu)
is exactly the scale type of
f
, and is unified for all the boundary components up to
\pm
-signs.

For brevity of notation, the elements
e_{i}
(or
e_{i}^{\prime}
) are called
peripheral cross-sections
in the following context. Indeed, they can be identified as the boundary components of a cross-section of the base orbifold removing the neighbourhoods of the singular points.
Note that the peripheral cross-sections are not unique, and they in fact depend on the precise Seifert invariants.
Indeed, on each component of
\partial M
, the basis
\{h_{M},e_{i}\}
determines an orientation compatible with each other, and further determines the invariant
\sum\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}
.

In particular, when
\lambda=\pm\mu
, a Hempel pair of scale factor
\pm 1
is a pair of homeomorphic Seifert manifolds, according to the classification of Seifert fibrations.

In the following, we denote
\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=\left\{\frac{\alpha}{p}\mid\alpha\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}},\,p\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\right\}
as the localization. Since
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion-free,
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
injects into
\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
