.
The following lemma is based on the calculation of homology.

Lemma 8.15
.

Let
(M,N)
be a Hempel pair of scale factor
\kappa=\mu^{-1}\lambda
, and
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
be a profinite isomorphism respecting the peripheral structure, with scale type
(\lambda,\mu)
. Choose presentations of the fundamental groups as in (
8.13
) and (
8.14
), so that

(8.16)

f(h_{M})=h_{N}^{\lambda}\text{ and }f(e_{i})=g_{i}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{o(g_{i})\mu}g_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}.

Then, in
\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i}=\lambda\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}-\mu\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\frac{q_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}.

Proof.

We first prove the case when
M
and
N
have orientable base orbifolds. In this case,
in
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})
we have

(8.17)

\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\left[e_{i}\right]=-\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{r}\left[a_{l}\right]=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}[h_{M}].

Similarly, in
H_{1}(N;\mathbb{Z})
we have

(8.18)

{\textstyle\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\left[e_{i}^{\prime}\right]=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}^{\prime}[h_{N}].}

Let
f_{\ast}:\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({M},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
be the isomorphism induced by
f
according to
Proposition 2.11
. Then, in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
,

\displaystyle{\textstyle\lambda\cdot\big(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}\big)}[h_{N}]=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\displaystyle f_{\ast}\Big((\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j})[h_{M}]\Big)}

\displaystyle(\ref{8.9equ1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\displaystyle f_{\ast}\Big(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\left[e_{i}\right]\Big)}

\displaystyle(\ref{8.9equ2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\displaystyle\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\left(\mu[e_{i}^{\prime}]+\rho_{i}[h_{N}]\right)}

\displaystyle(\ref{8.9equ1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\displaystyle\mu\cdot\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\left[e_{i}^{\prime}\right]+\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i})[h_{N}]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\displaystyle\Big(\mu\cdot\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}^{\prime}+\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i})\Big)[h_{N}]}

\displaystyle(\ref{8.9equ3})

According to
[
21
, Lemma 2]
,
[h_{N}]
is non-torsion in
H_{1}(N;\mathbb{Z})
when the base orbifold
\mathcal{O}_{N}
is orientable, i.e.
[h_{N}]\neq 0
in the finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}
-module
H_{1}({N},\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{free}}
. Thus, the above coefficients must be equal:

{\textstyle\lambda\cdot\big(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}\big)=\mu\cdot\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}^{\prime}+\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i})}.

Quotienting the non-zero integer
\prod_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}
implies

\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i}=\lambda\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}-\mu\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\frac{q_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}.

Next, we move on to the case when
M
and
N
have non-orientable base orbifolds. Let
M_{o}
and
N_{o}
be the two-fold covers of
M
and
N
corresponding to the centralizer of the fiber subgroups, i.e.
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{o}}\cong\overline{\pi_{1}M_{o}}
is the centralizer of
\overline{K_{M}}
, and so is
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{o}}
. Since
f(\overline{K_{M}})=\overline{K_{N}}
by
Proposition 8.2
,
f
restricts to an isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{o}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{o}}
that also respects the peripheral structure. In fact, the scale type of this isomorphism is also
(\lambda,\mu)
.

To be explicit, pick an element
\sigma\in\pi_{1}M\setminus\pi_{1}M_{o}
, and let
d_{i}=\sigma e_{i}^{-1}\sigma^{-1}
. Note that each boundary component as well as each singular fiber of
M
lifts to two components in
M_{o}
, and the conjugation by
\sigma
on
\pi_{1}M_{o}
corresponds to the deck transformation on
M_{o}
which is orientation-preserving but fiber-reversing. Thus,
\{h_{M}^{-1},d_{i}^{-1}\}=\{\sigma h_{M}\sigma^{-1},\sigma e_{i}\sigma^{-1}\}
determines an orientation on the boundary component, which is consistent with
\{h_{M},e_{i}\}
. By simultaneously reversing the two generators, the elements
e_{0},\cdots,e_{s},d_{0},\cdots,d_{s}
become the (conjugates of) peripheral cross-sections in
\pi_{1}M_{o}
. Together with the orientation on the fiber determined by the generator
h_{M}
, this determines the Seifert invariants of
M_{o}
as
(p_{1},q_{1}),(p_{1},q_{1}),\cdots,(p_{r},q_{r}),(p_{r},q_{r})
, i.e. copying the Seifert invariants of
M
twice with the same
\pm
-sign.

Similarly, pick
\delta\in\pi_{1}N\setminus\pi_{1}N_{o}
, and let
d_{i}^{\prime}=\delta e_{i}^{\prime-1}\delta^{-1}
. Then the Seifert invariants of
N_{o}
, determined by these peripheral cross-sections and the fiber generator
h_{N}
, are
(p_{1},q_{1}^{\prime}),(p_{1},q_{1}^{\prime}),\cdots,(p_{r},q_{r}^{\prime}),(p_{r},q_{r}^{\prime})
.

For each
0\leq i\leq s
,
f(e_{i})=g_{i}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{o(g_{i})\mu}g_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
where
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
. If
o(g_{i})=1
, i.e.
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{o}}
, then it is easy to verify that
f(d_{i})=g_{i}^{\prime}(d_{i}^{\prime})^{\mu}g_{i}^{\prime-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
, where
g_{i}^{\prime}=f(\sigma)g_{i}\delta^{-1}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{o}}
. If
o(g_{i})={-1}
, then similarly,
f(e_{i})=l_{i}(d_{i}^{\prime})^{\mu}l_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
and
f(d_{i})=l_{i}^{\prime}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{\mu}l_{i}^{\prime-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
, where
l_{i}=g_{i}\delta^{-1}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{o}}
and
l_{i}^{\prime}=f(\sigma)g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{o}}
.

This implies that
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{o}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{o}}
has scale type
(\lambda,\mu)
. The same calculation in the homology shows that

\begin{gathered}{\textstyle\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\big(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\left[e_{i}\right]+\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}[d_{i}]\big)=2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}[h_{M}]\in H_{1}(M_{o};\mathbb{Z}),}\\
{\textstyle\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}\big(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\left[e_{i}^{\prime}\right]+\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}[d_{i}^{\prime}]\big)=2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}(\prod\limits_{k\neq j}p_{k})q_{j}^{\prime}[h_{N}]\in H_{1}(N_{o};\mathbb{Z}).}\end{gathered}

Since
M_{o}
and
N_{o}
have orientable base orbifolds, the calculation in the first case implies that

2\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i}=\lambda\cdot 2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}-\mu\cdot 2\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\frac{q_{j}^{\prime}}{p_{j}}.

Quotienting the coefficient
2
yields the conclusion.
∎

8.4.
Realizing as homeomorphism

This subsection serves as a preparation for the proof of
Theorem 1.15
. In particular, we prove
Theorem 1.15
(
2
) with the graph manifolds replaced by Seifert fibered spaces.

Proposition 8.19
.

Let
M
and
N
be Seifert fibered manifolds with non-empty incompressible boundary. Suppose that
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism respecting the peripheral structure, and that
f
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at some selected boundary components
\partial_{0}M,\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M
of
M
, where
1\leq k+1\leq\#\partial M
.
1
1

1

The indices start from
0
just for consistency with the subsequent notations.

(1)

Then,
M
and
N
are homeomorphic.

(2)

There exists an element
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and two homeomorphisms
\Phi^{\pm}:M\to N
such that for each
0\leq i\leq k
,
f
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{\pm}
with coefficient
\pm\lambda
, where the
\pm
-signs are consistent.

It is worth pointing out that a homeomorphism between the manifolds is nothing more than a group isomorphism between their fundamental groups respecting the peripheral structure, as is proven by Waldhausen
[
37
]
.

Proposition 8.20
.

Let
M
and
N
be compact, irreducible 3-manifolds with non-empty incompressible boundary. Then any isomorphism
\varphi:\pi_{1}M\to\pi_{1}N
respecting the peripheral structure can be induced, up to conjugation, by
a homeomorphism
\Phi:M\to N
.

Thus, the homeomorphisms in
Proposition 8.19
can either be constructed in a concrete way, or be obtained from a group theoretical construction. Our proof will be a combination of these two methods.

Lemma 8.21
.

Let
M
be a major or a minor Seifert manifold. Let
e_{0},\cdots,e_{s}
denote the peripheral cross-sections and let
h_{M}
denote a generator of the fiber subgroup. For any
t_{0},\cdots,t_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{s}t_{i}=0
, there exists a homeomorphism
\Psi:M\to M
such that
\Psi_{\ast}(h_{M})=h_{M}
and
\Psi_{\ast}(e_{i})=g_{i}e_{i}g_{i}^{-1}h_{M}^{t_{i}}
, where every
g_{i}
belongs to the kernel of the orientation homomorphism
o
.

Proof.

In fact, this can be realized through vertical Dehn twists. Let
F
be a compact, connected, orientable sub-surface of the base orbifold
\mathcal{O}_{M}
which contains all the boundary components of
M
, while it contains no singular points. Then the union of fibers over
F
is a sub-manifold of
M
containing
\partial M
, which decomposes as
F\times S^{1}
.

We may choose disjoint arcs
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{s}
in
F
connecting the boundary components of
F\cap\partial\mathcal{O}_{M}
that correspond to
e_{0},\cdots,e_{s}
one-by-one. Let
\Psi_{i}
be the vertical Dehn twist (with appropriate orientation) supported on
N(\gamma_{i})\times S^{1}
, where
N(\gamma_{i})
denotes the neighbourhood of
\gamma_{i}
in
F
. Then
\Psi=\Psi_{1}^{t_{0}}\circ\Psi_{2}^{t_{1}+t_{0}}\circ\cdots\circ\Psi_{s}^{t_{s-1}+\cdots+t_{0}}
meets the requirement.

Group theoretically, if we fix the presentation of the fundamental group as in (
8.13
) or (
8.14
), then
\Psi_{\ast}
simply sends
e_{i}
to
e_{i}h_{M}^{t_{i}}
and fixes all the other generators.
∎

Lemma 8.22
.

Suppose
M
is a major Seifert manifold with a non-orientable base orbifold. Let
e_{0},\cdots,e_{s}
denote the peripheral cross-sections, let
h_{M}
be a generator of the fiber subgroup, and let
o:\pi_{1}M\to\pi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{M}\to\{\pm 1\}
be the orientation homomorphism. Then, for any
\epsilon_{0},\cdots,\epsilon_{s}\in\{\pm 1\}
, there exists a homeomorphism
\Theta:M\to M
such that
\Theta_{\ast}(h_{M})=h_{M}
and
\Theta_{\ast}(e_{i})=g_{i}e_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}g_{i}^{-1}
, where
o(g_{i})=\epsilon_{i}
.

Proof.

By composition, it suffices to prove the conclusion when exactly one of
\epsilon_{i}
is
-1
.
We first show that the conclusion holds when
\epsilon_{0}=-1
and
\epsilon_{1}=\cdots=\epsilon_{s}=1
.

Fix the presentation

\pi_{1}M=\left<\left.\begin{gathered}a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{s},\\
u_{1},\ldots,u_{g},h_{M}\end{gathered}\right.\left|\,\begin{gathered}e_{0}=\left(a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1}\cdots e_{s}u_{1}^{2}\cdots u_{g}^{2}\right)^{-1}\\
a_{j}^{p_{j}}h_{M}^{q_{j}}=1,\,[a_{j},h_{M}]=1,\\
[e_{i},h_{M}]=1,\,u_{i}h_{M}u_{i}^{-1}=h_{M}^{-1}\end{gathered}\right.\right>.

Denote
t=a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1}\cdots e_{s}u_{1}^{2}\cdots u_{g-1}^{2}
, and let
\theta:\pi_{1}M\to\pi_{1}M
be defined by:

\displaystyle\theta(h_{M})=h_{M},

\displaystyle\theta(a_{j})=a_{j},

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq r,

\displaystyle\theta(e_{i})=e_{i},

\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq s,

\displaystyle\theta(u_{k})=u_{k},

\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g-1,

\displaystyle\theta(u_{g})=t^{-1}u_{g}^{-1}.

It is easy to verify that
\theta
is a group isomorphism and
\theta(e_{0})=\theta(u_{g}^{-2}t^{-1})=u_{g}tu_{g}=u_{g}e_{0}^{-1}u_{g}^{-1}
, where
o(u_{g})=-1
. In particular,
\theta
respects the peripheral structure, so it is induced by a homeomorphism
\Theta
by
Proposition 8.20
, which satisfies our requirement.

Now we assume
\epsilon_{j}=-1
, and
\epsilon_{i}=1
for all
i\neq j
.
Note that the boundary components of
M
are symmetric, i.e. there exists a homeomorphism
\Phi
on
M
switching the two boundary components corresponding to
\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon_{j}
, while preserving the cross-section of the entire orbifold with the neighbourhoods of the singular points removed. Then up to composing with a conjugation,
\Phi_{\ast}
sends
h_{M}
to
h_{M}
, and sends
e_{i}
to
\alpha_{i}e_{\sigma(i)}^{o(\alpha_{i})}\alpha_{i}^{-1}
, where
\sigma(0)=j
and
\sigma(j)=0
. Let
\Theta_{j}=\Phi^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ\Phi
. One can easily verify that
\Theta_{j}
satisfies the requirement.

From another perspective, this is in fact realized by a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-equivariant homeomorphism on the two-fold cover
M_{o}
corresponding to the kernel of
o
, which swaps the two components in the pre-image of each boundary component of
M
corresponding to
e_{i}
with
\epsilon_{i}=-1
.
∎

Lemma 8.23
.

Let
M
be a Seifert fibered 3-manifold with incompressible boundary. Then there exists a homeomorphism
\tau:M\to M
preserving each boundary component, such that up to composing with a conjugation,
\tau_{\ast}
is a scalar multiplication by
-1
on each peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

When
M
is the minor Seifert manifold, this follows from the group theoretical construction as in
Proposition 8.20
, i.e. an automorphism of
\pi_{1}M=\left\langle a,b\mid bab^{-1}=a^{-1}\right\rangle
sending
a
to
a^{-1}
and
b
to
b^{-1}
.

Also note that there is an automorphism of the solid torus
V
, which induces the
-id
–map on its boundary.

Generally,
M
can be constructed in the following way. Take a compact, orientable surface
F
, and let
X=F\times S^{1}
. Choose some of the boundary components of
X
, and
M
is obtained through attaching either a solid torus or a minor Seifert manifold to each of them in an appropriate way.

Let
\sigma
be an orientation-reversing involution of
F
preserving each boundary component, and let
r
denote the reflection on
S^{1}
. Then the involution
\tau=(\sigma,r)
on
X
restricts to a scalar multiplication by
-1
on each boundary component. Thus, for any of the above chosen boundary components
\partial_{i}X
,
\tau|_{\partial_{i}X}
can be extended as a homeomorphism on the filled-in solid torus or minor Seifert manifold as shown by the above construction. This yields an automorphism of
M
satisfying the required condition.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Proposition 8.19
through combining the above constructions.

Proof of
Proposition 8.19
.

Note that
Proposition 8.19
holds apparently for the thickened tori. Thus, we assume that
M
and
N
are either major or minor Seifert manifolds in the following proof.

Suppose the scale type of
f
is
(\lambda,\mu)
.
Since
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at some of the boundary components, it follows from
Example 8.10
that

{\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda&\rho\\
0&\mu\end{matrix}\right)=\eta\cdot A}

for some
\eta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Thus,
\lambda=\pm\mu
. By changing the generator of the fiber subgroup, we may assume
\lambda=\mu
. Then
(M,N)
is a Hempel pair of scale factor
1
, and hence
M
and
N
are homeomorphic.

We follow the presentation of fundamental groups as in (
8.13
) or (
8.14
). Then,
{q_{j}^{\prime}\equiv q_{j}\pmod{p_{j}}}
. In fact, by replacing
a_{j}^{\prime}
with
a_{j}^{\prime}h_{N}^{c_{j}}
for some appropriate
c_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
and adjusting
e_{0}^{\prime}
correspondingly, we may assume
q_{j}^{\prime}=q_{j}
without loss of generality, which establishes an apparent isomorphism between
\pi_{1}M
and
\pi_{1}N
. This yields an apparent homeomorphism
\mathscr{F}:M\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N
by
Proposition 8.20
which we fix henceforth.

Based on this presentation, suppose
f(h_{M})=h_{N}^{\lambda}
and
f(e_{i})=g_{i}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{o(g_{i})\lambda}g_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
(
0\leq i\leq s
), where
o:\widehat{\pi_{1}N}\to\{\pm 1\}
is the orientation homomorphism. It then follows from
Lemma 8.15
that
\sum_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i}=0
. Fix peripheral subgroups
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M=\left\langle h_{M}\right\rangle\times\left\langle e_{i}\right\rangle
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}N=\left\langle h_{N}\right\rangle\times\left\langle e_{i}^{\prime}\right\rangle
.
Then
C_{g_{i}^{-1}}\circ f:\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\to\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}N}
is represented by a matrix.

(8.24)

C_{g_{i}^{-1}}\circ f\begin{pmatrix}h_{M}&e_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}h_{N}&e_{i}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}o(g_{i})\lambda&o(g_{i})\rho_{i}\\
0&o(g_{i})\lambda\end{pmatrix}.

Recall that
f
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{0}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M
. Thus, these matrices can be decomposed as
\lambda
multiplicating a matrix in
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, regardless of the choice of the conjugators
g_{i}
, according to
Proposition 6.7
.
Hence,
for
0\leq i\leq k
,

\rho_{i}=\lambda t_{i}
for some
t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

If
k=s
, then
\sum_{i=0}^{s}\rho_{i}=0
implies
\sum_{i=0}^{s}t_{i}=0
. If
k<s
, then we can choose
t_{k+1},\cdots,t_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}
so that
\sum_{i=0}^{s}t_{i}=0
. Then, by
Lemma 8.21
, there exists a homeomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi:\,M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}}

such that for each
0\leq i\leq s
,
\Psi_{\ast}(e_{i})=\alpha_{i}e_{i}^{\prime}\alpha_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{t_{i}}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in\ker(o)
.

Let
\epsilon_{i}=o(g_{i})\in\{\pm 1\}
.
When the base orbifolds of
M
and
N
are non-orientable, let
\Theta:M\to M
be the homeomorphism constructed in
Lemma 8.22
, and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{+}:\,M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

When the base orbifolds of
M
and
N
are orientable,
\epsilon_{i}=1
, and we simply let

\Phi^{+}=\Psi.

In either case, for each
0\leq i\leq s
, we have

(8.25)

\Phi^{+}_{\ast}(e_{i})=\beta_{i}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{\epsilon_{i}}\beta_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{t_{i}},

where
\beta_{i}\in\pi_{1}N
and
o(\beta_{i})=\epsilon_{i}
.

Now, we take
g_{i}^{+}=\beta_{i}g_{i}^{-1}
, so
o(g_{i}^{+})=1
. By (
8.24
),
C_{g_{i}^{+}}\circ f
sends
h_{M}
to
h_{N}^{\lambda}
and sends
e_{i}
to
\beta_{i}(e_{i}^{\prime})^{\epsilon_{i}\lambda}\beta_{i}^{-1}h_{N}^{\rho_{i}}
. Thus, according to (
8.25
), since
\rho_{i}=\lambda t_{i}
, we derive that

C_{g_{i}^{+}}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}=\lambda\otimes(\Phi^{+}_{\ast}|_{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}):\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\pi_{1}\partial_{i}N\cong\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}N}

for each
0\leq i\leq k
. In other words,
f
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{+}
with coefficient
\lambda
.

For the alternate
\pm
-sign, let
\tau:M\to M
be constructed by
Lemma 8.23
, which, up to conjugation, acts as a scalar multiplication of
-1
on each peripheral subgroup of
\pi_{1}M
. Then, we define
\Phi^{-}=\Phi^{+}\circ\tau
, and thus
f
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{-}
with coefficient
-\lambda
.
∎

9.
Graph manifolds

In our context, a graph manifold denotes a compact, orientable, irreducible 3-manifold with empty or incompressible toral boundary, which is not a closed
Sol
-manifold, and whose JSJ-decomposition consists of only Seifert fibered pieces. For consistency of proof, a single Seifert fibered manifold is also recognized as a graph manifold.

We shall denote a graph manifold as
M=(M_{\bullet},\Gamma)
, where
\Gamma
is the oriented JSJ-graph of
M
, and
M_{v}
is a Seifert fibered space for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
.

9.1.
Profinite isomorphism between graph manifolds

In this subsection, we consider the following setting. Let
M=(M_{\bullet},\Gamma)
and
N=(N_{\bullet},\Gamma^{\prime})
be two graph manifolds, and let
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
be an isomorphism. According to
Theorem 5.5
,
f
determines a congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups. For brevity of notation, we identify
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
through
{f^{\dagger}}
.

For any
e\in E(\Gamma)
, denote the two endpoints as
u=d_{0}(e)
and
v=d_{1}(e)
. Then the congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
implies the following commutative diagram.

(9.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{0}}^{-1}\circ f_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\varphi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\varphi_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{1}}^{-1}\circ f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Fix a free
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\left\{h_{M_{u}},e_{i}\right\}
for the peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{u}\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, where
h_{M_{u}}
is represented by a regular fiber, and
e_{i}
is a peripheral cross-section (see
§ 8.3
). When
M_{u}
is the minor Seifert manifold, we always view it as a Seifert fibration over the disk with two singular points of index
2
so as to avoid the ambiguity of
h_{M_{u}}
. Then,
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{u}}\subseteq\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{u}}
is a free
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
-module over the same basis
\left\{h_{M_{u}},e_{i}\right\}
according to
Lemma 6.1
. Similarly, we fix
\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{v}=\mathbb{Z}\left\{h_{M_{v}},e_{k}\right\}
,
{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{u}}={\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{N_{u}},e_{j}^{\prime}\right\}
, and
{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{v}}={\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{N_{v}},e_{l}^{\prime}\right\}
.

Over these bases, the maps
\widehat{\psi}
,
\widehat{\psi^{\prime}}
,
C_{g_{0}}^{-1}\circ f_{u}
, and
C_{g_{1}}^{-1}\circ f_{v}
can be represented by matrices in
\mathrm{GL}(2,\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
as shown by the following diagram.

(9.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{u}}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{M_{u}},e_{i}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{M_{v}},e_{k}\right\}=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{v}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}N_{u}}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{N_{u}},e_{j}^{\prime}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{N_{v}},e_{l}^{\prime}\right\}=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{l}N_{v}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{e,0}&\rho_{e,0}\\
0&\mu_{e,0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{0}}^{-1}\circ f_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{e,1}&\rho_{e,1}\\
0&\mu_{e,1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{1}}^{-1}\circ f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}_{e}&\beta^{\prime}_{e}\\
\gamma^{\prime}_{e}&\delta^{\prime}_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi^{\prime}}}

In particular, the upper-triangle matrices
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda&\rho\\
0&\mu\end{smallmatrix}\right)
(with indices omitted) follow from
Example 8.10
, and
\lambda,\mu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. Note that
\widehat{\psi}
is in fact the profinite completion of the gluing map in
\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{u},\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{v})
, so
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Similarly,
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}_{e}&\beta^{\prime}_{e}\\
\gamma^{\prime}_{e}&\delta^{\prime}_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 9.3
.

We follow the notation of (
9.2
), and omit the subscript
e
for brevity. Then,

(1)

\gamma=\pm\gamma^{\prime}\neq 0
;

(2)

\lambda_{1}=\pm\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}=\pm\lambda_{0}
;

(3)

\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}=\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}^{-1}\frac{\delta}{\gamma}-\mu_{0}^{-1}\rho_{0}
and
\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}=\mu_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{1}\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\mu_{1}^{-1}\rho_{1}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(4)

if
\lambda_{0}=\pm\mu_{0}
, then
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}\in\lambda_{0}\mathbb{Z}
, and consequently,
f_{u}
is peripheral
\lambda_{0}
-regular at
\partial_{i}M_{u}
and
f_{v}
is peripheral
\lambda_{0}
-regular at
\partial_{k}M_{v}
.

The calculation was mainly done in the proof of
[
40
, Theorem 10.1]
. We shall briefly reproduce this calculation for completeness.

Proof.

Note that
\gamma
is the intersection number of the regular fibers of
M_{u}
and
M_{v}
on the common toral boundary. The minimality of the JSJ-system of tori implies that
\gamma\neq 0
, for otherwise, gluing
M_{u}
and
M_{v}
along this common boundary in a fiber-parallel way still yields a Seifert fibered space. Similarly,
\gamma^{\prime}\neq 0
.

The commutative diagram (
9.2
) implies

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda_{1}\alpha+\rho_{1}\gamma&\lambda_{1}\beta+\rho_{1}\delta\\
\mu_{1}\gamma&\mu_{1}\delta\end{matrix}\right)=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda_{1}&\rho_{1}\\
0&\mu_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha^{\prime}&\beta^{\prime}\\
\gamma^{\prime}&\delta^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda_{0}&\rho_{0}\\
0&\mu_{0}\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda_{0}\alpha^{\prime}&\rho_{0}\alpha^{\prime}+\mu_{0}\beta^{\prime}\\
\lambda_{0}\gamma^{\prime}&\rho_{0}\gamma^{\prime}+\mu_{0}\delta^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right).

In particular, the lower-left entry implies
\lambda_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
. Since
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are non-zero integers, while
\lambda_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
,
[
40
, Lemma 2.2]
implies that
\gamma=\pm\gamma^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}=\pm 1
. Thus,
\mu_{1}=\pm\lambda_{0}
.
In addition, since
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
,
\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\pm 1
, and similarly
\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}&\beta^{\prime}\\
\gamma^{\prime}&\delta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\pm 1
. Thus,
\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{0}&\rho_{0}\\
0&\mu_{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\pm\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\rho_{1}\\
0&\mu_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, i.e.
\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}=\pm\lambda_{1}\mu_{1}
. Thus,
\mu_{1}=\pm\lambda_{0}
implies
\lambda_{1}=\pm\mu_{0}
. This proves (
1
) and (
2
).

Moreover, the lower-right entry implies

\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}=\frac{\mu_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}\delta-\mu_{0}^{-1}\rho_{0}\gamma^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}=\frac{\mu_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}\delta}{\lambda_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}\gamma}-\mu_{0}^{-1}\rho_{0}=\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}^{-1}\frac{\delta}{\gamma}-\mu_{0}^{-1}\rho_{0},

and the upper-left entry implies

\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}=\frac{\lambda_{0}^{-1}\lambda_{1}\alpha+\lambda_{0}^{-1}\rho_{1}\gamma}{\lambda_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}\gamma}=\mu_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{1}\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\mu_{1}^{-1}\rho_{1},

which proves (
3
).

When
\lambda_{0}=\pm\mu_{0}
, we have
\mu_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}=\pm 1
and
\lambda_{0}^{-1}\lambda_{1}=\pm 1
. The lower-right entry implies
\mu_{0}^{-1}\rho_{0}\gamma^{\prime}=\mu_{0}^{-1}\mu_{1}\delta-\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,
[
40
, Lemma 2.2]
then implies
\mu_{0}^{-1}\rho_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
, i.e.
\rho_{0}\in\mu_{0}\mathbb{Z}=\lambda_{0}\mathbb{Z}
. Similarly, the upper-left entry implies
\lambda_{0}^{-1}\rho_{1}\gamma=\alpha^{\prime}-\lambda_{0}^{-1}\lambda_{1}\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}
. Again,
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
and
[
40
, Lemma 2.2]
implies
\lambda_{0}^{-1}\rho_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
, i.e.
\rho_{1}\in\lambda_{0}\mathbb{Z}
. This finishes the proof of (
4
).
∎

9.2.
Profinite almost rigidity of graph manifolds

Theorem 9.4
.

The class of graph manifolds is profinitely almost rigid.

Remark 9.5
.

This was proved for the class of closed graph manifolds by Wilkes
[
40
]
, for which a finite list of numerical equations was introduced to determine precisely whether two closed graph manifolds are profinitely isomorphic.
In particular, Wilkes showed that closed graph manifolds are profinitely almost rigid but not profinitely rigid, with the exceptions constructed from gluing Hempel pairs.

As a preparation for the proof of
Theorem 9.4
, we briefly review the numerical invariants for an oriented graph manifold, and the readers are referred to
[
8
, Section 11.3]
for more details.

Fix a finite graph
\Gamma
as the JSJ-graph of a graph manifold
M
. We assume that
\Gamma
is not a single vertex, in which case the classification of graph manifolds reduces to the classification of Seifert fibered spaces. For each
v\in V(\Gamma)
, fix an oriented Seifert fibered 3-manifold
M_{v}
. Then we choose a presentation of
\pi_{1}M_{v}
as in
Section 8
:

\begin{aligned} &\pi_{1}M_{v}=\left<\left.\begin{gathered}h_{v},a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},\\
u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{g},v_{g},\\
e_{0,v},e_{1,v},\ldots,e_{s,v}\end{gathered}\,\right|\left.\,\begin{gathered}e_{0,v}=\left(a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1,v}\cdots e_{s,v}[u_{1},v_{1}]\cdots[u_{g},v_{g}]\right)^{-1}\\
a_{j}^{p_{j}}h^{q_{j}}=1,\,h\text{ central}\end{gathered}\right.\right>,\\
\text{or }&\pi_{1}M_{v}=\left<\left.\begin{gathered}a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},u_{1},\ldots,u_{g},\\
e_{0,v},e_{1,v},\ldots,e_{s,v},h_{v}\end{gathered}\right.\left|\,\begin{gathered}e_{0,v}=\left(a_{1}\cdots a_{r}e_{1,v}\cdots e_{s,v}u_{1}^{2}\cdots u_{g}^{2}\right)^{-1}\\
a_{j}^{p_{j}}h^{q_{j}}=1,\,[a_{j},h]=1,\\
[e_{i,v},h]=1,\,u_{k}hu_{k}^{-1}=h^{-1}\end{gathered}\right.\right>.\end{aligned}

We additionally require that

(1)

\{e_{i,v},h_{v}\}
determines the orientation on the boundary component induced by the given orientation of
M_{v}
;

(2)

0<q_{j}<p_{j}
for each
1\leq j\leq r
.

Then
\{e_{i,v},h_{v}\}_{i=0}^{s}
is called an admissible coordinate system on
\partial M_{v}
, following
[
8
, Definition 11.4]
.

Then, an oriented graph manifold
M
with underlying JSJ-graph
\Gamma
and corresponding Seifert pieces
M_{v}
is determined by the gluing maps
\psi_{e}
at each edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
. As in
§ 9.1
, the gluing map at
e\in E(\Gamma)
,
up to isotopy, is determined by a matrix:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{e}:\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M_{u})=\mathbb{Z}\{h_{u},e_{i,u}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\{h_{v},e_{k,v}\}=\pi_{1}(\partial_{k}M_{v})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

where
u=d_{0}(e)
,
v=d_{1}(e)
,
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, and
\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-1
by choice of orientation.

Let the JSJ-torus
T^{2}_{e}
inherit the boundary orientation of
M_{v}
on the
d_{1}
–side, then
\gamma_{e}=i(h_{u},h_{v})\neq 0
is the intersection number of the regular fibers at
T^{2}_{e}
.
Similarly,
\delta_{e}=i(e_{i,u},h_{v})
and
\alpha_{e}=i(e_{k,v},h_{u})
. And
\beta_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}
is uniquely determined by
\alpha_{e},\delta_{e},\gamma_{e}
from
\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-1
.

It is possible that different gluing matrices yield the same graph manifold.

[
8
, Theorem 11.2]
introduces a method to classify precisely the homeomorphism type of graph manifolds from the gluing matrices. For simplicity, we shall only need a partial result.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\overline{M}
be a graph manifold obtained from the same oriented Seifert fibered pieces through an alternate choice of gluing matrices:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\psi_{e}}:\pi_{1}(\partial_{i}M_{u})=\mathbb{Z}\{h_{u},e_{i,u}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\{h_{v},e_{k,v}\}=\pi_{1}(\partial_{k}M_{v}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{\alpha_{e}}&\overline{\beta_{e}}\\
\overline{\gamma_{e}}&\overline{\delta_{e}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

Then
\overline{M}\cong M

if the following conditions hold.

(1)

\overline{\gamma_{e}}=\gamma_{e}
, for each
e\in E(\Gamma)
;

(2)

\overline{\delta_{e}}\equiv\delta_{e}\pmod{\gamma_{e}}
, and
\,\overline{\alpha_{e}}\equiv\alpha_{e}\pmod{\gamma_{e}}
, for each
e\in E(\Gamma)
;

(3)

for any
v\in V(\Gamma)
such that
\#\partial M_{v}=deg_{\Gamma}(v)
(i.e.
M_{v}\cap\partial M=\varnothing
),

\sum_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{0}(e)=v}\frac{\delta_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-\sum_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{1}(e)=v}\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}=\sum_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{0}(e)=v}\frac{\overline{\delta_{e}}}{\overline{\gamma_{e}}}-\sum_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{1}(e)=v}\frac{\overline{\alpha_{e}}}{\overline{\gamma_{e}}}.

Proof.

We define an operation on the gluing matrices as follows.

(DT):
Choose a vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)
. For each element in
D_{v}=\{(e,i)\in E(\Gamma)\times\{0,1\}\mid d_{i}(e)=v\}
, assign an integer
t_{e,i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
\#\partial M_{v}=deg_{\Gamma}(v)
, we further require that
\sum_{(e,i)\in D_{v}}t_{e,i}=0
. First, for each
e\in E(\Gamma)
so that
(e,0)\in D_{v}
, replace
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&t_{e,0}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. And then, for each
e\in E(\Gamma)
so that
(e,1)\in D_{v}
, replace
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-t_{e,1}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

[
8
, Theorem 11.2]
implies that two choices of gluing matrices yield homeomorphic graph manifolds if one of them can be obtained from the other one through finite steps of operation (DT). In fact, (DT) is realized by a vertical Dehn twist on the Seifert piece
M_{v}
as shown in
Lemma 8.21
. The restriction
\sum t_{e,i}=0
when
\#\partial M_{v}=deg_{\Gamma}(v)
is the same restriction in the Dehn twist construction, as illustrated in
Lemma 8.21
; and this restriction is not needed when
\#\partial M_{v}>deg_{\Gamma}(v)
since we can adjust the twisting on the extra boundary component in
\partial M\cap M_{v}
accordingly.

It is easy to verify that
\big\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{\alpha_{e}}&\overline{\beta_{e}}\\
\overline{\gamma_{e}}&\overline{\delta_{e}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\big\}_{e\in E(\Gamma)}
can be obtained from
\big\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\big\}_{e\in E(\Gamma)}
through finitely many steps of (DT) if and only if the conditions (
1
), (
2
), (
3
) hold.
∎

In fact, condition (
3
) introduced in
Lemma 9.6
is equivalently determined by a rational number

\displaystyle\tau(v)=

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{0}(e)=v}\frac{\delta_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-\sum_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{1}(e)=v}\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-\sum_{j}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in E(\Gamma)\text{ adjoining }v}\frac{i(e_{i,v},h_{w})}{i(h_{v},h_{w})}-\sum_{j}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}.

In the second line,
e\in E(\Gamma)
with
d_{0}(e)=d_{1}(e)=v
is count twice, and
w
denotes the other endpoint of
e
. Note that the fraction
\frac{i(e_{i,v},h_{w})}{i(h_{v},h_{w})}
is independent with the orientation of the JSJ-torus as well as the orientation of
h_{w}
, so the fraction is well-defined.

The Seifert invariants
(p_{j},q_{j})
for
M_{v}
are fixed beforehand by the choice of fundamental group presentations. Thus, condition (
3
) holds if and only if
\tau(v)=\overline{\tau}(v)
. The number
\tau(v)
is also referred to as the
total slope
at
M_{v}

in
[
40
]
.

Therefore,
the (orientation-preserving) homeomorphism type of the graph manifold
M
can be determined by a list of numerical invariants:

(9.7)

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle\gamma_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ for each }e\in E(\Gamma);

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle\delta_{e}(\mathrm{mod}\,\gamma_{e})\text{ and }\alpha_{e}(\mathrm{mod}\,\gamma_{e})\text{ for each }e\in E(\Gamma);

\displaystyle(3)

\displaystyle\tau(v)\text{ for each }v\in\{v\in V(\Gamma)\mid\#\partial M_{v}=deg_{\Gamma}(v)\}.

Let us now return to the profinite isomorphism between graph manifolds.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be oriented graph manifolds over the same JSJ-graph
\Gamma
with the same oriented Seifert fibered pieces
M_{v}
. We fix the presentation of
\pi_{1}M_{v}
as in the beginning of this subsection, and
M
and
M^{\prime}
can be described by the gluing matrices.

Suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}M^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism, so that the map
{f^{\dagger}}:\Gamma\to\Gamma
induced by
Theorem 5.5
is the identity map.
Suppose
v\in V(\Gamma)
is a vertex such that
\#\partial M_{v}=deg_{\Gamma}(v)
.
Then, the corresponding total slopes are equal up to a
\pm
-sign:
\tau(v)=\pm\tau^{\prime}(v)
.

Proof.

Let
f_{\bullet}
denote the induced congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graphs of the profinite groups.
Then,

\#\partial M_{v}=deg_{\Gamma}(v)
implies that
f_{v}
in fact respects the peripheral structure, as is completely witnessed in the JSJ-graph.
Therefore, by
Proposition 8.12
, there exist
\lambda,\mu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and
\rho_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
f_{v}(h_{v})=h_{v}^{\lambda}
and
f_{v}(e_{i,v})=g_{i}e_{i,v}^{o(g_{i})\mu}g_{i}^{-1}h_{v}^{\rho_{i}}
. Here
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}
, and
o
is the orientation homomorphism whose kernel is the centralizer of
h_{v}
.
By composing with a conjugation, we have

C_{g_{i}^{-1}}\circ f\begin{pmatrix}h_{v}&e_{i,v}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}h_{v}&e_{i,v}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}o(g_{i})\lambda&o(g_{i})\rho_{i}\\
0&o(g_{i})\mu\end{pmatrix}.

For any edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
with
d_{1}(e)=v
, we have a commutative diagram as in (
9.2
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M_{u}}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{u},e_{j,u}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{v},e_{i,v}\right\}=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{j}M_{u}}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{u},e_{j,u}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{v},e_{i,v}\right\}=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}o(g_{i})\lambda&o(g_{i})\rho_{i}\\
0&o(g_{i})\mu\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{i}}^{-1}\circ f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}_{e}&\beta^{\prime}_{e}\\
\gamma^{\prime}_{e}&\delta^{\prime}_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

Then,
Lemma 9.3
(
3
) implies that

(9.9)

\frac{\alpha^{\prime}_{e}}{\gamma^{\prime}_{e}}=\mu^{-1}\lambda\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}+\mu^{-1}\rho_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}.

Similarly, for
e\in E(\Gamma)
with
d_{0}(e)=v
we have:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{v},e_{i,v}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{w},e_{k,w}\right\}=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M_{w}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M_{v}}=\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{v},e_{i,v}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\left\{h_{w},e_{k,w}\right\}=\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{k}N_{w}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{e}&\beta_{e}\\
\gamma_{e}&\delta_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}o(g_{i})\lambda&o(g_{i})\rho_{i}\\
0&o(g_{i})\mu\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g_{1}}^{-1}\circ f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha^{\prime}_{e}&\beta^{\prime}_{e}\\
\gamma^{\prime}_{e}&\delta^{\prime}_{e}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

It again follows from
Lemma 9.3
(
3
) that

(9.10)

\frac{\delta_{e}^{\prime}}{\gamma_{e}^{\prime}}=\mu^{-1}\lambda\frac{\delta_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-\mu^{-1}\rho_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}.

We denote the Seifert invariants of
M_{v}
as
(p_{j},q_{j})
. Since
f_{v}
respects the peripheral structure, in
\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
we obtain:

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}(v)=

\displaystyle{\textstyle\sum\limits_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{0}(e)=v}}\frac{\delta_{e}^{\prime}}{\gamma_{e}^{\prime}}-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{e\in E(\Gamma):\,d_{1}(e)=v}}\frac{\alpha_{e}^{\prime}}{\gamma_{e}^{\prime}}-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{j}}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}\lambda\big({\textstyle\sum\limits_{d_{0}(e)=v}}\frac{\delta_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{d_{1}(e)=v}}\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}\big)-\mu^{-1}{\textstyle\sum\limits_{i}}\rho_{i}-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{j}}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}

\displaystyle(\ref{9.10equ1}),\,(\ref{9.10equ2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}\lambda\big({\textstyle\sum\limits_{d_{0}(e)=v}}\frac{\delta_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{d_{1}(e)=v}}\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}\big)-\mu^{-1}\big(\lambda{\textstyle\sum\limits_{j}}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}-\mu{\textstyle\sum\limits_{j}}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}\big)-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{j}}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}

\displaystyle(\text{\autoref{LEM: Sum slope}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}\lambda\big({\textstyle\sum\limits_{d_{0}(e)=v}}\frac{\delta_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{d_{1}(e)=v}}\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\gamma_{e}}-{\textstyle\sum\limits_{j}}\frac{q_{j}}{p_{j}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}\lambda\cdot\tau(v)

Note that
\tau(v),\tau^{\prime}(v)\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\mu^{-1}\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, by multiplicating the denominators, it follows from
[
40
, Lemma 2.2]
that either
\tau(v)=\tau^{\prime}(v)=0
, or
\mu^{-1}\lambda=\pm 1
and
\tau(v)=\pm\tau^{\prime}(v)
. This finishes the proof of this lemma.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem 9.4
.

Proof of
Theorem 9.4
.

Let
\mathscr{M}_{G}
denote the class of graph manifolds. We show that
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{G}}(M_{0})
is finite for any
M_{0}\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
.

According to
Theorem 5.5
, the profinite completion of the fundamental group of a graph manifold determines its JSJ-graph as well as the profinite completion of the fundamental group of the Seifert piece at each vertex. Thus, any manifold in
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{G}}(M_{0})
can be viewed as a graph manifold over the same JSJ-graph
\Gamma
.
We assume that
\Gamma
is non-trivial, for the trivial case follows directly from
Proposition 8.5
.

By
Proposition 8.5
, the profinite completion of the fundamental group of each Seifert piece determines the homeomorphism type of each Seifert piece to finitely many possibilities.
Thus,
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{G}}(M_{0})
can be divided into finitely many subsets
\Delta_{1},\cdots,\Delta_{n}
such that for any
M,M^{\prime}\in\Delta_{i}
, we can choose orientations on
M
and
M^{\prime}
, which determines the orientation on their Seifert pieces, so that
M_{v}\cong M^{\prime}_{v}
by an orientation preserving homeomorphism for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
.

It suffices to show that each
\Delta_{i}
is finite. Within
\Delta_{i}
, we can fix the presentation of each
\pi_{1}M_{v}
as in the beginning of this subsection, and describe the graph manifolds through the gluing matrices.
Then, within
\Delta_{i}
,
Lemma 9.3
(
1
) implies that the profinite completion determines the intersection number of the regular fibers
\gamma_{e}
at each
e\in E(\Gamma)
up to
\pm
-sign. Moreover,
Lemma 9.8
implies that the profinite completion determines, up to
\pm
-sign, the total slope
\tau(v)
of each vertex
v
such that
\#\partial M_{v}=deg_{\Gamma}(v)
. Note that when the intersection number
\gamma_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
is fixed, there are only finitely many choices of
\delta_{e}(\mathrm{mod}\,\gamma_{e})
and
\alpha_{e}(\mathrm{mod}\,\gamma_{e})
. Therefore, within
\Delta_{i}
, there are only finitely many choices for the numerical invariants listed in (
9.7
).
This implies the finiteness of
\Delta_{i}
, finishing the proof.
∎

9.3.
From peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity to homeomorphism

Although graph manifolds are not profinitely rigid, even among the closed ones
[
40
]
, an additional condition of peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regularity will imply certain rigidity.
This subsection is devoted to the proof of
Theorem 1.15
(
2
), elaborated below as
Theorem 9.11
. The proof is based on
Proposition 8.19
, through joining together homeomorphisms of the Seifert fibered pieces according to the gluing lemma (
Lemma 6.13
).

Theorem 9.11
.

Let
M=(M_{\bullet},\Gamma)
and
N=(N_{\bullet},\Gamma^{\prime})
be
graph manifolds with non-empty toral boundary. Suppose
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism respecting the peripheral structure, and that
f
is
peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at some of the boundary components
\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M
, where
1\leq k\leq\#\partial M
. Then,

(1)

M
and
N
are homeomorphic;

(2)

there exists an element
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and two homeomorphisms
\Phi^{\pm}:M\to N
such that for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
f
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by homeomorphism
\Phi^{\pm}
with coefficient
\pm\lambda
, where the
\pm
-signs are consistent.

Proof.

Let
f_{\bullet}
be the congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma)
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma^{\prime})
given by
Theorem 5.5
. According to
Corollary 6.11
, since
f
respects the peripheral structure, for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
,
f_{v}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
also respects the peripheral structure. Thus, by
Proposition 8.12
, for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
, we can define the scale type of
f_{v}
as
(\lambda_{v},\mu_{v})\in(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times})^{2}
, up to
\pm
-signs.

We claim that there exists a unified
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, such that for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
,
\lambda_{v}=\pm\lambda
and
\mu_{v}=\pm\lambda
. First, choose a vertex
v_{0}\in V(\Gamma)
such that
M_{v_{0}}
contains one of
\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M
. Then
f_{v_{0}}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at this boundary component, according to
Lemma 6.12
. As a consequence of
Example 8.10
,
f_{v_{0}}
has scale type
(\pm\lambda,\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. For any two adjacent vertices
u,v\in V(\Gamma)
(i.e. connected by an edge),
Lemma 9.3
(
2
) implies
\lambda_{u}=\pm\mu_{v}
and
\mu_{u}=\pm\lambda_{v}
. Thus, starting from
v_{0}
, the connectedness of
\Gamma
implies that
\lambda_{v}=\pm\lambda
and
\mu_{v}=\pm\lambda
for all
v\in V(\Gamma)
.

Therefore,
Lemma 9.3
(
4
) implies that for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
,
f_{v}
is peripheral
\lambda
-regular at the boundary components obtained from the JSJ-tori corresponding to the edges adjoining
v
.
In addition, according to
Lemma 6.12
, each
f_{v}
is also peripheral
\lambda
-regular at any of the listed boundary components
\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M
which belongs to
M_{v}
. Let these two kinds of boundary components be the “selected boundary components” of
M_{v}
.
Then, each
M_{v}
contains at least one selected boundary component, and
f_{v}
is peripheral
\lambda
-regular at all the selected boundary components.

Recall that
f_{v}
also respects the peripheral structure. Thus,
Proposition 8.19
implies
M_{v}\cong N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}
for each
v\in V(\Gamma)
, and there exists a pair of homeomorphisms
\Phi^{\pm}_{v}
such that
f_{v}
restricting on each selected boundary component of
M_{v}
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{\pm}_{v}
with coefficient
\pm\lambda
.
In particular, concerning those boundary components corresponding to the JSJ-tori, the gluing lemma,
Lemma 6.13
(
2
), implies that the homeomorphism
\{\Phi^{+}_{v}\}_{v\in V(\Gamma)}
are compatible with the gluing maps at all JSJ-tori. Thus, combining these homeomorphisms yields a homeomorphism
\Phi^{+}:M\to N
.

In addition, for
1\leq i\leq k
, suppose
\partial_{i}M
belongs to a JSJ-piece
M_{v}
. According to diagram (
6.9
),
f
restricting on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
is equivalent to
f_{v}
restricting on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
composing with a conjugation in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
. Since
f_{v}
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{+}_{v}=\Phi^{+}|_{M_{v}}
with coefficient
\lambda
, it follows by definition that
f
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{+}
with coefficient
\lambda
.

Similarly, the homeomorphisms
\{\Phi^{-}_{v}\}_{v\in V(\Gamma)}
are also compatible with the gluing maps at all JSJ-tori. Combining these homeomorphisms yields a homeomorphism
\Phi^{-}:M\to N
, where
f
restricting on each
\partial_{i}M
(
1\leq i\leq k
) is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{-}
with coefficient
-\lambda
.
∎

9.4.
Profinite almost auto-symmetry for graph manifolds

Generally, a profinite isomorphism between graph manifolds with boundary may not respect the peripheral structure, as for an isomorphism between their fundamental groups. In this case,
Theorem 9.11
is no longer applicable. In order to prove the profinite almost rigidity in the bounded case, we show that graph manifolds satisfy the profinitely almost auto-symmetric property.

Theorem 9.12
.

Let
M=(M_{\bullet},\Gamma)
be a graph manifold with non-empty boundary, and let
\mathcal{P}
be a collection of some boundary components of
M
. Then,
M
is profinitely almost auto-symmetric at
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

The proof of this theorem consists of the following two cases.

Case 1:
Suppose that each JSJ-piece containing a boundary component in
\mathcal{P}
also contains a boundary component in
\partial M\setminus\mathcal{P}
.

In this case,
M
is not the minor Seifert manifold. Denote
\mathcal{P}=\{\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\partial_{k}M\}
, and fix conjugacy representatives of peripheral subgroups
\pi_{1}\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\pi_{1}\partial_{k}M
. Then
Proposition 6.7
implies that there is a well-defined group homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}:\quad\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\cfrac{\mathrm{Aut}(\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M)}{\{\pm id\}}\cong(\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}))^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\;\;f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([\varphi_{1}],\cdots,[\varphi_{k}])}

such that for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
C_{g_{i}}\circ f|_{\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}}=\lambda_{i}\otimes\varphi_{i}
for some
g_{i}\in\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
and
\lambda_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

Suppose each
\partial_{i}M
belongs to a JSJ-piece
M_{v_{i}}
. According to diagram (
6.9
),
f
restricting on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
is equivalent to the induced isomorphism
f_{v_{i}}
restricting on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
composing with a conjugation in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
.
Let
\{h_{i},e_{i}\}
be the free
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of each
\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M
, where
h_{i}
generates the fiber subgroup of
M_{v_{i}}
, and
e_{i}
is a peripheral cross-section.
Then according to
Example 8.10
, any element in the image of
\mathcal{F}

can be represented by matrices

\left(\left[\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1&t_{1}\\
0&\pm 1\end{pmatrix}\right],\cdots,\left[\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1&t_{k}\\
0&\pm 1\end{pmatrix}\right]\right)

over these bases.
Consequently, there exists a subgroup
K\leq\mathrm{im}(\mathcal{F})
with index at most
2^{k}
such that matrices in
K
have identical
\pm
-signs in the diagonal elements. In other words, any element in
K
can be represented by matrices

\left(\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&t_{1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right],\cdots,\left[\begin{pmatrix}1&t_{k}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right]\right).

It suffices to show that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(K)\subseteq\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
. Indeed, this can be realized through vertical Dehn twists between each component in
\mathcal{P}
with an extra boundary component in
\partial M\setminus\mathcal{P}
in the corresponding JSJ-piece.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}^{-1}(K)
, and
\mathcal{F}(f)=\left(\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&t_{1}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right],\cdots,\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&t_{k}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]\right)
.
Suppose
M_{v}
is a JSJ-piece that contains a component in
\mathcal{P}
, and denote
\mathcal{P}\cap M_{v}=\partial_{i_{1}}M,\cdots,\partial_{i_{s}}M
. Let
\partial^{\prime}M_{v}\in\partial M\setminus\mathcal{P}
be an extra boundary component in
M_{v}
guaranteed by our assumption. Then
Lemma 8.21
implies that there exists a vertical Dehn twist
\Phi_{v}
along
\partial_{i_{1}}M,\cdots,\partial_{i_{s}}M
and
\partial^{\prime}M_{v}
, which induces
the automorphism represented by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&t_{i_{j}}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
on
\pi_{1}\partial_{i_{j}}M
,
and
\Phi_{v}
is meanwhile the identity map on each boundary component of
M_{v}
obtained from the JSJ-tori. For those Seifert pieces
M_{u}
containing no distinguished boundary components, simply take
\Phi_{u}=Id
. Then the homeomorphisms
\{\Phi_{w}\}_{w\in V(\Gamma)}
are all identity maps at the JSJ-tori. Hence, they are compatible, and yields a homeomorphism
\Phi
on
M
, so that
f
restricting on each
\partial_{i}M\in\mathcal{P}
is induced by
\Phi
with certain coefficient in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. In other words,
f\in\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
.

Case 2:
There exists a JSJ-piece
M_{v_{0}}
, such that
M_{v_{0}}
contains a boundary component in
\mathcal{P}
, but contains no extra boundary components in
\partial M\setminus\mathcal{P}
.

We prove this case through induction on
\#V(\Gamma)
.

When
\#V(\Gamma)=1
, it follows from our assumption that
\mathcal{P}=\partial M
. Thus, any
f\in\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
respects the peripheral structure, and it follows from
Theorem 9.11
that
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})=\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
.

Now we assume
\#V(\Gamma)>1
.
Let
\Gamma_{\text{uo}}
be the unoriented underlying graph of
\Gamma
. There is a group homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho:\quad\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma_{\text{uo}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\;\;f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\dagger}}}

where
{f^{\dagger}}
is the graph isomorphism within the congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
induced by
f
, according to
Theorem 5.5
. Let

\mathscr{G}^{0}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})=\ker(\rho),\;\text{and }\mathscr{G}^{0}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})=\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})\cap\mathscr{G}^{0}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P}).

Note that
\Gamma
is a finite graph, so
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma_{\text{uo}})
is a finite group, and
\mathscr{G}^{0}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
is finite-index in
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\mathscr{G}^{0}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
is finite-index in
\mathscr{G}^{0}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
.

Let
\Upsilon
be the full subgraph of
\Gamma
spanned by the vertex
v_{0}
. In other words,
V(\Upsilon)=\{v_{0}\}
, and
E(\Upsilon)=\{e\in E(\Gamma)\mid d_{0}(e)=d_{1}(e)=v_{0}\}
. Let
M_{\Upsilon}
be the submanifold of
M
corresponding to the subgraph
\Upsilon
, which is obtained from
M_{v_{0}}
by gluing some of its boundary components.
Let
\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{r}
be the connected components of
\Gamma\setminus\Upsilon
, and let
M_{\Gamma_{j}}
be the submanifold of
M
corresponding to the subgraph
\Gamma_{j}
.
We label the oriented edges connecting
\Upsilon
and
\Gamma\setminus\Upsilon
as
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{s}\}
. For each
1\leq k\leq s
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, let
\delta_{\alpha}(e_{k})=\Upsilon\text{ or }\Gamma_{j}
if
d_{\alpha}(e_{k})\in\Upsilon\text{ or }\Gamma_{j}
, and let
\partial_{\alpha,e_{k}}M_{\delta_{\alpha}(e_{k})}
be the corresponding boundary component of
M_{\delta_{\alpha}(e_{k})}
obtained from the JSJ-torus.

For any
f\in\mathscr{G}^{0}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})
, let
f_{\bullet}
be the congruent isomorphism on
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma)
. Then there are profinite isomorphisms

\begin{gathered}f_{\Upsilon}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\Upsilon}}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Upsilon)\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Upsilon)\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\Upsilon}},\quad\text{and}\\
f_{\Gamma_{j}}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\Gamma_{j}}}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{j})\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{j})\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\Gamma_{j}}}\end{gathered}

induced by
f_{\bullet}
on the subgraphs
\Upsilon
and
\Gamma_{j}
as shown in
Proposition 3.11
.

Our assumption implies that
f_{v_{0}}
and
f_{\Upsilon}
respect the peripheral structure, as the peripheral subgroups obtained from JSJ-tori are witnessed by the congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
, and the peripheral subgroups corresponding to
M_{v_{0}}\cap\mathcal{P}
are preserved by
f
, and thus preserved by
f_{v_{0}}
and
f_{\Upsilon}
according to
Lemma 6.8
. Moreover,
Lemma 6.12
implies that
f_{v_{0}}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at each component of
M_{v_{0}}\cap\mathcal{P}
, and
f_{\Gamma_{j}}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at each component of
M_{\Gamma_{j}}\cap\mathcal{P}
.
In particular,
f_{v_{0}}
has scale type
(\pm\lambda,\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. Then,
Lemma 9.3
(
4
) implies that
f_{\Upsilon}
and
f_{\Gamma_{j}}
are all peripheral
\lambda
-regular at the boundary components given by the JSJ-tori corresponding to
e_{1},\cdots,e_{s}
. Let

\mathcal{P}_{j}=(\mathcal{P}\cap M_{\Gamma_{j}})\cup(\partial M_{\Gamma_{j}}\cap\partial M_{v_{0}})=(\mathcal{P}\cap M_{\Gamma_{j}})\cup\left\{\partial_{\alpha,e_{k}}M_{\Gamma_{j}}\mid\delta_{\alpha}(e_{j})=\Gamma_{j}\right\}

be a collection of boundary components in
M_{\Gamma_{j}}
. Then,

f_{\Upsilon}\in\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M_{\Upsilon},\partial M_{\Upsilon}),\;\text{and }f_{\Gamma_{j}}\in\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M_{\Gamma_{j}},\mathcal{P}_{j}).

Indeed,
f_{\Upsilon}\in\mathscr{G}_{h}(M_{\Upsilon},\partial M_{\Upsilon})
according to
Theorem 9.11
. On the other hand, note that
M_{\Gamma_{j}}
has less JSJ-pieces than
M
, so
(M_{\Gamma_{j}},\mathcal{P}_{j})
either falls into Case 1, or falls into a proven case in Case 2. Thus, the induction hypothesis implies that
M_{\Gamma_{j}}
is profinitely almost auto-symmetric at
\mathcal{P}_{j}
,
i.e.
\mathscr{G}_{h}(M_{\Gamma_{j}},\mathcal{P}_{j})
is finite index in
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M_{\Gamma_{j}},\mathcal{P}_{j})
.

Therefore, we can find finitely many representatives
f^{\star}_{1},\cdots,f^{\star}_{n}\in\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}^{0}(M,\mathcal{P})
such that for any
f\in\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}^{0}(M,\mathcal{P})
, there exists
1\leq i\leq n
such that
((f^{\star}_{i})^{-1}\circ f)_{\Gamma_{j}}\in\mathscr{G}_{h}(M_{\Gamma_{j}},\mathcal{P}_{j})
for each
1\leq j\leq r
.
Denote
f^{\prime}=(f^{\star}_{i})^{-1}\circ f
, and there exist homeomorphisms

\Phi_{\Upsilon}^{f^{\prime}}:M_{\Upsilon}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}M_{\Upsilon}\;\text{and }\Phi_{\Gamma_{j}}^{f^{\prime}}:M_{\Gamma_{j}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}M_{\Gamma_{j}}

such that

(1)

for each
\partial_{i}M\in\mathcal{P}
,
f^{\prime}_{x}
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi_{x}^{f^{\prime}}
with certain coefficient in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, where
x\in\{\Upsilon,\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{r}\}
such that
\partial_{i}M\subseteq M_{x}
;

(2)

for each
1\leq k\leq s
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
,
f_{\delta_{\alpha}(e_{k})}^{\prime}
restricting on
\partial_{\alpha,e_{k}}M_{\delta_{\alpha}(e_{k})}
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi_{\delta_{\alpha}(e_{k})}^{f^{\prime}}
with coefficient
\lambda_{\alpha,k}^{f^{\prime}}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

Then according to
Corollary 6.17
(
1
),
\lambda_{0,k}^{f^{\prime}}=\pm\lambda_{1,k}^{f^{\prime}}
for each
1\leq k\leq s
.

However, in order to apply the gluing lemma, it is required that
\lambda_{0,k}^{f^{\prime}}=\lambda_{1,k}^{f^{\prime}}
. Indeed, one can choose at most
2^{s}
representatives

\left\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{m}\right\}\subseteq\left\{g\in\mathscr{G}^{0}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(M,\mathcal{P})\mid g_{\Gamma_{j}}\in\mathscr{G}_{h}(M_{\Gamma_{j}},\mathcal{P}_{j}),\,\forall 1\leq j\leq r\right\},

and construct more representatives

\left\{f_{1}^{\star\star},\cdots,f_{N}^{\star\star}\right\}=\left\{f_{i}^{\star}\circ g_{l}\mid 1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq l\leq m\right\}\subseteq\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}^{0}(M,\mathcal{P})

such that for any
f\in\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}^{0}(M,\mathcal{P})
, there exists
1\leq i\leq N
such that
f^{\prime\prime}=(f^{\star\star}_{i})^{-1}\circ f
satisfies
f^{\prime\prime}_{\Gamma_{j}}\in\mathscr{G}_{h}(M_{\Gamma_{j}},\mathcal{P}_{j})
for each
1\leq j\leq r
, and
\lambda_{0,k}^{f^{\prime\prime}}=\lambda_{1,k}^{f^{\prime\prime}}
for each
1\leq k\leq s
.

In this case, according to
Corollary 6.17
(
2
), the homeomorphisms
\Phi_{\Upsilon}^{f^{\prime\prime}},\Phi_{\Gamma_{1}}^{f^{\prime\prime}},\cdots,\Phi_{\Gamma_{r}}^{f^{\prime\prime}}
are compatible at the JSJ-tori, and yield an ambient homeomorphism
\Phi^{f^{\prime\prime}}:M\to M
. In addition, for each
\partial_{i}M\in\mathcal{P}
, let
x\in\{\Upsilon,\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{r}\}
such that
\partial_{i}M\subseteq M_{x}
. According to diagram (
6.9
),
f^{\prime\prime}
restricting on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
is equivalent to
f_{x}^{\prime\prime}
restricting on
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{i}M}
composing with a conjugation in
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}
. Since
f_{x}^{\prime\prime}
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi_{x}^{f^{\prime\prime}}=\Phi^{f^{\prime\prime}}|_{M_{x}}
with certain coefficient, it follows that
f^{\prime\prime}
restricting on
\partial_{i}M
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi^{f^{\prime\prime}}
with certain coefficient in
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. In other words,
f^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{h}(M,\mathcal{P})
.
Therefore,
[\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}^{0}(M,\mathcal{P}):\mathscr{G}_{h}^{0}(M,\mathcal{P})]\leq N<+\infty
, finishing the proof.
∎

10.
Profinite almost rigidity for 3-manifolds

10.1.
Profinite rigidity in the Seifert part

Let us first prove
Theorem A
.

Theorem A
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are mixed 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary, and
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
is an isomorphism respecting the peripheral structure. Then the Seifert parts of
M
and
N
are homeomorphic.

Proof.

According to
Theorem 5.5
, the isomorphism
f:\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
induces a congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{M})
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma_{N})
. According to
Corollary 6.11
, for every
v\in V(\Gamma_{M})
, the isomorphism
f_{v}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{v}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{{f^{\dagger}}(v)}}
respects the peripheral structure. In addition,
{f^{\dagger}}
preserves the hyperbolic or Seifert type of the vertices. Thus,
{f^{\dagger}}
witnesses an isomorphism between the full subgraphs spanned by the Seifert vertices, and establishes a one-to-one correspondence between the connected components of the Seifert parts of
M
and
N
. It suffices to prove that the corresponding components of the Seifert parts are homeomorphic.

For any connected component
M_{0}
of the Seifert part
M
, which corresponds to a connected component
\Gamma_{0}
of the Seifert subgraph of
\Gamma_{M}
, let
N_{0}
denote the connected component of the Seiferft part of
N
corresponding to the subgraph
{f^{\dagger}}(\Gamma_{0})
. Then, by
Proposition 3.11
,
f_{\bullet}
establishes an isomorphism

f_{0}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{0}}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma_{0})\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},{f^{\dagger}}(\Gamma_{0}))\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{0}}

which also respects the peripheral structure according to
Corollary 6.11
.

Since
M
and
N
are mixed manifolds, one can choose an edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{M})
which connects a vertex
v\in V(\Gamma_{0})
with a hyperbolic vertex
u\in V(\Gamma)
.
The boundary gluing at the JSJ-tori corresponding to
e
and
{f^{\dagger}}(e)
can be described as

\begin{gathered}\hbox to271pt{\vbox to17.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 135.50078pt\lower-8.70195pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-135.50078pt}{-3.0978pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -187.49 -4.29)} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
