Since
M_{u}
and
N_{{f^{\dagger}}(u)}
are hyperbolic,
Theorem 7.2
implies that
f_{u}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at the boundary component
\partial_{i}M_{u}
. Thus, according to
Corollary 6.17
(
1
),
f_{0}
is also peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at
\partial_{k}M_{0}
. Recall that
f_{0}
respects the peripheral structure. Hence,
Theorem 9.11
implies that
M_{0}
and
N_{0}
are homeomorphic.∎

10.2.
Lack of rigidity without respecting peripheral structure

Given the fundamental group
G
of an irreducible 3-manifold with incompressible toral boundary, when
G
is marked with its peripheral structure, it determines the unique homeomorphism type of the manifold as shown by
Theorem 1.5
. Without marking of the peripheral structure, the homeomorphism type of 3-manifolds with this fundamental group may not be unique, for instance, when the manifold is not purely hyperbolic. Yet, in this case, the isomorphism type of
G
is enough to determine the JSJ-decomposition group theoretically by
[
28
, Theorem 3.1]
, that is, it determines the JSJ-graph of groups up to congruent isomorphism. In particular, the isomorphism type of
G
uniquely determines the isomorphism type of the fundamental groups of the Seifert part of the corresponding manifold.

Unfortunately, this fails to hold when
G
is replaced by merely
\mathcal{C}(G)
. Although
\widehat{G}
marked with the peripheral structure, or equivalently
\mathcal{C}(G;\pi_{1}\partial_{1}M,\cdots,\pi_{1}\partial_{n}M)
, determines the homeomorphism type of the Seifert part within mixed manifolds,
\widehat{G}
, or equivalently
\mathcal{C}(G)
, itself may not even determine the fundamental group of the Seifert part as shown by the following construction.

Theorem C
.

There exists a pair of irreducible, mixed 3-manifolds
M
and
N
with incompressible toral boundary, such that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
, while the fundamental groups of the Seifert part of
M
and
N
are not isomorphic.

Proof.

We shall construct examples over the JSJ-graph
\Gamma
shown in
Figure 2
.

Three vertices in a row, labeled u,v,w in order, connected by two edges between them forming a tree.

Figure 2.
JSJ-graph
\Gamma
for a non-rigid example

Let
M_{u}=N_{u}=X
be a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold. Let
F
be a compact, orientable surface with at least three boundary components, and let
{M_{v}=N_{v}=F\times S^{1}=Y}
.
Let
M_{w}
be the Seifert fibered space
(S;(p,q))
and
N_{w}
be the Seifert fibered space
(S;(p,q^{\prime}))
, such that:

(1)

the compact surface
S
has exactly one boundary component;

(2)

\chi(S)<0
, so that
M_{w}
and
N_{w}
are major Seifert manifolds;

(3)

q^{\prime}\equiv\kappa q\pmod{p}
for some
\kappa\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, i.e.
(M_{w},N_{w})
is a Hempel pair of scale factor
\kappa
;

(4)

q^{\prime}\not\equiv\pm q\pmod{p}
, so that
M_{w}
and
N_{w}
are not homeomorphic.

Let us first clarify the peripheral subgroups of these JSJ-pieces. For the hyperbolic piece
X
, fix a boundary component
T\subseteq\partial X
and a conjugacy representative of peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}T
. For the intermediate manifold
Y=F\times S^{1}
, let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\pi_{1}F
represent two boundary components of
F
, and let
h
denote a generator of
\pi_{1}(S^{1})=\mathbb{Z}
. This defines two peripheral subgroups
\pi_{1}\partial_{1}Y=\mathbb{Z}\{h,e_{1}\}
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{2}Y=\mathbb{Z}\{h,e_{2}\}
for which we shall construct the gluing.
For the Seifert fibered space
M_{w}
, let
h_{w}
represent a regular fiber in
\pi_{1}M_{w}
, and let
e_{w}
denote a peripheral cross-section with appropriate orientation yielding the Seifert invariants. Then, let
\pi_{1}\partial M_{w}=\mathbb{Z}\{h_{w},e_{w}\}
be a conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup. Likewise, let
h_{w}^{\prime}
represent a regular fiber in
\pi_{1}N_{w}
, let
e^{\prime}_{w}
represent the appropriate peripheral cross-section, and let
\pi_{1}\partial N_{w}=\mathbb{Z}\{h_{w}^{\prime},e^{\prime}_{w}\}
be a conjugacy representative of the peripheral subgroup.

Now we define
M
and
N
through the gluing maps at these peripheral subgroups. First, fix a gluing map
\psi:\pi_{1}T\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\pi_{1}\partial_{1}Y=\mathbb{Z}\{h,e_{1}\}
. Second, since
p
and
q
(resp.
p
and
q^{\prime}
) are coprime, we can choose

A=\begin{pmatrix}-q&\ast\\
p&\ast\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\quad\text{and}\quad B=\begin{pmatrix}-q^{\prime}&\ast\\
p&\ast\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})

such that
\det A=\det B=-1
.

Let
M
be defined by the gluing maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}M_{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}\{h,e_{2}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\{h_{w},e_{w}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial M_{w}}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\subseteq
\supseteq
\supseteq
\mathop{=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-q&\ast\\
p&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\mathop{=}

Note that the lower-left entry of
A
is non-zero, so the gluing between
M_{v}
and
M_{w}
is not fiber-parallel. Thus,
Figure 2
is indeed the JSJ-decomposition of
M
, by
[
1
, Proposition 1.6.2]
.

Similarly, let
N
be defined by the gluing maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}N_{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial_{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}\{h,e_{2}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\{h_{w}^{\prime},e_{w}^{\prime}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\partial N_{w}}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\subseteq
\supseteq
\supseteq
\mathop{=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-q^{\prime}&\ast\\
p&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\mathop{=}

We show that
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
by constructing a congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups. We first deal with the Hempel pair
(M_{w},N_{w})
. Let

\rho=\kappa\frac{q}{p}-\frac{q^{\prime}}{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}.

In fact,
\rho\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
since
q^{\prime}\equiv\kappa q\pmod{p}
.
Then,
[
40
, Theorem 10.7]
constructs an isomorphism
f_{w}:\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{w}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{w}}
respecting the peripheral structure, with scale type
(\kappa,1)
, such that

f_{w}(h_{w})=(h_{w}^{\prime})^{\kappa}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{w}(e_{w})=ge^{\prime}_{w}g^{-1}{(h_{w}^{\prime})}^{\rho}

for some
g\in\ker(o:\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{w}}\to\{\pm 1\})
. Thus, restricting on the peripheral subgroup
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M_{w}}
,

C_{g}^{-1}\circ f_{w}\begin{pmatrix}h_{w}&e_{w}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}h_{w}^{\prime}&e^{\prime}_{w}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

We claim that

B^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}A=\begin{pmatrix}1&\beta\\
0&\kappa\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{for some }\beta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}.

In fact,
B^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
p&q^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

, so
B^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
p\kappa&p\rho+q^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
p(-q\kappa+p\rho+q^{\prime})&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Recall that
\rho=\kappa\frac{q}{p}-\frac{q^{\prime}}{p}
, so
p(-q\kappa+p\rho+q^{\prime})=0
, i.e.

B^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
0&\gamma\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is an upper triangle matrix. In addition,

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\alpha q^{\prime}&\ast\\
\ast&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)=B\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
0&\gamma\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\kappa q+\rho p&\ast\\
\ast&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-q^{\prime}&\ast\\
\ast&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Thus,
\alpha=1
since
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion-free. Note that
\gamma=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
0&\gamma\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\det(B^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)A)=\kappa
. Thus,
B^{-1}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
0&\gamma\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\beta\\
0&\kappa\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Then, we deal with the intermediate manifold
Y=F\times S^{1}
. Indeed, since
{\#\partial F\geq 3}
, the fundamental group
\pi_{1}F
can be presented as a free group over the generators
e_{1},e_{2},b_{3},\cdots,b_{r}
. Thus,

\pi_{1}Y=\left\langle e_{1},e_{2},b_{3},\cdots,b_{r}\right\rangle\times\left\langle h\right\rangle=F_{r}\times\mathbb{Z}
. It follows from (
2.10
)
that
\widehat{F_{r}\times\mathbb{Z}}\cong\widehat{F_{r}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}(\widehat{\pi_{1}Y})\cong\mathrm{Aut}(\widehat{F_{r}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
be defined by

\tau(e_{1})=e_{1},\;\tau(e_{2})=e_{2}^{\kappa}h^{\beta},\;\tau(b_{i})=b_{i},\;\text{and }\tau(h)=h.

In particular, restricting on the peripheral subgroups
\pi_{1}\partial_{1}Y
and
\pi_{1}\partial_{2}Y
, we have

\displaystyle\tau\begin{pmatrix}h&e_{1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}h&e_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix};

\displaystyle\tau\begin{pmatrix}h&e_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}h&e_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\beta\\
0&\kappa\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
\tau
does NOT respect the peripheral structure whenever
\kappa\neq 1
, as shown by
Proposition 8.12
.

Combining these constructions,
(id_{\widehat{\pi_{1}X}},\tau,f_{w})
establishes a congruent isomorphism between the JSJ-graphs of profinite groups
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma)
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma)
, as shown by the following two commutative diagrams.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{1}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F_{r}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{1}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F_{r}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\cong
\supseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\psi}}
\subseteq
\cong

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{2}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\{h,e_{2}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\{h_{w},e_{w}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial M_{w}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{w}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{2}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\{h,e_{2}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\{h_{w}^{\prime},e_{w}^{\prime}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{1}\partial N_{w}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{w}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\supseteq
\mathop{=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\beta\\
0&\kappa\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-q&\ast\\
p&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\mathop{=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\kappa&\rho\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\subseteq
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{w}}
\supseteq
\mathop{=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-q^{\prime}&\ast\\
p&\ast\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\mathop{=}
\subseteq

Therefore, according to
Proposition 3.11
and
Proposition 5.2
, we obtain an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma)\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

which does not respect the peripheral structure.

Denote the Seifert parts of
M
and
N
as
M_{S}
and
N_{S}
respectively, which correspond to the subgraph consisting of
v
,
w
and the edge adjoining them.
We now show that
\pi_{1}M_{S}
and
\pi_{1}N_{S}
are not isomorphic.

Indeed, if there is an isomorphism
\varphi:\pi_{1}M_{S}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\pi_{1}N_{S}
, the group theoretical JSJ-decomposition
[
28
, Theorem 3.1]
implies a congruent isomorphism
\varphi_{\bullet}
between their JSJ-graph of fundamental groups. Note that
\pi_{1}M_{w}\not\cong\pi_{1}Y=F_{r}\times\mathbb{Z}
since
M_{w}
contains an exceptional fiber. Therefore,
\varphi
must induce an isomorphism
\varphi_{w}:\pi_{1}M_{w}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}\pi_{1}N_{w}
. Since both
M_{w}
and
N_{w}
have exactly one boundary component, which is adjoint to
Y
, the isomorphism
\varphi_{w}
obtained from the congruent isomorphism indeed respects the peripheral structure. Thus,
Theorem 1.5
implies that
M_{w}
and
N_{w}
are homeomorphic, which is a contradiction with our construction.
∎

In particular, this construction yields
\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N}
while
\pi_{1}M\not\cong\pi_{1}N
.

10.3.
Profinite almost rigidity

In this subsection, we prove
Theorem D
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
denote the class of compact, orientable 3-manifolds; let
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
denote the sub-class of
\mathscr{M}
consisting of boundary-incompressible 3-manifolds; and let
\mathscr{M}_{0}
denote the sub-class of
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
consisting of irreducible 3-manifolds with empty or incompressible toral boundary. Then
Theorem D
is restated as follows.

Theorem D
.

Any compact, orientable 3-manifold with empty or toral boundary is profinitely almost rigid in
\mathscr{M}
.

We shall first reduce
Theorem D
to the most crucial case.

Lemma 10.1
.

Suppose
M\in\mathscr{M}
. Then, there exists
M^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{1}M\cong\pi_{1}M^{\prime}
. In addition, if
M
has empty or toral boundary, then so does
M^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is a standard application of the loop theorem
[
32
]
. Indeed,
M^{\prime}
can be constructed starting from
M
by repeatedly attaching 2-handles to the compressible boundary components of
M
while preserving the fundamental group, and then capping off the emerging boundary spheres by 3-handles, until there are no more compressible boundary components. This process can also be viewed as attaching suitable compression-bodies to the compressible boundary components of
M
. In particular, if
M
has empty or toral boundary, then
M^{\prime}
constructed above is a Dehn filling of
M
, so
M^{\prime}
also has empty or toral boundary.
∎

Corollary 10.2
.

Theorem D
holds if any compact, orientable 3-manifold with empty or incompressible toral boundary is profinitely almost rigid in
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose
M\in\mathscr{M}
has empty or toral boundary. Let
M^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
be the manifold constructed in
Lemma 10.1
such that
\pi_{1}M\cong\pi_{1}M^{\prime}
. We claim that

(10.3)

\Delta_{\mathscr{M}}(M)=\bigcup_{N\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}(M^{\prime})}\left\{M_{\star}\in\mathscr{M}\mid\pi_{1}M_{\star}\cong\pi_{1}N\right\}.

The “
\supseteq
” direction of (
10.3
) is clear.
On the other hand, for any
M_{\star}\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}(M)}
, let
M_{\star}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
be constructed according to
Lemma 10.1
such that
{\pi_{1}M_{\star}}\cong{\pi_{1}M_{\star}^{\prime}}
. Then
\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\star}^{\prime}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\star}}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M}\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}M^{\prime}}
, i.e.
M_{\star}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}(M^{\prime})
. This implies that
M_{\star}
belongs to the right-hand side of (
10.3
).

Note that
M^{\prime}
has empty or incompressible toral boundary. If
M^{\prime}
is profinitely almost rigid in
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
, then
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}(M^{\prime})
is finite. In addition, for each
N\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}(M^{\prime})
, the set
\{M_{\star}\in\mathscr{M}\mid\pi_{1}M_{\star}\cong\pi_{1}N\}
is finite according to
Theorem 1.6
. Thus, the union
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}}(M)
is finite, finishing the proof.
∎

Moreover, the profinite detection of prime decomposition (
Theorem 4.1
) reduces the proof to the sub-class of irreducible 3-manifolds, and earlier results on
Sol
-manifolds
[
11
]
, hyperbolic manifolds
[
18
]
and graph manifolds (
Theorem 9.4
) further reduces the proof to the profinite rigidity of mixed 3-manifolds, which is the crucial part.

Lemma 10.4
.

Theorem D
holds if any mixed 3-manifold is profinitely almost rigid in
\mathscr{M}_{0}
.

Proof.

According to
Corollary 10.2
, it suffices to show that any
M\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
with empty or incompressible toral boundary is profinitely almost rigid in
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
. In fact, for any
N\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}(M)
,
Corollary 4.2
implies that
N
also has empty or toral boundary.

In addition, suppose the prime decomposition of
M
is
M\cong M_{1}\#\cdots\#M_{m}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{\#r}
, where
M_{i}\in\mathscr{M}_{0}
. Then
Theorem 4.1
implies the prime decomposition
N\cong N_{1}\#\cdots N_{m}\#(S^{2}\times S^{1})^{\#r}
, where
N_{i}\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq m
. Note that the homeomorphism type of
N
can be determined by the homeomorphism type of each
N_{i}
together with an orientation chosen for each
N_{i}
. Thus,
\#\Delta_{\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}(M)<+\infty
if and only if
\#\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M_{i})<+\infty
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.

Therefore,
Theorem D
holds if
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
is finite for any
M\in\mathscr{M}_{0}
.

When
M
is a closed
Sol
-manifold, any
N\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
is also closed according to
Corollary 4.2
.
[
43
, Theorem 8.4]
further implies that
N
is also a
Sol
-manifold. The conclusion follows from the profinite almost rigidity of
Sol
-manifolds deduced from the profinite almost rigidity of polycyclic-by-finite groups
[
11
]
.

Similarly, when
M
is either a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold, a Seifert fibered space, or a graph manifold, any
N\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
is also either finite-volume hyperbolic, Seifert fibered, or a graph manifold according to
Theorem 5.5
, and the finiteness of
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
follows from
Theorem 7.1
,
Proposition 8.5
, or
Theorem 9.4
respectively.

The only remaining case is that
M
is mixed. Thus,
Theorem D
holds if any mixed 3-manifold
M\in\mathscr{M}_{0}
is profinitely almost rigid in
\mathscr{M}_{0}
.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of
Theorem D
.

Proof of
Theorem D
.

Based on
Lemma 10.4
, it suffices to show that any mixed 3-manifold
M
is profinitely almost rigid in
\mathscr{M}_{0}
. According to
Theorem 5.5
, all manifolds in
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
can be identified over the same JSJ-graph
\Gamma
with each vertex fixed as either hyperbolic type or Seifert type, so that for each
N\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
, there exists a congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}:(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Gamma)\to(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma)
such that
{f^{\dagger}}=id_{\Gamma}
.

Let us first list some notations related to the oriented graph
\Gamma
.
Let
V_{\text{hyp}}(\Gamma)
denote the collection of hyperbolic vertices in
\Gamma
, and each
v\in V_{\text{hyp}}(\Gamma)
is also viewed as a subgraph of
\Gamma
. Let
\Lambda_{1},\cdots,\Lambda_{m}
be the connected components of the full subgraph spanned by the Seifert vertices.
Let
\Gamma_{\ast}
be an oriented graph obtained from
\Gamma
by collapsing each
\Lambda_{i}
to a vertex.
Then we can identify
V(\Gamma_{\ast})
as
V_{\text{hyp}}(\Gamma)\cup\{\Lambda_{1},\cdots,\Lambda_{m}\}
, and identify
E(\Gamma_{\ast})
as the collection of edges in
\Gamma
adjoint to a hyperbolic vertex. Let
\delta_{0,1}
denote the relation map in
\Gamma_{\ast}
, which is to say for
e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
,
\delta_{\alpha}(e)=\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})
if and only if
d_{\alpha}(e)
belongs to the subgraph
\Upsilon
.

For each
N\in\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
and
\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})
, denote by
N_{\Upsilon}
the submanifold of
N
corresponding to the subgraph
\Upsilon
, which is either a hyperbolic manifold or a graph manifold. For any
e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, let
\partial_{\alpha,e}N_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}
denote the boundary component of
N_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}
obtained from the
\delta_{\alpha}
-side of the JSJ-torus corresponding to
e
. We define

\mathcal{P}_{\Upsilon}^{N}=\left\{\partial_{\alpha,e}N_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}\mid\delta_{\alpha}(e)=\Upsilon,\;e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})\text{ and }\alpha\in\{0,1\}\right\}

as a collection of boundary components of
N_{\Upsilon}
.

Then there are profinite isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}M_{\Upsilon}}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{M}},\Upsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Upsilon)\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\Upsilon}},\quad\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

induced by the congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
on the subgraphs
\Upsilon
according to
Proposition 3.11
.

According to
Theorem 7.1
for the hyperbolic pieces, and
Theorem 9.4
for the graph submanifolds, the profinite completion determines the homeomorphism type of
N_{v}
(
v\in V_{\text{hyp}}
) and
N_{\Lambda_{i}}
to finitely many possibilities.
To be more precise, we can divide
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)
into finitely many subsets
\Delta_{1},\cdots,\Delta_{n}
, such that for any
N
and
N^{\prime}
belonging to the same subset
\Delta_{j}
, there is a homeomorphism
\Psi_{\Upsilon}:N^{\prime}_{\Upsilon}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N_{\Upsilon}
for each
\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})
so that
\Psi_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}(\partial_{\alpha,e}N^{\prime}_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)})=\partial_{\alpha,e}N_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}
for each
e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.
In other words, the manifolds in
\Delta_{j}
have the same hyperbolic and graph manifold pieces, with the same boundary pairing.
In order to show the finiteness of each
\Delta_{j}
, it remains to determine how these pieces are glued up along the JSJ-tori.

For each
\Delta_{j}
, we fix a representative
N\in\Delta_{j}
. Then, for any
N^{\prime}\in\Delta_{j}
, we fix the aforementioned homeomorphisms
\Psi_{\Upsilon}:N^{\prime}_{\Upsilon}\to N_{\Upsilon}
, and identify
N^{\prime}_{\Upsilon}
with
N_{\Upsilon}
through these homeomorphisms in the following context. In addition, we fix a congruent isomorphism
f^{N^{\prime}}_{\bullet}:(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N^{\prime}}},\Gamma)\to(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Gamma)
such that
(f^{N^{\prime}})^{\dagger}=id_{\Gamma}
.
Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{N^{\prime}}_{\Upsilon}:\;\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\Upsilon}}=\widehat{\pi_{1}N^{\prime}_{\Upsilon}}\cong\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N^{\prime}}},\Upsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N}},\Upsilon)\cong\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\Upsilon}},\;\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

be the isomorphisms induced by the congruent isomorphism
f_{\bullet}
on the subgraphs
\Upsilon
according to
Proposition 3.11
.
Then,
f^{N^{\prime}}_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}
preserves the conjugacy class of
\overline{\pi_{1}\partial_{\alpha,e}N_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}}
in
\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\delta_{\alpha}(e)}}
, for any
e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.

According to
Theorem 7.2
(
1
), for each
v\in V_{\text{hyp}}(\Gamma)
,
f^{N^{\prime}}_{v}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at all boundary components in
\mathcal{P}^{N^{\prime}}_{v}=\mathcal{P}^{N}_{v}
. Thus,
f^{N^{\prime}}_{v}
can be viewed as an element in
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(N_{v},\mathcal{P}^{N}_{v})
. In addition, any component in
\mathcal{P}^{N^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{i}}=\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Lambda_{i}}
is attached to a hyperbolic piece, i.e. attached to a component in
\mathcal{P}^{N}_{v}
for some
v\in V_{\text{hyp}}(\Gamma)
. Thus, the gluing lemma (
Corollary 6.17
(
1
)) implies that
f^{N^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{i}}
is peripheral
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
-regular at all components in
\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Lambda_{i}}
. Therefore,
f^{N^{\prime}}_{\Lambda_{i}}
can be viewed as an element
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(N_{\Lambda_{i}},\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Lambda_{i}})
.

Moreover, for each
\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})
,
N_{\Upsilon}
is profinitely almost auto-symmetric at
\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Upsilon}
according to
Corollary 7.3
and
Theorem 9.12
respectively.
In other words,
\mathscr{G}_{h}(N_{\Upsilon},\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Upsilon})
is finite index in
\mathscr{G}_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}}(N_{\Upsilon},\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Upsilon})
. Thus, there exist finitely many representatives
N^{\star}_{1},\cdots,N^{\star}_{s}
in
\Delta_{j}
so that for each
N^{\prime}\in\Delta_{j}
, there exists
1\leq k\leq s
such that for all
\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((f^{N^{\star}_{k}}_{\bullet})^{-1}\circ f^{N^{\prime}}_{\bullet})_{\Upsilon}=(f^{N^{\star}_{k}}_{\Upsilon})^{-1}\circ f^{N^{\prime}}_{\Upsilon}:\;\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\Upsilon}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pi_{1}N_{\Upsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

belongs to
\mathscr{G}_{h}(N_{\Upsilon},\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Upsilon})
.

Denote
f^{\prime}_{\bullet}=(f^{N^{\star}_{k}}_{\bullet})^{-1}\circ f^{N^{\prime}}_{\bullet}
as the congruent isomorphism between
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N^{\prime}}},\Gamma)
and
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{N^{\star}_{k}}},\Gamma)
. Then, for each
\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})
there exists a homeomorphism
\Phi_{\Upsilon}:N_{\Upsilon}=N^{\prime}_{\Upsilon}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}(N^{\star}_{k})_{\Upsilon}=N_{\Upsilon}
such that for each
\partial_{\alpha,e}N_{\Upsilon}\in\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Upsilon}
,
f_{\Upsilon}^{\prime}
restricting on
\partial_{\alpha,e}N_{\Upsilon}
is induced by the homeomorphism
\Phi_{\Upsilon}
with coefficient
\lambda_{\alpha,e}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
. In particular, for each
e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})
,
\lambda_{0,e}=\pm\lambda_{1,e}
according to
Corollary 6.17
(
1
).

In order to apply the gluing lemma, one actually requires
\lambda_{0,e}=\lambda_{1,e}
. Similar to the proof of
Theorem 9.12
, one can choose more (a total of at most
2^{\#E(\Gamma_{\ast})}s
) representatives
N^{\star\star}_{1},\cdots,N^{\star\star}_{t}
in
\Delta_{j}
, such that for each
N^{\prime}\in\Delta_{j}
, there exists
1\leq l\leq t
such that
f^{\prime}_{\Upsilon}=(f^{N^{\star\star}_{l}}_{\Upsilon})^{-1}\circ f^{N^{\prime}}_{\Upsilon}\in\mathscr{G}_{h}(N_{\Upsilon},\mathcal{P}^{N}_{\Upsilon})
, and
\lambda_{0,e}=\lambda_{1,e}
for each
e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})
. In this case, according to
Corollary 6.17
(
2
), the homeomorphisms
\{\Phi_{\Upsilon}\}_{\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})}
are compatible with the gluing maps of
N^{\prime}
and
N^{\star\star}_{l}
at each JSJ-torus corresponding to
e\in E(\Gamma_{\ast})
. As a result,
\{\Phi_{\Upsilon}\}_{\Upsilon\in V(\Gamma_{\ast})}
yields an ambient homeomorphism
\Phi:N^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}N^{\star\star}_{l}
.

Therefore,
\Delta_{j}=\left\{N^{\star\star}_{1},\cdots,N^{\star\star}_{t}\right\}
is finite, and
\Delta_{\mathscr{M}_{0}}(M)=\Delta_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Delta_{n}
is also finite, which finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of
Theorem 2.5

Theorem 2.5
might be well-known to experts. As we haven’t found a documented version of its proof, we supplement this in the appendix.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be finitely generated groups. Suppose
H_{1},\cdots,H_{m}
are subgroups of
G
, and
H_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,H_{m}^{\prime}
are subgroups of
G^{\prime}
.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathcal{C}(G;H_{1},\cdots,H_{m})=\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime};H_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,H_{m}^{\prime})
.

(2)

There exists an isomorphism as profinite groups
f:\widehat{G}\to\widehat{G^{\prime}}
, such that
f(\overline{H_{i}})
is a conjugate of
\overline{H_{i}^{\prime}}
in
\widehat{G^{\prime}}
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.

(3)

There exists an isomorphism as abstract groups
f:\widehat{G}\to\widehat{G^{\prime}}
, such that
f(\overline{H_{i}})
is a conjugate of
\overline{H_{i}^{\prime}}
in
\widehat{G^{\prime}}
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.

Proof.

(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
2
): Let
\mathcal{U}_{n}=\{U\leq G\mid[G:U]\leq n\}
, and
\mathcal{V}_{n}=\{V\leq G^{\prime}\mid[G^{\prime}:V]\leq n\}
. Since
G
and
G^{\prime}
are finitely generated,
\mathcal{U}_{n}
and
\mathcal{V}_{n}
are finite for each
n
. Let
U_{n}=\bigcap_{U\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}U
and
V_{n}=\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{n}}V
, then
U_{n}
and
V_{n}
are finite-index normal subgroups of
G
and
G^{\prime}
respectively.

For each
n
, since
\mathcal{C}(G;H_{1},\cdots,H_{m})=\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime};H_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,H_{m}^{\prime})
, there exists
K\lhd_{f}G
and an isomorphism
\psi:G/K\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}G^{\prime}/V_{n}
such that
\psi(H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap K))
is a conjugate of
H_{i}^{\prime}/(H_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{n})
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.

We claim that
K=U_{n}
.

For each
V\in\mathcal{V}_{n}
, consider the homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{V}:G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}/V_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}/V.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V}}

Since
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{n}}\mathrm{ker}(\pi_{V})=1
, it follows that
\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{n}}\mathrm{ker}(f_{V})=K
. On the other hand,
\left|G/\mathrm{ker}(f_{V})\right|=\left|G^{\prime}/V\right|\leq n
, which implies that
\mathrm{ker}(f_{V})\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
. Therefore,
K=\bigcap\mathrm{ker}(f_{V})\supseteq U_{n}
.
In particular, this shows that
\left|G/U_{n}\right|\geq\left|G/K\right|=\left|G^{\prime}/V_{n}\right|
. By switching
G
and
G^{\prime}
, we can symmetrically prove that
\left|G^{\prime}/V_{n}\right|\geq\left|G/U_{n}\right|
. Thus,
\left|G/U_{n}\right|=\left|G^{\prime}/V_{n}\right|=\left|G/K\right|
. This implies that
K=U_{n}
, i.e.
G/U_{n}\cong G^{\prime}/V_{n}
.

For each
n
, denote

X_{n}=\left\{\psi:G/U_{n}\xrightarrow{\tiny\cong}G^{\prime}/V_{n}\mid\psi(H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap U_{n}))\sim H_{i}^{\prime}/(H_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{n}),\,\forall 1\leq i\leq m\right\},

where
\sim
means conjugation in
G^{\prime}/V_{n}
. The above construction shows that
X_{n}
is non-empty, and
X_{n}
is also finite due to the finiteness of
G/U_{n}
and
G^{\prime}/V_{n}
.

Notice that
U_{n-1}/U_{n}
is the intersection of all subgroups in
G/U_{n}
with index no more than
n-1
,
and so is
V_{n-1}/V_{n}
in
G^{\prime}/V_{n}
. Thus, any element
\psi\in X_{n}
sends
U_{n-1}/U_{n}
to
V_{n-1}/V_{n}
. Consequently,
\psi
induces an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}:G/U_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}/V_{n-1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

which sends

\frac{H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap U_{n})}{(H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap U_{n}))\cap(U_{n-1}/U_{n})}=\frac{H_{i}}{H_{i}\cap U_{n-1}}

to a conjugate of

\frac{H_{i}^{\prime}/(H_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{n})}{(H_{i}^{\prime}/(H_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{n}))\cap(V_{n-1}/V_{n})}=\frac{H_{i}^{\prime}}{H_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{n-1}}.

Therefore, we have constructed a map
X_{n}\to X_{n-1}
sending
\psi\in X_{n}
to
\widetilde{\psi}\in X_{n-1}
, which makes
\left\{X_{n}\right\}
an inverse system of non-empty finite sets over a directed partially ordered set. It follows from
[
25
, Proposition 1.1.4]
that
\varprojlim_{n}X_{n}
is non-empty.

Choose
(\psi_{n})_{n\geq 0}\in\varprojlim X_{n}
. Indeed, we obtain a series of compatible isomorphisms
\psi_{n}:G/U_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}G^{\prime}/V_{n}
. As
U_{n}
and
V_{n}
are cofinal systems of finite-index normal subgroups in
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, by taking inverse limits, we derive an isomorphism of profinite groups

\hat{\psi}:\widehat{G}=\varprojlim G/U_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varprojlim G^{\prime}/V_{n}=\widehat{G^{\prime}}.

Denote

Y_{n,i}=\left\{g_{n,i}\in G^{\prime}/V_{n}\mid\psi_{n}(H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap U_{n}))=g_{n,i}(H_{i}^{\prime}/(H_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{n}))g_{n,i}^{-1}\right\}.

Then
Y_{n,i}
is a non-empty finite set. Note that the maps
(\psi_{n})
are compatible, and thus the quotient map
G^{\prime}/V_{n}\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime}/V_{n-1}
induces a map
Y_{n,i}\to Y_{n-1,i}
, making
\{Y_{n,i}\}
an inverse system. Again,
[
25
, Proposition 1.1.4]
implies that
\varprojlim_{n}Y_{n,i}
is non-empty.
Note that

\overline{H_{i}}=\varprojlim H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap U_{n})\subseteq\widehat{G},\;\text{and }\quad\overline{H_{i}^{\prime}}=\varprojlim H_{i}^{\prime}/(H_{i}^{\prime}\cap V_{n})\subseteq\widehat{G^{\prime}}.

Thus, by choosing
\hat{g}_{i}\in\varprojlim Y_{n,i}\subseteq\varprojlim G^{\prime}/V_{n}=\widehat{G^{\prime}}
, the above construction shows that
\hat{\psi}(\overline{H_{i}})=\hat{g}_{i}\cdot\overline{H_{i}^{\prime}}\cdot\hat{g}_{i}^{-1}
.

(
2
)
\Rightarrow
(
3
) is trivial.

(
3
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
): Condition (
3
) actually implies that

\mathcal{C}(\widehat{G};\overline{H_{1}},\cdots,\overline{H_{m}})=\mathcal{C}(\widehat{G^{\prime}};\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}},\cdots,\overline{H_{m}^{\prime}}).

Thus, it suffices to show that

(A.1)

\mathcal{C}(G;H_{1},\cdots,H_{m})=\mathcal{C}(\widehat{G};\overline{H_{1}},\cdots,\overline{H_{m}}),

and

(A.2)

\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime};H_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,H_{m}^{\prime})=\mathcal{C}(\widehat{G^{\prime}};\overline{H_{1}^{\prime}},\cdots,\overline{H_{m}^{\prime}}).

Since
G
is finitely generated, it follows from a deep theorem of Nikolov-Segal
[
20
]
that every finite-index subgroup in
\widehat{G}
is open.
Thus,
Proposition 2.3
implies that there is a one-to-one correspondence between the finite-index normal subgroups of
G
and the finite-index normal subgroups of
\widehat{G}
, by sending
U\lhd_{f}G
to
\overline{U}\lhd_{o}G
. We claim that

(A.3)

\scalebox{.89}{
$\left[\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$G$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$U$};\,\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$H_{1}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$(H_{1}\cap U)$},\cdots,\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$H_{m}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$(H_{m}\cap U)$}\right]$}=\scalebox{.8}{$\left[\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$\widehat{G}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$\overline{U}$};\,\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$\overline{H_{1}}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$(\overline{H_{1}}\cap\overline{U})$},\cdots,\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{$\overline{H_{m}}$}\big/\lower 2.58334pt\hbox{$(\overline{H_{m}}\cap\overline{U})$}\right].$
}

In fact, let
\iota:G\to\widehat{G}
be the canonical homomorphism. Consider the homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi:\;G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}/\overline{U}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Note that
\phi(G)
is dense in
\widehat{G}/\overline{U}
, since
\iota(G)
is dense in
\widehat{G}
. In addition, since
\overline{U}\lhd_{o}\widehat{G}
, the quotient group
\widehat{G}/\overline{U}
is a finite discrete group. Therefore,
\phi
is surjective. On the other hand,
\ker(\phi)=\iota^{-1}(\overline{U})=U
. Thus,
\phi
induces an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{0}:G/U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}/\overline{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

between finite groups.

By construction, for each
1\leq i\leq m
,
\phi_{0}(H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap U))
is a dense subgroup of
\overline{H_{i}}/(\overline{H_{i}}\cap\overline{U})
. Since
\overline{H_{i}}/(\overline{H_{i}}\cap\overline{U})\leq\widehat{G}/\overline{U}
is finite and discrete, we conclude that
\phi_{0}(H_{i}/(H_{i}\cap U))=\overline{H_{i}}/(\overline{H_{i}}\cap\overline{U})
.

Therefore, we obtain (
A.3
) through
\phi_{0}
, which implies (
A.1
). Similarly, (
A.2
) holds, which finishes the proof of this theorem.
∎
